# Body Contact and Battles

> **Rule set:** Written to NHL rules. Where IIHF, USA Hockey, Hockey Canada or typical rec-league
> rules differ in a way that changes how you play, it is flagged inline. This document contains more
> of those flags than any other in this guide, because **whether body checking is legal at all
> depends entirely on which league you are in.** **Playing in Britain? The IIHF flags are yours** —
> every level of UK hockey runs the IIHF book, amended locally; see
> [UK and England Rules](../foundation/uk_rules.md), and read **"If you play in Britain"** in §1
> before you play a shift.
>
> **Related:** [UK and England Rules](../foundation/uk_rules.md) · [Defending the Rush](../systems/defending_the_rush.md) · [Skating](skating.md) · [Puck Handling](puck_handling.md) · [Defender](../positions/defender.md) · [Risk Management](../hockey-iq/risk_management.md) · [Equipment](../off-the-ice/equipment.md)

## Which book you are under decides what is legal — and the boards do not care

Two of the seven ideas in [Core Principles](../foundation/core_principles.md) govern this page. **Which book you are under decides what is legal for you.** Your age group, league and country settle whether you may body check or be checked, and nothing on the ice tells you which, so ask before your first game. **The boards do not care which book you are under, or how fast you are going.** How you meet a wall is decided several strides out, in any league, because a wall does not need an opponent to hurt you. Core Principles covers both: [which book you are under](../foundation/core_principles.md#2-which-book-you-are-under-and-where-you-sit-inside-it-decides-what-is-legal) and [choosing how you meet the boards](../foundation/core_principles.md#7-choose-how-you-meet-the-boards-before-you-have-to).

## Key focus

**Find out what your league allows before you play.** Leaning on each other while you chase the puck is legal almost everywhere; a body check is banned in many leagues and age groups ([§1](#1-which-contact-rules-are-you-actually-playing-under)). ⚠️ **Get the answer in writing, and until you have it, do not hit anyone.**

**Take the puck with your route and your stick, not your body.** Pick your line early, hold it, and add nothing when you arrive — an arm, shoulder, hip or late veer turns the angle into a check. Stick on the puck, never on hands or body.

**Hold the ice you are standing on — you can defend a whole shift without a hit.** Get in front first and lengthen their route to the puck, stick down and free hand off them. **Under Hockey Canada or CARHA adult rules, never lean or body to keep a spot.**

**Arrive at a battle with position, not just speed, then get low and wide.** Land with your body between the opponent and the puck and the wall on the far side — first there in the wrong place is worse than second in the right one.

**In a corner, the first player in stops the puck; the second stays out of the pile and reads where it comes out.** Pin the puck, not the player, and with your stick tied up kick the puck free — never a player. Then move it before the next opponent arrives.

**Go to the net front to score, and hold it to defend.** Attacking, screen outside the crease, stick down for tips and rebounds, and never touch the goalie. Defending, take the inside spot beside the screener and play their stick — never walk them out or cross-check them.

**Never hit a player who cannot see you coming, and never finish a check once the puck has gone.** If you can see their numbers, pull up and play the puck — in every book here but USA Hockey and the IIHF the lightest call for a hit that stays on the back ends your game; under the IIHF, plan on it.

**When the wall is coming, meet it with your skates parallel to the boards and take it on your forearm and hip, head up and chin off your chest.** Never turn your back to the checker and never duck — a tucked chin is how a vertebra fractures, even at walking speed.

**Win your position with your feet, not your stick or your hands.** If you are beaten, skate back rather than reaching, hooking or holding. When it boils over: gloves on, helmet on, hands down, skate away.

**If a player may have hurt their head or neck, nobody moves them except to keep their airway clear, somebody phones, and their helmet stays on — no exception.** Do not help them up.

## Overview

Most games are decided by who wins the scrappy three-second contests for a loose puck on the wall, in the corner and at the net front — that is coaching experience, not a counted figure. This document is about winning them without getting hurt, ejected or penalised.

**Start with your league, because the same hit is legal in one game and a penalty in the next.** Body *contact* — two players leaning on each other while chasing the same puck — is legal almost everywhere, though CARHA's adult rules penalise deliberate bodying, pushing and shoving, and in IIHF women's hockey it stops at the wall: never use the boards to push, pin or eliminate her. A body *check* — deliberately using your body to separate an opponent from the puck — is banned in many leagues: younger age groups, non-check adult classifications, every CARHA adult league, and girls' and women's play under USA Hockey and Hockey Canada. The IIHF book, which Britain plays under, permits a restricted form in women's hockey instead. **In Britain, your competition's Rules of Competition give the answer for your age group** (§1). ⚠️ **Until you have your league's answer, do not hit anyone.** "Non-check" does not mean "non-contact", so expect to be leaned on and learn to hold your ground.

**Most pucks are taken without a hit, and that works in every league.** *Angling* steers a carrier by the route you skate: pick your line early, take away the middle, keep your momentum heading toward your own net and arrive stick on puck, adding nothing. The *stick lift* goes **low on the shaft, well below their bottom hand**, where no book reaches it. **Never chop at the hands**: in all six books read here that can get you thrown out of the game with nobody hurt ([Stick Checks](#3-stick-checks--legal-effective-and-under-used)). *Body position* defends a whole shift: get in front first and make their route to the puck longer ([Body Position as Defence](#4-body-position-as-defence)). One thing is never yours in any game: walking a net-front screener out.

**Battles are won by position, base and support more often than by strength.** Arrive with your body between the opponent and the puck and the wall on the far side, then get low and wide. In the corner the first player stops the puck and the second reads where it comes out. On a loose puck the inside lane beats getting there first, and you decide what to do with it before you touch it. With your stick tied up, your feet move the puck — never a player. At the net front, attackers screen, tip and bury rebounds; defenders hold their ground beside the screener and play the stick.

**As the checker, three rules do not bend: never hit a player from behind or one turned toward the wall, never contact the head, face or neck, and never finish a check once the puck has gone.** A hit from behind that stays on the back has no bare minor in any book here, and a player facing you is no licence to hit high — IIHF Rule 48.1: *"There is no clean check to the head or neck."* **Play it as though the onus were absolute** ([Checking from behind](#checking-from-behind-and-what-the-onus-does-and-does-not-mean)).

**As the player being hit, you choose how you meet the wall.** Head up, chin off your chest, and when the wall is coming, skates parallel to the boards and the contact on your forearm and hip. **Never turn your back to the checker, and never duck — two different injuries.** A turned back makes it a hit from behind, and USA Hockey warns that *"Even a light hit from behind could inflict a severe head or neck injury."* A tucked chin straightens the neck, and USA Hockey says the injury from that head-first collision with the boards or a post *"can occur at walking speed."* Never turn into a hit to draw the call: the NHL, IIHF and PWHL waive the penalty when the turn was deliberate, and in every book it is your spine on the line.

**Stay out of the box with your feet.** Hooking, holding and tripping are what a beaten player reaches for; skate to get your position back. When it boils over: gloves on, helmet on, hands down, skate away. The books most amateurs play under price a fight, joining one and leaving the bench far higher than the NHL does ([Playing Physical Without Taking Penalties](#12-playing-physical-without-taking-penalties)).

**After a hit to the head or neck, the instinct to help them up is the dangerous one.** Nobody moves them except to keep their airway clear, somebody phones, and their helmet stays on — no exception ([If it happens anyway](#if-it-happens-anyway--a-suspected-neck-injury-on-the-ice)).

---

## 1. Which Contact Rules Are You Actually Playing Under?

Before you read another word, find out which category your league falls into — and then check the game in front of you, because the two can come apart: under **USA Hockey Rule 604(b)**, a checking team playing a non-checking one is in a no-checking game whatever its own classification says. Getting this wrong is how good players end up in the penalty box, and how careless players hurt someone.

### The two categories

```facts
Key: Body contact and body checking are not the same thing — one is legal almost everywhere, limited in CARHA's adult book and at the wall in IIHF women's hockey; the other only in specific classifications
Rule: Hockey Canada defines body contact as incidental contact of two opposing players pursuing the puck in the same direction — no penalty
Rule: USA Hockey defines a body check as intentional physical contact by a skater to an opponent in control of the puck
Rule: Hockey Canada's mechanical test for a check — opposite-directional force, leaving your skating lane, or extending an arm, shoulder or hip after angling
Key: Body contact is legal everywhere but CARHA's adult book; IIHF women's hockey bars using the boards to push, pin or eliminate an opponent; body checking only in some leagues
```

**Body contact** is incidental or positional contact between two players who are both playing the puck — leaning on each other, rubbing shoulders as you skate the same direction, boxing someone out at the net front, sealing a player off along the wall. Hockey Canada's rulebook defines body contact as "incidental contact of two opposing players in pursuit of the puck on the ice in the same direction" and states plainly that no penalty is assessed for it. **Body contact is legal in every league, at every age, in every division — with one published exception and one limit at the wall.** If your league runs the CARHA adult book, Rule 49(a) reaches a player who intentionally bodies, pushes or shoves an opponent, so deliberate leaning is not free there; the rule is set out in full [below](#where-body-checking-is-and-is-not-permitted). And in IIHF women's hockey the seal along the wall is position only: get there first, take the ice between her and the puck and hold it, but do not use the boards to push, pin or eliminate her (Rule 101.1).

**Body checking** is a deliberate act: you use your body to separate an opponent from the puck. USA Hockey defines a body check as "intentional physical contact, from the front, diagonally from the front or straight from the side, by a skater to an opponent who is in control of the puck." Hockey Canada adds the mechanical test — body checking occurs "when a player makes deliberate contact with an opposing player with opposite-directional force, when a player leaves their skating lane to make contact, or when a player extends an arm, shoulder, or hip after angling an opponent." **Body checking is legal only in specific classifications.**

### Where body checking is and is not permitted

**In short:** Find out, in writing, whether your league and this particular game allow body checking; your age alone does not settle it.

```facts
Rule: USA Hockey Rule 604(a) bans body checking at 12U and below, in all girls'/women's classifications and in all non-check adult classifications — and the same rule lets a local governing body prohibit it in any classification, so check what your own association has decided
Rule: Hockey Canada bans body checking at U13 and below, throughout female hockey, and in "any other divisions approved by a Member of Hockey Canada" — so being above the U13 line does not settle it (Hockey Canada Rule 7.3)
Rule: When a checking team plays a non-checking team, body checking is prohibited outright (USA Hockey Rule 604(b))
Rule: A third USA Hockey prohibition sits outside Rule 604 — body checking is "prohibited during all Blind Hockey games", penalised "under the guidelines within Rule 604", with no age or classification limb, so it reaches adult rosters the 604(a) list does not
Never: Assume your league permits checking — USA Hockey Rule 604(a) lets a local governing body prohibit it in any classification, so ask your own association and get the answer in writing
Rule: CARHA has no checking division — 49(a) makes intentionally bodying, pushing or shoving an opponent a minor or major anywhere, a major if it injures; a major ends your night (30(a)) and brings at least a one-game suspension (32(d)) (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Rule: In CARHA adult leagues, avoid contact — 49(a) reaches a player who "does not avert body contact"; 49(c) excuses accidents, but accidental non-stick head, face or neck contact is a minor (50(a)), and a player driven from behind into the boards or net unable to protect themselves a match (53(b))
Rule: CARHA Rule 32(a) ejects you on three penalties in a game — its Note 1 tallies any combination of minors, bench minors and majors, and Rule 49(b)'s Note counts a boarding double minor as two of the three, so boarding plus one more minor is your night (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Key: "Non-check" does not mean "non-contact" — USA Hockey says a Competitive Contact game can be very physical
```

| League / level | Body checking? | Source |
|---|---|---|
| **NHL** | Yes | NHL rulebook |
| **USA Hockey — 14U and older boys'/men's check classifications** | Yes, **unless** your local governing body has prohibited it — 604(a) lets it do that in any classification | USA Hockey Rule 604(a) |
| **USA Hockey — 12U and below, all Girls'/Women's classifications, and all non-check Adult classifications** | **No** | USA Hockey Rule 604(a) |
| **Hockey Canada — U13 and below** | **No** | Hockey Canada Rule 7.3 |
| **Hockey Canada — female hockey** | **No** | Hockey Canada Rule 7.3 |
| **Hockey Canada — any division a Member of Hockey Canada has approved** | **No** | Hockey Canada Rule 7.3 (the rulebook does not list which. Ask your league) |
| **Hockey Canada — U15 and above (male)** | Yes, **unless** your division is one of those Member-approved above | Hockey Canada Rule 7.3 |
| **IIHF women's international** | Restricted — see below | IIHF Rule 101.1 |
| **Women's hockey in Britain — the rule-book layer** | Restricted, same as IIHF; your league may go further, and WNIHL does | IIHF Rule 101.1, unamended by the In-House Rules |
| **PWHL** | Restricted — see below | PWHL Rule 52.1 |
| **British hockey — the four IHUK regulations read here** | **Junior U10 and U12 no, U14 and above yes; NIHL Division 1 and 2 yes; WNIHL no** — set by the competition regulations, since neither rule book states an age | IHUK Rules of Competition 2026-27 |
| **Adult rec / beer leagues** | **Ask your league** — your association may have barred it whatever the age list says | USA Hockey Rule 604(a); Hockey Canada Rule 7.3 |

Six things that catch people out:

- **Under Hockey Canada, being above the U13 line does not mean you are in a checking division.** Rule 7.3 has a third limb people miss: it applies "only in divisions of U13 and below, in female hockey, **and any other divisions approved by a Member of Hockey Canada**." The rulebook does not say which divisions those are, so an adult or U15 division can be non-check by approval and you will not find it in the playing rules. Ask your league.
- **A local governing body can ban checking in any classification — including one your age would otherwise put you in.** USA Hockey Rule 604(a) says so straight after its age list: *"A local governing body may prohibit body checking in any classification and is encouraged to provide Competitive Contact options at all levels of recreational play."* Clearing 14U tells you what the national rule permits, not what your association did with it — and the officials enforce your association's decision. **Being an adult does not put you in a checking class by default either.** USA Hockey's Glossary names the adult classifications — *"Adult non-check, Adult U.S., Adult Elite and Adult Women"* — and two of the four are non-check, Adult Women under 604(a)'s girls'/women's limb. Ask, and get it in writing.
- **When a checking team plays a non-checking team, nobody checks — and it is not the coaches' call.** USA Hockey Rule 604(b) prohibits body checking outright in that game, and the Casebook puts exactly that question — a non-league fixture where *"[b]oth coaches agree to play the game under the Body Checking category rules"* — and answers *"No"*: *"Coaches do not have the authority to decide what rules they choose to play under on any given day"* (Rule 604 Situation 1).
- **One USA Hockey prohibition sits outside Rule 604 altogether, and it is the one that reaches adults.** It is in the book's *Disabled Hockey* section rather than its playing rules: *"Body checking is prohibited during all Blind Hockey games. Body checking shall be penalized under the guidelines within Rule 604 in the Official Rules of Ice Hockey."* It carries **no age or classification limb** at all. A Blind Hockey roster *"can comprise all youth-aged players (players under 18), **all adult-aged players (18 and older)**, or mixed youth and adult-aged players"*, so it reaches adult rosters the 604(a) list does not. **Check the game, not the age group.** *(Only the Blind Hockey body-checking provision is described here; the Sled Hockey rules carry a separate Non-Contact Player provision this document does not cover)*.
- **"Non-check" does not mean "non-contact."** USA Hockey states it in as many words: "Non-check hockey does not mean no contact and the Competitive Contact Category game can be very physical."
- **One published adult book answers the question for you, and its answer is no.** If your league runs the **CARHA Hockey** rule book — **CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only, and no reader in minor hockey** — there is no checking division at all. ⚠️ **So never body, push or shove anyone on purpose, get out of the way of contact you could avoid, and keep your hands, arms and shoulders below an opponent's collar even in a scramble you did not start.** Accidental contact draws no penalty under **49(c)**, so the line is intent rather than touch — except at the head and from behind into the boards. CARHA is not a general rec-hockey standard; it is here because it answers this question for many adult readers. Ask your league whether it is your book.

  **What it costs.** **Rule 49(a)** assesses *"[a] Minor or Major penalty, at the discretion of the Referee, based upon the intent and the violence of the impact with the opposing player… to any player who intentionally bodies, pushes, shoves, stands in front of an opponent for the purpose of making contact, and/or does not avert body contact with an opponent. When injury results from intentional body contact a Major penalty shall be assessed."* That last limb, *"does not avert body contact"*, reads as a duty to get out of the way rather than only a ban on starting contact *(a reading of the rule's grammar; the book does not gloss it)*.

  Its case situations price a body check at **centre ice**, nobody hurt, at a minor or a major. One **into the boards** is a double minor under **49(b)**, a major if the player is injured, and a match if the referee judges it serious. **49(d)** adds a minor or a major for deliberate contact after the whistle. Accidental contact *"in the head, face or neck with any part of the player’s body or equipment other than a stick"* is still a minor (**Rule 50(a)**; a stick goes to Rule 62), and a hit from behind into the boards is a match under **Rule 53(b)** where the player could not protect themselves.

  **A major there** ⚠️ **is the end of your night, plus the next game:** **Rule 30(a)** rules any player assessed a major *"off the ice for the remainder of the game (Major penalty plus Game Misconduct)"*, its only exception an accidental high stick that injures nobody (Note to **Rule 62(b)**). **Rule 32(d)** adds an automatic suspension of at least one game. **Rule 32(a)** ejects you on **three penalties in one game**, counting *"[a]ny combination of minor, bench minor, or major penalties"* — ⚠️ **and a 49(b) boarding double minor counts as two of the three**, so a boarding call plus one more minor ends it.

### If you play in Britain

**In short:** Take your checking format from your own competition's Rules of Competition, and until you know it, do not hit anyone.

```facts
Rule: Your book is the IIHF Rule Book, amended by the In-House Rules — the USA Hockey and Hockey Canada rows above are not yours
Rule: Neither the IIHF Rule Book nor the In-House Rules states an age at which body checking becomes legal — your competition's Rules of Competition carry it instead, and only four of IHUK's were read here: junior, U10, NIHL Division 1 and 2 and the WNIHL
Rule: IHUK's Rules of Competition 2026-27 set the format: junior U10 and U12 "non-checking" and U14, U16 and U19 "checking"; NIHL Division 1 and 2 "checking"; WNIHL "non-checking" at U16 and senior — and the U10 document adds, in terms, "Bodychecking is NOT allowed in U10 matches"
Never: Assume Britain mirrors USA Hockey's 12U or Hockey Canada's U13 — the British junior line falls between U12 and U14, and it comes from a competition regulation, not from either rule book
Action: Get your own division's contact status in writing from your league, and until you have an answer, do not hit anyone
Never: Settle it by asking whether IHUK runs your league — IHUK's In-House Rules reach NIHL National, the SNL and BUIHA Varsity, and no Rules of Competition for any of them was read for this guide
Never: Read IHUK's junior, U10, NIHL 1 and 2 and WNIHL Rules of Competition as settling the EIHL, BUIHA, NIHL National, the PNIHL, the SNL or Scottish domestic hockey outside the IHUK junior structure
Rule: British junior categories are U19/U16/U14/U12/U10 by birth year and unisex, "not classified as 'Mens' within the UK" (In-House Rules, Rule 200)
Rule: In women's hockey under EIH, Adult runs from the 14th birthday and Under 16 from the 10th (In-House Rules, Rule 100.1)
Rule: In British women's hockey at every age, adult included, any major penalty carries an automatic game misconduct and ejection — In-House Rule 101's ejection bullet applies "in all cases" and states no age scope (In-House Rules, Rule 101)
Rule: The age-scoped half of In-House Rule 101 is its other bullet, which applies IIHF Rule 201.1 to non-adult women's categories — it does not narrow the ejection bullet beside it
Key: Read junior hockey the same way at every age, U19 to U10 — In-House Rule 201 scopes the IIHF junior rules to all junior groups without naming 201.1, so treat a major as ending the game
```

Every age line set out above is North American — USA Hockey's and Hockey Canada's — and none of them transfers to Britain. **Your book is the IIHF Rule Book**, adopted by Ice Hockey UK and the BUIHA for *"England, Wales, Scotland and Northern Ireland (the Home Countries)"*, with the **In-House Rules** on top of it as amendments. So the IIHF rows and flags in this document — Rule 43 on checking from behind, Rule 42 on charging, Rule 48 on head contact, Rule 101.1 on women's hockey — describe your game; the USA Hockey and Hockey Canada rows do not. [UK and England Rules](../foundation/uk_rules.md) has the full amendment list.

**Neither rule book states an age at which body checking becomes legal in Britain.** The IIHF book restricts checking in one place only, Rule 101.1 on women's hockey, and its junior rules say nothing about contact; the In-House Rules add nothing either. ⚠️ **So do not borrow a North American age line: the answer lives in your competition's own *Rules of Competition*.** Four of IHUK's 2026-27 editions were read for this document — junior, U10, NIHL Division 1 and 2, and WNIHL.

**For IHUK junior hockey**, the *Junior Rules of Competition 2026-27* set **U10** *"Cross-ice jamboree, non-checking"*, **U12** *"Full ice, non-checking, running clock"*, and **U14**, **U16** and **U19** each *"Full ice, checking, stop clock"*. So the British junior line falls **between U12 and U14** — a year later than USA Hockey's and a year earlier than Hockey Canada's, which is why guessing from either fails. The *U10 Rules of Competition* says it in terms: *"Bodychecking is NOT allowed in U10 matches."*

**In the senior game the league decides it, not your age.** NIHL Division 1 and 2 is *"Full ice, checking, stop clock"*. The WNIHL sets **both** U16 and senior as *"Full ice, non-checking, stop clock"* — tighter than IIHF Rule 101.1, which permits a limited form of body checking, so plan around the tighter one. ⚠️ **And being a woman does not by itself put you in a non-checking game**: the NIHL document contemplates that *"[s]enior women may register WNIHL + NIHL in different clubs"*, and NIHL Division 1 and 2 is a checking format.

That does not conflict with the rule books. The IIHF book never grants a right to check — it defines a legal check and penalises illegal ones — so a competition declaring its games non-checking adds a restriction to a silence.

**Those four documents are the whole of the British answer given here.** None sets a checking format for the **EIHL**, **BUIHA** university hockey, **NIHL National**, the **PNIHL**, the **SNL**, or Scottish domestic competitions outside the IHUK junior structure, and no regulation for those was read. ⚠️ **That is "unanswered", not "different there" — do not read either way into it.** Asking whether IHUK runs your league does not settle it: IHUK owns and operates the NIHL leagues, NIHL National included, but the document covering that tier was not one of the four. IHUK can also move a line mid-season by Rule Bulletin. ⚠️ **So if one of the four covers you, take the format from your own season's Rules of Competition; otherwise ask your club or league secretary and get it in writing — and until you have an answer, do not hit anyone.**

**The age bands themselves differ.** British junior categories — **U19, U16, U14, U12 and U10** — go by **birth year** (In-House Rule 200), and *"are unisex and are not classified as 'Mens' within the UK"*, so there is no British *boys'* checking division to map a USA Hockey row onto. In women's hockey, **Adult runs from the player's 14th birthday** and Under 16 from the 10th to the 16th (Rule 100.1).

**Plan around one consequence in British women's hockey, at every age, adult included: any foul that draws a major also gets you thrown out of the game.** In-House Rule 101 adds a game misconduct and ejection to every major *"in all cases"*, with no age scope. The bullet above it, which applies IIHF Rule 201.1 to non-adult categories, does not narrow it. For **junior** hockey, In-House Rule 201 applies the IIHF junior playing rules to *"all junior age groups"* without naming 201.1, so read the same consequence across U19 to U10 as an interpretation rather than the rule's own words. Either way, treat a major for charging, boarding or checking from behind as ending your game, and confirm with your league.

### Women's hockey — the nuance that is usually got wrong

**In short:** Under every women's book, hold your ground and play the puck; never step or glide into an opponent.

```facts
Key: "Women's hockey has no hitting" is too blunt — the answer depends on which book you are under
Rule: USA Hockey and Hockey Canada prohibit body checking in all girls'/women's classifications
Rule: IIHF women's rules allow body checking only with a clear intention of playing or gaining possession of the puck — any step or glide into an opponent is at least a minor, and the boards may never be used to push, pin or eliminate her (IIHF Rule 101.1)
Rule: Two players in pursuit of the puck may push and lean into each other only while "possession of the puck" remains "the sole object" of both — leaning on after that is an illegal hit (IIHF Rule 101.1)
Rule: The PWHL permits angling with the body to separate an opponent from the puck (PWHL Rule 52.1)
Never: Step or glide into an opponent, or move into a puck carrier trying to avoid you — that is an illegal hit
Rule: IIHF women's rules forbid using the boards to eliminate, push or pin an opponent (IIHF Rule 101.1)
Rule: A stationary player is entitled to their ice, and a puck carrier skating directly at her must avoid contact (IIHF Rule 101.1)
Rule: An illegal hit in women's hockey is a minor, or a major plus an automatic game misconduct (IIHF Rule 101.1)
Rule: Britain applies IIHF Rule 101.1, the women’s body-checking rule, unamended, though the WNIHL's Rules of Competition label both its levels non-checking; the 14th-birthday Adult threshold is in neither 101.1 nor IIHF Rule 100.1, but a domestic amendment (In-House Rules, Rule 100.1)
Rule: IIHF Rule 100.1 sets women's Senior at eighteen by 31 December of the year the Championship Season ends, with a waiver for a 16th or 17th birthday that year, and Under 18 at fifteen to eighteen — four years above the British Adult line
```

The common statement "women's hockey has no hitting" is too blunt. What is accurate:

- **USA Hockey and Hockey Canada prohibit body checking in all girls'/women's classifications.** That part is simple and absolute.
- **IIHF women's rules permit a restricted form of body checking — the rule-book layer women's hockey in Britain plays under, though the WNIHL goes further and makes both its levels non-checking.** IIHF Rule 101.1 allows body checking *"when there is a clear intention of playing the puck or attempting to 'gain possession' of the puck"*, and two players chasing the puck *"are reasonably allowed to push and lean into each other provided that 'possession of the puck' remains the sole object of the two"* Players. **What is banned is the hit: never step or glide into her.** *"Any move by a Player to step or glide into an opposing Player will be assessed at least a minor penalty (2') for an 'illegal hit'"*, and the rule runs from a minor to a major plus an automatic game misconduct. Three further clauses decide most real contests:
  - **The boards.** Where two or more players are competing for possession, *"they are not allowed to use the boards to make contact with an opponent to eliminate her from the play, push her into the boards, or pin her along the boards."*
  - **Standing your ground is protected.** *"A Player, who is stationary, is entitled to that area of the ice. It is up to the opponent to avoid body contact with such a Player… the opponent is obliged to skate around the stationary Player."* And: *"Players are allowed to 'hold their ground' any time that they have established their position on the ice."* Everything in Section 4 of this document is available to you.
  - **The obligation runs both ways.** *"If a Player with the puck is skating directly at an opponent who is stationary, it is the obligation of the puck carrier to 'avoid contact'."* But if the carrier makes every effort to avoid contact and the stationary player moves into her, it is the *defender* who takes at least a minor.
- **The PWHL permits the same restricted form — finish the angle with your feet, never with a step into her.** PWHL Rule 52.1: "In the PWHL, a form of Bodychecking is permitted. Players may angle their opponent by using their body in order to separate her opponent from the puck." Same-direction contact that dislodges the puck is legal; "opposite-directional force," leaving your skating lane to make contact, or extending an arm, shoulder or hip *after* angling are illegal hits. Its lowest limb has no direction or lane test to fail: *"[a]ny move by a player to step or glide into an opposing player will be assessed at least a Minor Penalty (2') for an 'illegal hit'."* And if the carrier *"makes every effort to 'avoid contact'"* and you move into her, that is at least a minor on you.

**In Britain, Rule 101.1 is your rule as written** — the In-House Rules do not amend it, though the WNIHL makes both its levels non-checking. What they do change is what a major costs: **at every age, adult included, a major in women's hockey also gets you thrown out of the game** (In-House Rule 101, *"in all cases"*). So an illegal hit serious enough to draw the 101.1 major ends your game whatever your age; the 14th-birthday Adult line changes your age category, not that.

**That 14th-birthday Adult line is British, not international — do not cite it to the IIHF.** The IIHF's own Rule 100.1 sets women's "Senior" at eighteen by 31 December of the year the Championship Season ends, with a waiver for a 16th or 17th birthday that year, and "Under 18" at fifteen to eighteen. Rule 101.1, the body-checking rule itself, carries no age at all. See [UK and England Rules](../foundation/uk_rules.md).

In practice, the women's-game standard is very close to the standard for **legal angling everywhere** — which is the subject of the next section.

### Before the puck drops — the warm-up is not outside the rules

**In short:** Keep your hands off opponents and stay in your own half in the warm-up — no book here treats it as off the record.

```facts
Rule: Three books penalise the pre-game warm-up — USA Hockey 502(b), "Penalties may also be assessed during the warm-up period"; Hockey Canada 2.2(j), the penalty "served at the start of the game"; CARHA 27(b), for infractions "committed during the pre-game warm-up, when observed by the Referee"
Rule: IIHF Rule 5.6's "No penalties are to be assessed but a report of the incident must be submitted to the Proper Authorities" is the enforcement clause for that rule's own terms on warm-up equipment, roster and timing — identical in both editions, and not a general amnesty for warm-up conduct
Rule: A warm-up fight is penalised on the night under the IIHF — players fighting "other than during the periods of the game shall be assessed a major penalty for fighting and an automatic game misconduct" (Rule 46.8 in the 2026/27 book; the same rule is numbered 46.9 in 2025/26 Version 1.1)
Rule: In the EIHL the warm-up is a non-contact period by rule — Casebook 86.6(i), "Physical contact between players on opposing teams is not allowed during the warmup and players are not allowed to skate over the centre red line during this time"
Rule: The EIHL Casebook reverses IIHF 5.6 for the Elite League alone — referees "are authorised to call all penalties provided for in the rule book from the start of warm-up time until 30 minutes after the end of the game, if they see the offence take place" (86.6)
Rule: An EIHL warm-up violation reaches the bench — suspension and/or fine for players, team officials and the club, and the head coach "who instigated (started) or provoked such activity" is liable too (Casebook 86.6, with 5.6(1))
Never: Put a hand on an opponent, or cross the centre red line, in the warm-up — no book here treats it as off the record, and under the IIHF a warm-up fight is a fighting major and an automatic ejection
```

The warm-up is the one stretch of the night when you share the ice with opponents and there is usually no referee standing on it, which is exactly why a shove at the red line can become a suspension rather than two minutes. The books do not answer it the same way.

- **Three of the books simply penalise it.** **USA Hockey Rule 502(b)**: *"[p]enalties may also be assessed during the warm-up period."* **Hockey Canada Rule 2.2(j)** has the referee *"penalize any player who commits a violation of the rules during the pre-game warm-up"*, and *"[t]he penalty will be served at the start of the game"* — so your team opens shorthanded. ⚠️ **Its Note 1 lets a player finish the warm-up before serving a minor, bench minor or misconduct — not a major, match or game misconduct.** And **4.1(a)** puts the player's name *"on the Official Game Report"*. **CARHA Rule 27(b)** penalises infractions *"committed during the pre-game warm-up, when observed by the Referee"*, with the same Official Game Report consequence *(CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)*.
- **The IIHF looks like the exception, and it is the book most British readers are under — but its Rule 5.6 is narrower than it reads.** **Rule 5.6**, identical in the 2025/26 (Version 1.1) and 2026/27 editions, sets the terms of the on-ice warm-up — equipment properly worn, who may take part, no warm-up at the end of a period or at a stoppage — and then ends: *"No penalties are to be assessed but a report of the incident must be submitted to the Proper Authorities."* ⚠️ **That is the enforcement clause for those terms, not a general amnesty for anything done in the warm-up.**
- **A warm-up fight is penalised on the night under the IIHF — a British reader must not get this wrong.** **Rule 46.8** in the 2026/27 book (46.9 in 2025/26 Version 1.1): *"Players involved in fighting other than during the periods of the game shall be assessed a major penalty for fighting and an automatic game misconduct."* Only under the 2026/27 book, not the 2025/26 one the In-House Rules adopt, can a player who only *"defends"* themselves have the game misconduct waived at the referee's discretion — ⚠️ **the ejection, not the major**. **In British women's or junior hockey do not count on even that**, because a major ejects there (In-House Rules 101 and 201). The IIHF's own warm-up rule, **86.6**, points the same way: it keeps each team in its own end and points a fight before the puck drops to 46.9, which hands one before the start of the game or a period to 46.8 (2026/27 numbering; in 2025/26, 86.6 points to 46.10, which leaves it to 46.9). **So "nothing is called in the warm-up" is false under the IIHF for the one thing most likely to happen in one.**
  - **The IIHF book does not reconcile 5.6 and 46.8, and this guide does not invent a reconciliation.** Read 5.6 as scoped to its own subject and the fighting rule as scoped to fighting — the reading the rest of the book supports, and the safe one either way. For warm-up conduct the fighting rule does not reach, 5.6's route applies: nothing called on the night, but a report to the Proper Authorities. Supplementary discipline has no five-minute ceiling and can reach games. ⚠️ **Do not read "no penalties are to be assessed" as "nothing happens."**
- **The EIHL reverses that for the Elite League alone, and adds a contact rule of its own.** **EIHL Casebook 26-27 Rule 86.6**: *"Physical contact between players on opposing teams is not allowed during the warmup and players are not allowed to skate over the centre red line during this time. Violations of these rules will be handled by the standby referee who oversees the warmup."* **So in that league the warm-up is a non-contact period by rule, whatever the game itself permits.** The same rule reverses IIHF 5.6: *"Referees are authorised to call all penalties provided for in the rule book from the start of warm-up time until 30 minutes after the end of the game, if they see the offence take place."* And the cost reaches the bench: *"players, team officials and the clubs will be subjected to a suspension and/or fine"*, including *"the Head Coach of the team who instigated (started) or provoked such activity"*. Casebook **5.6(1)** also reaches a player who crosses the centre red line *"(inclusive of body and/or equipment)"* to start *"any sort of unsportsmanlike behaviour (including shooting pucks at opponent's end)"*.
  - **Two things in that rule qualify each other.** The next limb says warm-up violations *"cannot be enforced by on-ice officials because they are not on the ice to witness the violations first-hand, but these may be reviewed after the game by the proper authorities"* — while the authorisation above is conditioned *"if they see the offence take place"*, and a standby referee does oversee the warm-up. ⚠️ **Read together: what is seen can be called, and what is not seen is reviewed afterwards. Neither makes the warm-up free.**
  - **The Casebook prints that authorisation twice.** The Preamble's copy omits *"if they see the offence take place"*; the copy at 86.6 carries it. Quote the 86.6 version, which is printed with the rule.
- **Scope this to the EIHL and no further.** The In-House Rules for 2026-27, which govern NIHL, the SNL, BUIHA Varsity, junior and women's hockey, contain no warm-up conduct provision beyond a pre-warm-up equipment check. So there **the IIHF book stands unmodified, both halves of it**: Rule 5.6's report route for the warm-up terms it covers, **and a major plus an automatic game misconduct for a warm-up fight** (Rule 46.8 in the 2026/27 book, 46.9 in 2025/26). See [UK and England Rules](../foundation/uk_rules.md).

### What this means for how you read the rest of this document

**In short:** Almost everything here applies in every league, and nothing in it is a licence to hit where your league forbids it.

```facts
Key: Almost everything here applies in every league — angling, stick checks, body position, receiving a hit safely, battles, shot blocking
Priority: Only Section 5's legal body check needs a full-checking game, and it is not free even there. Walking a screener out is never yours, in any game — they have no puck, so it can be called interference
Rule: Walking a screener out can be called interference in any game — NHL 56.2(iii), IIHF 56.2(III), PWHL 57.2(iii) make a deliberate check on a puckless player a minor; a violent or reckless one can be a major (56.4, 57.4), in some books with an ejection, and one that injures can end your game
Rule: Women's hockey is never full-checking — IIHF Rule 101.1 allows body checking only to play or gain the puck, never using the boards to push, pin or eliminate an opponent, and makes any step or glide into one an illegal hit; the WNIHL bars checking at U16 and senior
Rule: Women's hockey bars using the boards on her in any wall battle (IIHF Rule 101.1) — hold ice by the wall you reached first, but never push, pin or eliminate her against it
Never: Treat anything here as a licence to hit, push or shove an opponent in a league that does not allow it
Key: Holding a position you have established is written as legal in the NHL, IIHF, USA Hockey, PWHL and, for defenders, CARHA — moving an opponent off theirs is not, and CARHA adds a duty to avert contact
Rule: The NHL, the IIHF and the PWHL write it in full — Rule 56.1 in the first two, 57.1 in the third — and USA Hockey in its Standard of Play summaries, scoped to no age or category: "entitled to the ice they occupy". Hockey Canada has no such sentence; CARHA grants defenders the standing half only
Rule: The PWHL grants body position in its interference rule — 57.1, a player is "allowed the ice she is standing on (body position) and is not required to move in order to let an opponent proceed"
Rule: As a CARHA defender, hold a spot you took but do not lean, body or stand in front of someone to make contact — 66(a) Note 2 lets defenders "stand their ground", but the side with the puck "shall not be allowed to 'run' deliberate interference"; 49(a) prices contact (CARHA adult leagues only)
Rule: CARHA adds a duty to avert body contact — 49(a) reaches a player who "stands in front of an opponent for the purpose of making contact" or "does not avert body contact with an opponent" (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
```

Almost everything here applies in **every league, including yours** — angling, stick checks, body position, receiving a hit safely, board, corner and net-front battles, loose-puck races, shot blocking and staying out of the box. **Three things are not.** The first is **delivering a legal body check** (§5), which applies only if your league permits **full** body checking — women's hockey does not, and its restricted standard is in §1. ⚠️ **Under USA Hockey Rule 604(b) it also depends on the fixture:** the section is not yours in a game between a checking team and a non-checking one, whatever your own classification says.

**The second is the net-front walk-out (§9), and it is not yours in any game: never drive a screener off their spot.** The screener has no puck, so what governs is **interference**, which no book scopes to a division. Establishing position **first** makes holding your spot legal; it never makes moving theirs legal. NHL and IIHF **Rule 56.1** let you hold the ice you are standing on but penalise *"Moving laterally and without establishing body position, then making contact with the non-puck carrier"*, and **USA Hockey Rule 625(a)(4)** names *"[a] defending player who changes their skating lane or foot speed in an effort to play the body of an opponent who is no longer in control of the puck"*.

**What it costs.** **NHL 56.2(iii)** and the PWHL's 57.2(iii) give a minor for deliberately checking *"an opponent, including the goalkeeper, who is not in possession of the puck"*, and the IIHF's 56.2(III) for one *"not or no longer deemed in possession"*. Hockey Canada 8.3(i) and CARHA 66(a)(1) reach any player who impedes an opponent *"not in possession of the puck"*, and USA Hockey's interference rule carries no category scope. **That minor is not the ceiling:** a violent or reckless walk-out can cost a major, in some books an ejection ([What actually gets called](#what-actually-gets-called)). Where checking is barred, the walk-out is also a push: a minor under USA Hockey 604(c) and Hockey Canada 7.3(a). **Neither minor is the ceiling in its own book**: each runs on to a major plus a game misconduct **with nobody hurt** and then a match ([Defending the net front](#defending-the-net-front)). **In women's hockey it is an illegal hit under IIHF Rule 101.1:** at least a minor, up to a *"Major penalty (5') and automatic game misconduct penalty"*, with no injury condition in either.

**The third is scoped to the women's game rather than to checking: using the boards on an opponent** anywhere along the wall. IIHF Rule 101.1 forbids players competing for the puck from using the boards *"to make contact with an opponent to eliminate her from the play, push her into the boards, or pin her along the boards"*. Beat her to the wall, take the ice between her and the puck, hold it and play the puck — pin the puck, not the player. All three are flagged where they appear.

**The principle to carry: holding a position you have already established is not what these rules penalise; moving an opponent off theirs is.** Under CARHA even that needs care. **Rule 49(a)** reaches a player who *"stands in front of an opponent for the purpose of making contact, and/or does not avert body contact with an opponent"*. Note 2 to its Rule 66(a) still says defending players *"are entitled to 'stand their ground' or 'shadow' the attacking players"*. So the line runs between holding a spot you took and leaning or bodying to keep it *(CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)*. Nothing in this document is a licence to hit in a league that does not allow it.

---

## 2. Angling — Legal in Every League, If You Hold Your Lane and Add Nothing

**In short:** Pick your line early, hold it, and add nothing when you arrive; the angle is skated, not thrown.

**Angling is steering an opponent to where you want them by choosing your skating route, not by hitting them.** USA Hockey's glossary defines it as "a legal defensive skill used to direct/control the puck carrier to an area that closes the gap and creates an opening that is too small for the puck carrier."

**It is legal everywhere, provided you pick your line early, hold it, and add nothing when you arrive.** You may close off the boards and force a carrier to slow down or turn back inside, as long as you do not bump, push or shove them to do it. ⚠️ **Veering across into an opponent at the last instant to make the contact happen is not angling, however gentle the contact turns out to be.** The angle is skated, not thrown: what keeps it legal is the lane you chose three strides ago, and the arm, shoulder or hip you do not add once you arrive.

**Where the books draw that line.** Hockey Canada's interpretation of its no-checking rule: "A player may steer or direct an opposing player into the boards, without actually touching or body-checking them. This is acceptable." USA Hockey's Casebook rules an angle along the boards that knocks the carrier into them and off the puck **no penalty**, *"provided the Team B player does not use any overt hip, shoulder or forearm action to make contact with the opponent and simply maintains their skating lane and focuses on closing the gap and gaining possession of the puck"* (Standard of Play, Situation 15). Hockey Canada makes the same contact a body check *"when a player leaves their skating lane to make contact"*, or *"extends an arm, shoulder, or hip after angling an opponent"* (Rule 7.3). The PWHL writes both limbs in the same words (Rule 52.1).

**IIHF Rule 101.1, the British women's rule, makes the movement itself the foul**: *"Any move by a Player to step or glide into an opposing Player will be assessed at least a minor penalty (2') for an 'illegal hit'."* USA Hockey, Hockey Canada and the PWHL grant angling in terms; the NHL book never uses the word, and angling is legal there because nothing in it reaches a contactless skating route.

This is how good defenders actually take the puck. Not by hitting people.

![](diagram:rush-gap-and-angle)

**That picture is borrowed from [Defending the Rush](../systems/defending_the_rush.md)**, and the distances in its caption are gap-control targets for a defender retreating on a one-on-one; this section sets no distance of its own. What carries across is the geometry: the defender's body between the carrier and the middle, the shaded ice the route takes away, and only the wall left open. What no still picture shows is that the defender was already at speed — **a gap cannot be closed from a standstill**, which is why an angle has to start before you need it.

### The four things you are managing

**In short:** Skate at the ice they want, with momentum back toward your net, and arrive under control, stick in the lane.

```facts
Action: Skate at the ice they want, not at the player. Take away their good ice and force them to bad ice
Position: Approach on an angle that leaves your momentum heading back toward your own net, not sideways across the ice
Technique: Arrive under control — slow your feet as the gap shrinks, knees bent so you can still change direction
Never: Cross over as you close — a crossover commits your weight, and a good attacker cuts back against it
Rule: Hold the skating lane you chose — USA Hockey's Casebook clears the angler who "simply maintains their skating lane", and Hockey Canada makes the same contact a body check "when a player leaves their skating lane to make contact" (Rule 7.3)
Technique: Stick on the ice in a sweeping manner, blade covering the inside lane while your body covers the outside
Goal: A gap too small for the puck carrier to cut inside
```

**1. Your route, not their route.** Skate at the ice they want, not at the player. If they are trying to get to the middle, your line takes the middle away and leaves them the wall. Coaches call the middle **good ice** and the outside **bad ice**; USA Hockey's coaching write-up puts the aim as *"to take away an opponent's good ice and force them to bad ice"*.

**2. Momentum back toward your own net.** Approach on an angle that leaves you travelling *back* toward the goal you are defending, not sideways across the ice. Seth Appert, head coach of the U.S. National Under-18 Team, in the same write-up: "When you're skating to take away time and space from your opponent, one thing you need to have for the proper angle is momentum back toward your net." If they get rid of the puck, you are already going the right way and you stay above them (between them and your net). If you angled flat across the ice and they beat you, you are chasing.

![](diagram:angling-your-route)

**3. Closing speed.** Arrive under control. If you close at full speed, one inside cut beats you and you are 20 feet out of the play. Slow your feet as the gap shrinks and keep your knees bent so you can change direction. The gap you are aiming for is small enough that a cut inside is not available — that is what "creates an opening that is too small for the puck carrier" means.

- **Do not cross over as you close.** A crossover commits your weight to one direction, and a good attacker cuts back against a committed defender. Stay on an inside edge through the last few strides so you can still change direction. (Crossovers are for *building* the speed that gets you to the angle, not for arriving on it — see [Skating](skating.md).)

**4. Stick on the ice, blade in the passing lane.** Appert again: "you want your stick on the ice in a sweeping manner and protecting good ice." Your stick covers the inside — the lane back to the middle — while your body covers the outside. At the moment of contact, "if your opponent still has the puck, you want your stick on the puck."

The picture above draws the first two of these four: the route the defender skates, and the direction that route leaves them travelling. The other two — closing speed, and where the stick is — are not things a still picture can show.

### Where the angle ends

```facts
Action: In a non-checking league, end the angle with a seal — take away the last of their space and arrive stick-on-puck
Key: Both leaning, both playing the puck, both going the same direction is legal competitive contact
Action: In a checking league, the same angle ends with a rub-out or a check under the Section 5 rules
Priority: The angle comes first, the contact is the last step
Never: Veer across into them at the last instant, or add an arm, shoulder or hip once you arrive — either turns a legal angle into a body check
Never: Throw a hit without an angle — a hit from bad position is where most penalties come from, on the coaching account rather than a count of why penalties were taken
Rule: One adult book limits where the angle may end — CARHA 49(a) reaches a player who "stands in front of an opponent for the purpose of making contact" or "does not avert body contact", so the seal is not automatically safe there (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
```

In a **non-checking league**, the angle ends with a seal: your body position takes away the last of their space, you arrive stick-on-puck, and you either take it or force a dump. Contact happens — both leaning, both playing the puck, both going the same direction — and that is legal competitive contact. ⚠️ **Under CARHA the seal is not automatically safe: ride it with the puck, never through the player.** **CARHA Rule 49(a)** (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only) reaches a player who *"stands in front of an opponent for the purpose of making contact, and/or does not avert body contact with an opponent"* — a duty to get out of the way, on a reading of the rule's grammar that CARHA does not gloss. **Rule 49(c)** keeps genuinely accidental contact outside it, except to the head, face or neck (a minor, 50(a)) or from behind into the boards or net (a match, 53(b)). What a penalty there costs is in [§1](#where-body-checking-is-and-is-not-permitted).

In a **checking league**, the same angle ends with a rub-out or a check, delivered under the rules in Section 5. **The angle comes first; the contact is the last step.** A hit thrown without an angle is thrown from bad position, which is where most penalties come from — on the coaching account, since no published study codes *why* a penalty was taken ([Game Management, "Frustration penalties"](../systems/game_management.md#frustration-penalties)).

**Angling is a skating skill before it is a defensive skill.** See [Skating](skating.md) for the crossovers, pivots and edge control it demands, and [Defending the Rush](../systems/defending_the_rush.md) for how angling combines with gap control on an oncoming attack.

---

## 3. Stick Checks — Legal, Effective, and Under-Used

A **stick check** is any attempt to win the puck with your stick rather than your body. All four below are legal skills in every league — but *where* you take them decides whether they stay legal, and the books do not draw that line in the same place. The rule most players are taught is this:

> **Stick on the puck is legal.** ⚠️ **Stick on their hands or their body is a penalty.**

**You will often hear a third clause — "or stick on their stick" — and it is not universal.** NHL Rule 55.1 (Hooking): "When a player is checking another in such a way that there is only stick-to-stick contact, such action is not to be penalized as hooking." The IIHF and the PWHL carry it too, and it covers a player **checking** an opponent, not stick contact generally. **USA Hockey, Hockey Canada and CARHA make the opponent's stick hookable** (USA Hockey Rule 623's Note; Hockey Canada Rule 8.2; CARHA's Glossary), so under those three "it was only stick-on-stick" is no defence. And NHL Rule 61.1 (Slashing): "Any forceful or powerful chop with the stick on an opponent's body, the opponent's stick, or on or near the opponent's hands that, in the judgment of the Referee, is not an attempt to play the puck, shall be penalized as slashing."

Read that second one carefully. Chopping *at their stick* is still slashing if it is a chop rather than an attempt to play the puck. The difference is intent and violence, and referees are watching for it.

### The poke check

```facts
Technique: A short, straight jab of the blade at the puck, one hand or two, from a controlled distance
Read: Use it when you are square to a carrier with the puck out in front — receiving, looking up, or beginning a deke
Technique: Top hand on the knob, extend from the shoulder — do not lunge with your whole body
Action: Poke, recover, stay skating
Never: Poke from too far away — if your blade cannot reach the puck without lunging, you are too far away
```

**What it is:** A short, straight jab of the blade at the puck, one hand or two, from a controlled distance.

**When it works:** When you are square to the puck carrier and they have the puck out in front of them — receiving a pass, looking up, or beginning a deke. It is also the natural finish to an angle, when the carrier has run out of room.

**How to do it:** Keep your top hand on the knob and extend from the shoulder; do not lunge with your whole body. The moment you commit your feet, a good carrier steps around your stick and you have taken yourself out of the play. Poke, recover, stay skating.

**The mistake:** Poking from too far away, missing, and being beaten. If your blade cannot reach the puck without you lunging, you are too far away.

### The stick lift

```facts
Technique: Blade under their shaft low, below their bottom hand and a hand's width clear of the glove — lift crisply up, not sideways
Action: Step through as their blade comes off the ice and take the puck
Read: Lift late — as they are about to shoot, pass or move the puck, not while they are just carrying it
Read: A stick lift on an opponent bent low over the puck goes up at a face down at stick height — catching that face is a high stick however low your hands were. Take it under the shaft, or leave it alone
Position: Works from behind and beside an opponent, at the net front, in a board battle and on a backcheck
Never: Take the lift on a player who does not have the puck and has no pass or shot arriving at their blade — what makes it legal is that it plays the puck; on anyone else it can be restraint
Rule: Stick-to-stick is exempt from hooking only in the NHL, IIHF and PWHL books, and only while checking (NHL and IIHF 55.1; PWHL 56.1) — USA Hockey 623, Hockey Canada 8.2 and CARHA's Glossary name the opponent's stick as a hookable surface (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Rule: IIHF 55.1 makes a stick against, or near, the opponent's hands hooking; its next sentence exempts stick-to-stick contact while checking, and the book reconciles neither. Below their bottom hand the two sentences never meet — no contact at the hands for the first to catch. Low is still the answer
Rule: The NHL's hooking rule writes no hands sentence but its slashing rule does — and so do IIHF 61.1 and PWHL 63.1, word for word, and USA Hockey 634, which drops the "attempt to play the puck" let-out; Hockey Canada 9.3 and CARHA 80 reach a chop there without naming the hands
Rule: Hockey Canada answers the missed stick lift directly, in junior and senior only: Interpretation 2 to Rule 9.5(a), item ii, makes an attempted lift that accidentally contacts the head or neck and causes injury a double minor
Rule: Under Hockey Canada, in minor and female hockey, a lift that catches the head is Rule 7.6 head contact — a minor if accidental, a major and game misconduct on violence, mandatory if it injures (7.6(b)), and a match if they could not protect themselves (7.6(c))
```

**What it is:** You place your blade under the shaft of their stick, **below the bottom hand and with a clear gap above it**, and lift their blade off the ice — then take the puck out from underneath it.

**Why it is worth learning:**

- It takes the puck with **far less** penalty risk than a poke check (tripping) or a body check (a dozen possible penalties). Not none: every book protects the hands, by one route or another. ⚠️ **Take it under the shaft, below their bottom hand, and you are outside every book's hands provision** — the details are in [Where the stick lift becomes a penalty](#where-the-stick-lift-becomes-a-penalty).
- It works from **behind and beside** an opponent, where you cannot legally do anything else. You cannot restrain a player from behind — NHL Rule 56.1 says a player behind an opponent "may not use his stick, body or free hand in order to restrain his opponent" — but lifting the stick of a player *who has the puck* is playing the puck, not restraining a player.
- It is the correct tool at the **net front**, in a **board battle**, and on a **backcheck** — the three places where amateurs instead reach, hook, or cross-check and go to the box.
- It leaves you **balanced**. You never leave your feet or lean past your centre of gravity, so if it fails you are still in the play.

**How to do it:** Get your blade under their shaft *low*, **below their bottom hand and a hand's width clear of the glove**. That is also where the leverage is: the fulcrum is their top hand, so a contact point further down the shaft has the longer lever arm. Lift crisply — up, not sideways. As their blade comes off the ice, step through and take the puck with your other hand still on your stick. Do it late: lift as they are about to shoot, pass, or move the puck, not while they are just carrying it.

#### Where the stick lift becomes a penalty

```facts
Never: Lift the stick of a player with no puck on or arriving at their blade — it plays no puck, and can be called hooking or holding. With the puck arriving, lift low and let go at once
Never: Catch their glove or forearm on the way up, or yank the stick down or across. That is slashing, roughing, or holding the stick
Rule: Hockey Canada allows the lift "for the purpose of making a play on the puck" (Interpretation 1 to Rule 8.2(a)), but makes it hooking where it contacts the hands and impedes their ability to "pass, shoot, receive, or otherwise propel the puck" — receive is the one that catches you
Rule: A lift that misses an opponent bent low is a high stick however low your own hands were — the reference is the struck player's waist (NHL 60.3; IIHF 60.3 in the 2026/27 book; IIHF Situation Handbook 2026/27, Situation 60.13). In both books, at least a minor, and a double minor where injury results
Rule: A missed lift into a bent-over face is not capped at a double minor: IIHF 60.4, USA Hockey 621(b) and Hockey Canada 7.6(b), and 7.7(b) if highly violent, reach a major and game misconduct with nobody hurt (USA Hockey: a 602(a) match too); NHL 60.4 and PWHL 61.4 match penalties need intent to injure
Rule: CARHA 62(b) — CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only — is a major for contact above an opponent's shoulders, accidental or not, injury or not, with no minor rung there and no windup carve-out; its Note adds a game misconduct when intentional or when it injures
Rule: A chop at the hands reaches a major plus a game misconduct with nobody injured in every book read here — NHL 61.3 with 61.5, IIHF 61.3, PWHL 63.3 with 63.5, USA Hockey 634(b) (plan on a match too, 602(a)), Hockey Canada 9.3(b), and CARHA 80(a) with 30(a) (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Rule: A hooking major comes with a game misconduct, and needs an injury under NHL 55.3 and Hockey Canada 8.2(b), but under IIHF 55.3 and USA Hockey 623(b) reckless endangerment is enough (USA Hockey: plan on a match too, 602(a)) — so a chop at the hands is never the cheaper charge
Rule: Three minor penalties for stick infractions in the same game end your game under Hockey Canada Rule 4.8(b)(i) — a Game Ejection. The rule defines stick infractions as Rules 9.1 to 9.5, so slashing counts and hooking does not — and 9.5 is Junior and Senior only
Rule: Hockey Canada's Interpretation 2 to Rule 4.8(b) counts a double minor as a single infraction towards its three-stick-minor Game Ejection, so a double minor for slashing puts one mark against you rather than two
Rule: A Hockey Canada Game Ejection is not a game misconduct and triggers no automatic suspension — Rule 4.8's Note 1 says so in terms, so a third stick minor costs the rest of that game and the book attaches nothing past the final whistle
Key: Penalties are counted as well as priced — three cheap stick minors end your game under Hockey Canada, and the other books run in-game counts of their own
```

**Lift as the puck arrives, low, and let go.** A lift on a player with no puck on or arriving at their blade plays no puck, and a stick that impedes a player without it can be called hooking or holding, depending on the book. Catching a glove on the way up is slashing or roughing in any of them; yanking the stick down or across is holding the stick.

**At the hands, every book reaches you, by different routes.** 🇬🇧 IIHF Rule 55.1 makes it hooking on location alone: *"If the stick goes against the opponent's hands / or near the opponent's hands, it shall be penalized as 'hooking'."* Its very next sentence exempts stick-to-stick contact while checking, and the book does not reconcile the two — but below the bottom hand they never meet. USA Hockey's Rule 623 catches a stick on the hand or arm that *"takes away the ability for the opponent to pass or shoot the puck with a normal amount of force"*. **Hockey Canada wrote an interpretation about this exact technique:** a player may *"lift an opposing player's stick, for the purpose of making a play on the puck"*, but *"if this action contacts the opponent's hands and impedes their ability to pass, shoot, **receive**, or otherwise propel the puck, then a penalty for Hooking must be assessed"* (Interpretation 1 to Rule 8.2(a)). Note *receive*: taking a pass is the commonest moment a lift lands on the hands.

The NHL's hooking rule has no hands sentence, but its slashing rule does — NHL 61.1 catches a forceful chop *"on or near the opponent's hands"* that *"is not an attempt to play the puck"*. IIHF 61.1 and PWHL 63.1 say the same word for word, and USA Hockey Rule 634 drops the *"attempt to play the puck"* let-out, so there the force of the chop decides. Hockey Canada 9.3 and CARHA 80 reach a chop at the hands without naming them. CARHA never uses the word *lift*, so expect a lift there to be judged under hooking. The IIHF's interference rule, 56.1, also names stopping a player without the puck from *"receiving a pass"* — one more reason to let go at once.

**And a lift that misses an opponent who is bent low over the puck is a high stick, however low your own hands were.** The reference point is the player you hit, not you: NHL 60.3 and the IIHF's 2026/27 60.3 both write *"above the height of the opponent's waist"*. The **IIHF Situation Handbook 2026/27** answers this exact case at **Situation 60.13**: a missed stick lift on a player *"bent over digging the puck out of the corner"*, the striker's stick *"only slightly above the height of their waist"*, gets *"Yes, this is a minor (or double-minor) penalty for high-sticking."* ⚠️ **And the double minor is not the ceiling.** IIHF **60.4**, the same in both editions, and **USA Hockey 621(b)** reach a major plus a game misconduct for reckless endangerment with nobody hurt — under USA Hockey compulsorily, and plan on a match too (602(a)). Hockey Canada reaches that tier on the degree of violence through **7.6(b)** and **7.7(b)**, and NHL 60.4 and PWHL 61.4 are match penalties for an attempted or deliberate injury.

**Hockey Canada answers this case directly, and by division.** In junior and senior, Interpretation 2 to Rule 9.5(a) says that *"When an attempted lifting of the opponent's stick, which results in accidental contact to the head or neck area, causes injury, a double Minor will be assessed"* (item ii). It takes *"any aggressive, reckless, negligent or forceful action of the stick (e.g. pitchfork action in lifting the stick or hooking an opponent)"* that injures to a major plus a game misconduct (item vi). ⚠️ **Below junior the trigger is lower, not higher:** 7.6(a) assesses a minor *"to any player who accidentally contacts an opponent in the head, face, or neck, in minor and female hockey"*, with no injury required — so a missed lift that merely brushes a bent-over face is already a penalty.

**Under CARHA** ⚠️ **it is harsher still** (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only). **Rule 62(b)** makes stick contact above the normal height of an opponent's shoulders, *"accidentally or intentionally"*, **a major** *"whether or not injury results"* — no minor rung above the shoulders, and no route back down, because its head-contact Rule 50 sends stick contact back to Rule 62. Its case situations price a swing at the puck that makes contact *"above waist height of opponent"* at a *"Minor Penalty"* and one *"striking opponent above the normal height of the player's shoulder"* at a *"Major Penalty"*. 62(b)'s Note adds a game misconduct where the high stick was intentional, or accidental and injuring. CARHA writes no windup or follow-through carve-out, so an accidental high stick that injures nobody, a minor at the floor in the other five books, is a five-minute major there. **None of that is a reason to stop lifting sticks; it is the reason to take this one under the shaft or not at all.** The rest of the divergence is in [The stick above the shoulders](#the-stick-above-the-shoulders).

**Which charge you take is the reason to lift rather than chop, even where you think you would get away with it.** Under the NHL and Hockey Canada a hooking major, which carries a game misconduct, needs an **injury** (NHL 55.3; Hockey Canada 8.2(b)), where a slashing major needs only severity of contact (NHL 61.3) or *"the degree of violence of the impact"* (Hockey Canada 9.3(b)). Hockey Canada also bars a match for hooking (8.2(c)), while 9.3(c) provides one for slashing. ⚠️ **Under the IIHF and USA Hockey the two run level, and both run high:** each reaches a major **plus a game misconduct** for recklessly endangering an opponent with nobody hurt. It is at the referee's discretion under IIHF 55.3 and 61.3, and *"shall be assessed"* under USA Hockey 623(b) and 634(b), where you should also plan on a match for a reckless one (602(a)).

**Nor is that the chop's ceiling in most books.** NHL 61.4 and PWHL 63.4 write a match for an attempted or deliberate injury by slashing, and Rule 21.1 in both says one *"shall be imposed"* for a deliberate attempt to injure. **CARHA 80(d)** writes one too, with **CARHA 48(h)** reaching a slash, a high stick and a cross-check alike (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only). **The IIHF writes no match penalty for anything**, so the major plus the automatic game misconduct is its ceiling *inside the game*. Above that, 61.6 allows Supplementary Discipline, 20.7 requires every major to be reported to the Proper Authorities, and 28.1 lets them act *"whether or not such offense has been penalized by the Referee(s)"*. What that costs in your league is your league's discipline to set.

**Under Hockey Canada, cheap stick minors also add up.** Rule 4.8(b)(i) assesses a **Game Ejection** *"[w]hen a player is assessed three Minor penalties for 'stick infractions' in the same game"* — Rules 9.1 to 9.5: butt-ending, cross-checking, slashing, spearing, and high-sticking, which is 9.5 and Junior and Senior only. So a slashing minor counts and a hooking minor does not. Below Junior a high stick is head contact under Rule 7.6, which runs a separate count of three under 4.8(b)(ii). A double minor counts as one mark (Interpretation 2 to Rule 4.8(b)). A Game Ejection ends that game only — *"A Game Ejection is not equal to a Game Misconduct penalty and a player receiving a Game Ejection penalty does not trigger any automatic suspension"* (Rule 4.8, Note 1) — though your league's own discipline may still act on it.

**The NHL, IIHF and USA Hockey books carry no stick-specific count, but every book escalates within a game on a counter of some kind.** [Rules Primer](../foundation/rules_primer.md) sets them out. **Find your own book's count before you take a third cheap stick minor.**

### The sweep check

```facts
Technique: A low, wide arc — blade flat, shaft nearly parallel to the ice, sweeping through the puck or the passing lane
Read: Use it when beaten to the outside, defending a pass across the slot, or covering the pass on a 2-on-1
Technique: Bottom hand slides down the shaft, get low, sweep through the ice — not through the air
Never: Sweep at knee or waist height — that is a hook or a slash
Never: Lean your body after the sweep. Sweep with your stick instead and keep your feet under you
```

**What it is:** A low, wide arc of your stick across the ice — blade flat, shaft nearly parallel to the ice — sweeping through the puck or the passing lane.

**When it works:** When you are beaten to the outside and reaching across, when you are defending a pass across the slot, or when you are down low and want to take away a lane without committing your body. Defencemen use it on a 2-on-1 to cover the pass while the goalie takes the shooter.

**How to do it:** Bottom hand slides down the shaft, get low, sweep through the ice — not through the air. The blade must stay on the ice; a sweep at knee or waist height is a hook or a slash.

**The mistake:** Sweeping with your body leaning after it. Sweep with your stick and keep your feet under you.

### The press-down (stick press)

```facts
Technique: Lay your blade or shaft on top of their blade, pinning it to the ice so they cannot shoot, pass or move the puck
Read: Use it in tight — net front, board battles, faceoff scrambles — when you want a teammate to arrive
Key: The lift takes their blade up so you can take the puck; the press takes it down so they cannot use it
Never: Press with force, chop down repeatedly, or press on their hands — a press is a hold-and-pin, not a chop
```

**What it is:** You lay your blade or shaft down on top of their stick blade, pinning it to the ice so they cannot shoot, pass, or move the puck.

**When it works:** In tight — net front, board battles, faceoff scrambles, and any time the puck is sitting between you and an opponent and you want a teammate to arrive. It is the opposite tool to the lift: the lift takes their blade *up* so you can take the puck; the press takes their blade *down* so they cannot use it.

**Where it becomes a penalty:** Pressing down with force, repeatedly chopping down, or pressing down on their hands. A press is a hold-and-pin, not a chop.

> **All four of these work in non-contact leagues.** If you play in a beer league and want to become a genuinely better defensive player this season, learn the stick lift.

---

## 4. Body Position as Defence

**In short:** Get in front first and hold the ice you stand on, stick down, free hand off them, and never lean.

You can defend an entire shift without ever making a deliberate hit. **Get in front first, make their route to the puck longer, and hold the ice you are standing on — stick down, free hand off them.** In most books the rule is on your side, and the fullest passage is worth reading whole. NHL Rule 56.1 (Interference), under "Body Position":

> "A player is allowed the ice he is standing on (body position) and is not required to move in order to let an opponent proceed. A player may 'block' the path of an opponent provided he is in front of his opponent and moving in the same direction. ... A player is always entitled to use his body position to lengthen an opponent's path to the puck, provided his stick is not utilized (to make himself 'bigger'...); his free hand is not used and he does not take advantage of his body position to deliver an otherwise illegal check."

USA Hockey codifies the same idea for non-checking play. Its legal competitive contact: a skater is entitled to the ice they occupy while keeping skating speed and body position between an opponent and a loose puck; may stand their ground and need not move; may block an opponent when in front and moving the same way; and may use body position, holding their skating lane, to force an opponent onto a longer route to the puck.

**Four of the six books write that grant in full** — the NHL and the IIHF at Rule 56.1, the PWHL at 57.1, and USA Hockey in the Standard of Play sentences just quoted. **Two do not, so do not carry "the rulebook is on your side" into either.** Hockey Canada writes no body-position entitlement: Rule 8.1's arm strength move *assumes* you have position rather than granting it, ⚠️ **and Rule 8.3's interference minor reaches impeding an opponent** *"who is not in possession of the puck"*, with no body-position carve-out anywhere in that book's restraining-fouls section.

**In a CARHA adult league,** ⚠️ **hold a spot you already took and never lean or body to keep it.** Note 2 to **Rule 66(a)** says defending players *"are entitled to 'stand their ground' or 'shadow' the attacking players"*, but **Rule 49(a)** reaches a player who *"intentionally bodies, pushes, shoves, stands in front of an opponent for the purpose of making contact, and/or does not avert body contact with an opponent"*. That is a minor or, at the referee's discretion, a major, mandatory *"[w]hen injury results from intentional body contact"*. **Any major there rules you off for the rest of the game** (Rule 30(a)). Accidental contact draws no penalty under **49(c)**, except to the head, face or neck (still a minor, 50(a)) or from behind into the boards or net (a match penalty, 53(b)). **CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only; see §1.**

### What that gives you

**In short:** Take away ice, box out and seal the wall lane without leaning or pushing, and regain lost position with your feet only.

