```facts
Action: Take away ice — standing in the place your opponent needs to be is a complete defensive act
Technique: Box out — inside shoulder ahead of theirs, hips between them and the puck, and hold it without leaning on them or pushing
Action: Seal along the wall — get to the lane out first and hold it; the boards close off the other side, and you never press or pin them into it
Rule: A player who has lost position must skate to gain or re-establish it in order to make a check (NHL Rule 56.1)
Never: Use your stick to make yourself wider, your free hand to hold, pull, tug or grab, or the position as cover for a check that would otherwise be illegal — the three provisos of NHL and IIHF 56.1
Rule: The free hand may fend off an opponent or their stick, but may not hold their stick or body — NHL and IIHF Rule 56.1; Hockey Canada 8.1 permits blocking a stick but not holding it
Rule: USA Hockey writes the fend-off permission in its Casebook only, and conditions it — control the puck (Standard of Play Situation 6) or be reaching for it, engaged in a corner or in front of the goal (Situation 7), hold a position you "legally established", and never push off to create space
Rule: In CARHA, 49(a) reaches a player who "stands in front of an opponent for the purpose of making contact" or "does not avert body contact" — a minor, or a major that ejects you (30(a)) and is mandatory on injury; 66(a) Note 2 still lets a defender "stand their ground" (CARHA adult leagues only)
Rule: Hockey Canada writes no body-position grant — 8.1 assumes position while permitting an arm strength move, and 8.3's interference minor reaches impeding an opponent "who is not in possession of the puck", with no body-position carve-out in its restraining-fouls section
```

**Taking away ice.** Standing in the place your opponent needs to be is a complete defensive act. You do not have to touch them. Making a player skate 15 extra feet is often worth more than a hit, because it buys your teammates time to get back.

**Boxing out.** Get your inside shoulder ahead of theirs, put your hips between them and the puck, and hold it — without leaning on them or pushing to keep it. This is the basketball rebound principle and it is exactly how you defend the net front and how you win a loose puck in a crowd.

**Sealing.** Along the wall, put your body between the opponent and the ice they want and hold it; the boards close off the other side. You are not pushing them into the boards — you are occupying the lane out. In IIHF women's hockey the wall is not yours to use. Hold ground you reached first and play the puck, and if she is there first, skate around her. Two players chasing the puck may reasonably push and lean into each other while the puck is all they are after. Push her into the boards or pin her along them and it is an illegal hit under Rule 101.1: a minor, or a major and an automatic game misconduct.

**Skating to re-establish position.** The line you must not cross is in the same rule: a player behind an opponent who does not have the puck "must skate in order to gain or reestablish his proper position in order to make a check." If you have lost position, the only legal way to get it back is with your feet. Reaching with your stick is hooking; reaching with your free hand is holding.

**The three things that turn legal position into a penalty** are the three provisos of NHL and IIHF Rule 56.1: using your stick to make yourself wider; using your free hand to hold, pull, tug or grab; and — the one that gets called at the wall — taking *"advantage of his body position to deliver an otherwise illegal check"*. ⚠️ **A seal you were entitled to hold does not make a late hit, a charge or an arrival from behind legal.** The free hand may "fend off" an opponent or their stick, but "may not be used to hold an opponent's stick or body". Hockey Canada 8.1 likewise permits blocking an opponent's stick with the hands, arms or body but not holding it.

**USA Hockey allows the fend-off too, in its Casebook, with conditions the NHL's sentence does not carry.** ⚠️ **Read every condition before you take a hand off your stick.** You must be **in control of the puck** (Standard of Play Situation 6) or, under Situation 7, **reaching for it** while physically engaged in a corner or in front of the goal. The position must be one you **legally established**, and the arm may hold what you have, never take more. Situation 6 ends *"if they grab the stick, sweater or arm of the opponent, or use the free arm to push off and create space, a penalty should be assessed"*. Situation 5 allows a net-front defender to steer with the stick or forearm only *"as long as the arm(s) are not extended."*

---

## 5. Delivering a Legal Body Check

**In short:** Body check only where your game allows it; checking from behind, head contact, charging and boarding are penalties in every division.

**This section applies only in games that permit body checking.** If you play 12U or below, girls' or women's hockey under USA Hockey or Hockey Canada, U13 and below in Canada, any Canadian division a Member of Hockey Canada has approved as non-check, a non-check adult league or any CARHA league, everything below is a penalty. ⚠️ **So skip to Section 6 — but read the last four sections of this one first:** *Four fouls no division is exempt from*, *Boarding, and the player who cannot see you coming*, *Checking from behind, and what the onus does and does not mean*, and *And what these fouls cost beyond tonight*. Checking from behind, head contact, charging and boarding are penalties in *every* division: Hockey Canada scopes 7.3 and USA Hockey scopes 604, and neither scopes those four. **All four can end your game in at least one book.**

**And the bar is not only a property of your own league:** ⚠️ **under USA Hockey Rule 604(b), a single fixture against a non-checking team is a no-checking game whatever your own classification is.**

**In Britain the answer is not in a rulebook but in your competition's Rules of Competition.** IHUK's put the junior checking line between U12 and U14, so ask your league which side of it your division sits before you read on (see "If you play in Britain" in §1). **Women's hockey in Britain runs IIHF Rule 101.1**, so the restricted standard in §1 is the most you have and this section is not yours. **In the WNIHL, whose Rules of Competition make both levels non-checking, even that body checking is not yours.**

**What it costs.** Where checking is barred, a check is not capped at a minor. USA Hockey 604 runs minor, then major plus game misconduct for a reckless check, and plan on a match for any reckless one (602(a)). Hockey Canada 7.3 runs minor, then major plus game misconduct for a violent check — mandatory if it injures — and a match for one meant to injure. Under CARHA, Rule 49(a) makes a body check a minor or a major, a major where it injures. By Rule 30(a) a major ends your game, and Rule 32(d) adds at least a one-game suspension (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only).

**If CARHA is your book,** ⚠️ **the four foul sections below are priced mostly in the other five books, so read your own ladders here first.** Every act they prohibit is prohibited under CARHA too, but the ladders differ. **Your rules are CARHA 49 (boarding and body contact), 50 (head contact), 52 (charging) and 53 (checking from behind), and 53 is the one to read first.** Its floor is *"[a] Minor penalty plus a Game Misconduct"* for intentionally hitting an opponent from behind anywhere on the ice — **so the lowest rung still ends your game**. A major plus a game misconduct is compulsory *"[i]f a player is injured"* (53(a)). **53(b)** is a match where a player driven from behind into the boards or goal net could not protect themselves, and its Note tells referees *"not to substitute other penalties when a player is checked from behind in any manner."*

The rest of the CARHA ladder: **52(a)** prices charging at a minor or a major, which ejects you, and says *"If more than two steps or strides are taken, it shall be considered a charge"*. **52(b)** makes a major plus a game misconduct compulsory for charging a goalie in the crease, or for any charge that injures. **50** covers head contact by anything but a stick — a minor if accidental (50(a)), a double minor or a major plus a game misconduct if intentional (50(b)), and a match for a deliberate injury or attempt (50(c)). A stick goes to Rule 62, which §3 covers. **49(b)** turns any infraction that sends an opponent into the boards into a double minor — ⚠️ **and that double minor counts as two penalties toward the three that eject you under Rule 32(a)**, so a boarding call plus one more minor ends your game. **CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only; see §1.**

### A body check is the last step of a progression, not the first thing you learn

```facts
Never: Deliver anything in this section in a league that does not permit checking — everything below is a penalty there
Rule: A body check under USA Hockey 604(c) at 12U and below, in all girls'/women's and all non-check adult classifications is not capped at a minor — 604(d) is a major plus a game misconduct where it recklessly endangers — plan on a match too (602(a), 604(e))
Rule: Hockey Canada prices a body check in divisions of U13 and below, in female hockey and in any other division a Member has approved — 7.3(a) is a minor, 7.3(b) is a major plus a game misconduct on the degree of violence with nobody hurt and mandatory on injury, and 7.3(c) is a match penalty
Rule: Body checking is prohibited at 12U and below, in all girls'/women's classifications and in all non-check adult classifications (USA Hockey Rule 604(a))
Rule: Hockey Canada 7.3(a) bars body checking at U13 and below, throughout female hockey, and in any other division a Member has approved — so being above the U13 line does not settle it
Rule: In Britain the body-checking age is not in either rule book but in your competition's Rules of Competition — IHUK's put the junior checking line between U12 and U14. Ask your league; women's hockey runs IIHF Rule 101.1, and the WNIHL bars checking at both levels
Key: League legality is a floor, not a readiness test
Technique: The progression is skating, angling, stick checks, contact confidence, body blocking, take out/rub out, solid body checks
Priority: If you cannot angle, lift a stick cleanly or stay balanced through contact, the missing skill is the step you skipped
Key: Body play should begin to be taught at 8-and-Under, years before checking is legal
Priority: A body check is the fourth line of defence after angling, stick checks and body blocks — not the first tool to reach for
```

League legality is a floor, not a readiness test. USA Hockey's *Introduction To Body Contact* sets out body play as an ordered progression, and a solid body check is step seven of seven:

1. **Skating**
2. **Angling**
3. **Stick checks**
4. **Contact confidence** — *"being comfortable with incidental body contact – contact that occurs when competing for a puck"*
5. **Body blocking**
6. **Take out / rub out checks**
7. **Solid body checks**

The manual's own justification for the order: *"It is important that coaches teach this progression of body play skills. This will allow players to enjoy the game more and will also make it a safer sport."* It also notes that body play skills should **begin** to be taught at 8-and-Under, years before checking is legal, precisely because the earlier steps are what make the last one safe.

Two things follow. **For a player:** if you cannot yet angle a puck carrier off the puck, lift a stick cleanly, or stay balanced through incidental contact, the missing skill is not the check — it is the step you skipped, and it is the reason the check will go wrong. Sections 2, 3 and 4 of this document are steps 1–5. **For a coach:** the first four levels of body play, in USA Hockey's own ordering, are angling, stick checks, body blocks and body checks — a body check is the fourth line of defence, not the first one to reach for.

### What a legal check is, precisely

**In short:** Where checking is allowed, check only the puck carrier, front or side, with hips and shoulders, to win the puck, never to punish.

```facts
Rule: Not legal where checking is barred — USA Hockey 604(a) at 12U and below, all girls'/women's and non-check adult; Hockey Canada 7.3 at U13 and below, in female hockey, and in any other division a Member of Hockey Canada has approved
Rule: IIHF women's play allows a check only with clear intent to play or gain the puck, never using the boards to push, pin or eliminate an opponent; a step or glide into one is at least a minor (Rule 101.1). Britain's age and the WNIHL's checking ban are in IHUK's Rules of Competition, not a rulebook
Rule: A body check is not legal in a CARHA-affiliated adult league either — that book has no checking division, 49(a) makes one a minor or a major anywhere on the ice and a major where it injures; a major costs you the rest of the game (30(a)) and at least the next one (32(d))
Goal: To gain possession of the puck — NOT to punish or intimidate an opponent
Targets: Where the game you are in permits a check at all — an opponent in control of the puck, hit from the front, diagonally from the front, or straight from the side
Technique: Trunk only — hips and shoulders. Hands, forearm, stick and elbow are all illegal
Position: Where the game you are in permits a check at all — contact above their knees and at or below their shoulders; under Hockey Canada, above their hips, because 8.7 makes contact below them clipping
Technique: Start with your stick on the puck — the blade of the player delivering the check must be below the knees (USA Hockey)
Rule: Where checking is barred, an illegal body check is intentional contact using overt hip, shoulder, forearm or torso action — USA Hockey Rule 604 Note 1, scoped by its own words to "the purpose of this rule" and by 604(a) to the Competitive Contact classifications
Key: If your stick is up, you are about to commit a cross-check, a high stick or a head-contact penalty
```

USA Hockey's **Glossary** entry for *Body Checking* is the most explicit definition in the sport, so use it as your checklist — it is the Glossary that carries it, not Rule 604. Its mirror image is **USA Hockey Rule 604, Note 1**, which defines the illegal version. Mind the scope: 604 is titled *"Body Checking (Competitive Contact Categories)"* and its Note opens *"For the purpose of this rule"*, so it governs the classifications where checking is **barred**. **In a checking league the hip and shoulder action it names is the *legal* check — the forearm never is, in any classification** (the Glossary and the Declaration bar it book-wide). Where checking is barred, it reads: *"an illegal body check is defined as when a player makes intentional physical contact with the opponent using overt hip, shoulder, forearm or torso action. This includes physically forcing the opponent off the puck and with no effort to legally play the puck."* Everything below is what keeps you on the legal side of that line:

- **Purpose:** to gain possession of the puck — "NOT to punish or intimidate an opponent."
- **Target:** an opponent **in control of the puck**.
- **Direction:** from the front, diagonally from the front, or straight from the side.
- **Your contact surface:** the **trunk only** — hips and shoulders. Hands, forearm, stick and elbow are all illegal.
- **Their contact area:** **above the knees and at or below the shoulders.**
- **Your stick:** "Proper body checking technique starts with stick on puck, therefore the stick blade of the player delivering the check must be below the knees."

**The IIHF book defines the same check in its own glossary**, and if you play in Britain this is the definition you are actually being officiated against: *"A legal body check is one in which a Player checks an opponent who is in possession of the puck, by using their hip or body from the front, diagonally from the front or straight from the side. Legitimate body checking must be done for the purpose of separating the opponent from the puck, only with the trunk of the body (hips and shoulders) and must be above the opponent's knees and at or below the shoulders."* Note what it does **not** contain: **no stick-below-the-knees clause.** That one is USA Hockey's coaching standard, and it is worth adopting anyway — but it is not a rule you are held to under the IIHF book.

**Play to that envelope whatever your book says, because of the NHL, IIHF, USA Hockey and Hockey Canada books it is the most demanding** — a check that satisfies it does not offend the other three (a comparison drawn here, not a rule any book states). ⚠️ **But it is not the end of the test.** It does not carry over to the PWHL or CARHA, which restrict contact further. Clipping and kneeing also sit beside it in every book, scoped to no division: under Hockey Canada 7.8 a knee contact is kneeing *"on an otherwise legal hit"* ([Delivering a Legal Body Check: Technique](#delivering-a-legal-body-check-technique)).

**Two of those four books never define a legal check at all.** The **NHL rulebook contains no positive definition of a body check**. **Hockey Canada** defines body checking by prohibition — deliberate contact *"with opposite-directional force"*, leaving your skating lane, or extending an arm, shoulder or hip after angling *"to maximize physical contact"* — in its Glossary, with no division scope. The same wording in **Rule 7.3** applies only where checking is banned.

**The check starts with your stick on the puck — that is what separates a coached player from an amateur.** If your stick is up, you are about to commit a cross-check, a high stick, or a head-contact penalty. If your stick is down on the puck, your hands are down, your elbows are down, and your shoulder arrives where it should.

### Delivering a Legal Body Check: Technique

**In short:** Where checking is allowed, hit the chest square, elbows and hands down, and skate through so you finish over the puck.

```facts
Rule: Not legal where checking is barred — USA Hockey 604(a) at 12U and below, all girls'/women's and non-check adult; Hockey Canada 7.3 at U13 and below, in female hockey, and in any other division a Member of Hockey Canada has approved
Rule: IIHF women's play allows a check only with clear intent to play or gain the puck, never using the boards to push, pin or eliminate an opponent; a step or glide into one is at least a minor (Rule 101.1). Britain's age and the WNIHL's checking ban are in IHUK's Rules of Competition, not a rulebook
Rule: A body check is not legal in a CARHA-affiliated adult league either — that book has no checking division, 49(a) makes one a minor or a major anywhere on the ice and a major where it injures; a major costs you the rest of the game (30(a)) and at least the next one (32(d))
Technique: Where you may check at all, play the body, not the head — the point of contact is the chest or upper body, square
Never: Extend the body upward or outward to reach — NHL Rule 48.1 counts that as evidence head contact was avoidable
Technique: Elbows down, hands down — an extended elbow is elbowing (NHL Rule 45.1) whether or not it injures
Rule: Getting low means your own base, not where you hit — lowering your body to check at or below their knees is clipping (NHL, IIHF and PWHL 44.1 word for word, USA Hockey 639, Hockey Canada 8.7, drawn at the hips), and NHL 44.3 makes the major mandatory on injury, 44.5 the game misconduct with it
Rule: Going low at a turned back is checking from behind, not clipping, under IIHF 43.1 ("in any manner"; no minor at 43.2) and CARHA 53, whose Note bars any other call; USA Hockey 608: no bare minor, ejects one head first, its Casebook one forceful at the wall, and if reckless, plan on a match (602(a))
Rule: Kneeing: leading with or extending a knee; Hockey Canada 7.8 adds an opponent's knee "on an otherwise legal hit", from a double minor; USA Hockey 628 a minor plus misconduct, a mandatory major and game misconduct if reckless — plan on a match (602(a)); NHL, IIHF 50 start at a minor; any major ejects
Action: Where you may check at all, hit through rather than at — skate through the contact with your feet moving so you finish standing over the puck
Rule: Charging is judged on distance travelled (NHL Rule 42.1); IIHF Rule 42.1 penalises building speed by "multiple strides", USA Hockey and CARHA 52 more than two (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only), Hockey Canada two or more
Rule: Never leave your feet to check — jumping is charging in its own right, whatever the stride count (NHL Rule 42.1, a player who "skates, jumps into or charges an opponent in any manner"; IIHF Rule 42.1, one who "jumps to check an opponent")
Technique: For an open-ice check, where the game you are in permits one, the opponent must be one stride away — drive the shoulder into the chest, do not lunge forward
Never: Hit a player who is turned toward the boards
```

**Play the body, not the head.** The point of contact is the chest or the upper body, square. Do not extend upward or outward to reach — NHL Rule 48.1 lists "unnecessary extension of the body upward or outward" as evidence that head contact was avoidable, and IIHF Rule 48.1 is blunter still: "There is no clean check to the head or neck."

**Elbows down, hands down.** An extended elbow is elbowing (NHL Rule 45.1) regardless of whether it causes injury. Hands and forearms into a check are roughing or head contact.

**And down has a floor: getting low is about your own base, not where you hit them.** The legal contact area is *above the knees and at or below the shoulders*. ⚠️ **Drop your body to reach an opponent at or below the knees and it is clipping — under Hockey Canada, anywhere below the hips — in every division.** [The wide low base](#the-wide-low-base) in §7 and holding the net front *"low and wide"* in [§9](#defending-the-net-front) are about your own hips and knees, never the height of the contact.

**Never take the low route at a player whose back is turned.** From behind, the books that address it price the hit as checking from behind rather than clipping, and that rule starts higher.

**Keep your knee out of the hit, and keep off theirs.** Leading with your knee is kneeing in every book, and under Hockey Canada a check that satisfies everything above is still kneeing if it makes contact with your opponent's knee (7.8).

**Hit through, not at.** Skate through the contact with your legs, keeping your feet moving. Stopping at the point of contact and swinging your upper body is how you end up off balance and reaching. Driving *through* on your skates keeps you upright and — the actual point — leaves you standing over the puck when they are not.

**Time it, don't chase it.** Charging is judged on distance travelled and speed built up: USA Hockey and CARHA call more than two strides a charge, Hockey Canada two or more, and the IIHF leaves "multiple" to the referee. **If you are winding up from across the ice, you have already committed the penalty.**

**And never leave your feet.** Leaving your feet to check is charging in its own right, however short your approach: the NHL penalises "a player who **skates, jumps into** or charges an opponent in any manner" (Rule 42.1), and the IIHF's definition begins with a player who *"either **jumps to check an opponent**"* (Rule 42.1).

**For an open-ice check, the working constraint is tighter than either rule.** *Introduction To Body Contact* calls the open-ice shoulder check a *"[h]igh risk check, possibility of only partial or no body contact with the puck carrier"* and sets the condition for attempting one at all: *"Opponent must be close enough so that only one stride forward is required to make contact (gap control)"*. One stride, not two. If you need more than one stride to get there, you do not have the gap — close it with your feet first, or angle instead. The same entry adds: *"[d]rive shoulder into opponent's chest, do not lunge forward"*.

**Never hit a player who is turned toward the boards.** This is not a technique point, it is the line between hockey and a spinal injury. See below.

**What it costs.**

- **Charging.** NHL Rule 42.1: "Charging shall mean the actions of a player who, as a result of distance traveled, shall violently check an opponent in any manner." USA Hockey's **Standard of Play and Rule Emphasis – Body Checking**: "A player cannot take more than two strides or travel an excessive distance to accelerate through a body check for the purpose of punishing the opponent", and **Rule 607** carries the same test. **CARHA Rule 52's Note** sets the same threshold — *"[i]f more than two steps or strides are taken, it shall be considered a charge"* (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only). **Hockey Canada Rule 7.4** counts a player who *"builds up speed by taking two or more strides immediately prior to making contact"* or *"[t]ravels an excessive distance with the sole purpose of delivering a hit"*. 🇬🇧 **IIHF Rule 42.1**, the British test, has a stride element without a number: *"the actions of a Player who either jumps to check an opponent, builds up speed by taking multiple strides immediately prior to making contact and / or travels an excessive distance with the sole purpose of delivering such a hit and / or violently checks an opponent in any manner."*

- **Clipping.** **IIHF Rule 44.1** (the NHL's the same in the singular): *"A Player may not deliver a check in a 'clipping' manner, nor lower their own body position to deliver a check on or below an opponent's knees."* The IIHF's Situation Handbook (Situation 44.1, both editions) has a defender skating backwards who *"lowers the body in an attempt to make a hip check"* so low that contact is at the knees, and answers: *"No. This would result in a penalty for clipping."* **So the low hip check is the illustration the rule is written against.** **USA Hockey Rule 639** requires deliberateness: *"the act of deliberately leaving the feet or lowering the body for the purpose of making contact with the opponent at or below the knees."* **Hockey Canada Rule 8.7** draws the line at the hips — a player who *"uses their body to make contact below an opponent's hips"* — and its Interpretation 1 puts *"the onus … on the player lowering themselves to ensure that they do not commit a clipping infraction, by contacting their opponent at or below the hips."*

- **A low hit from behind.** **IIHF Rule 43.1** reaches a hit from behind *"in any manner (i.e., high-sticking, cross-checking, etc.)"*, and 43.2 leaves *"no option to award a minor penalty"* — 🇬🇧 ⚠️ **so under the British book a low hit from behind cannot be written down to clipping's minor.** **CARHA**, which has no clipping rule, tells referees *"not to substitute other penalties when a player is checked from behind in any manner"* (Note to Rule 53), and 53(a)'s floor is a minor plus a game misconduct.

  **USA Hockey 608** runs from a minor plus a misconduct up to a mandatory major plus a game misconduct where the hit is reckless or sends them head first into the boards or goal frame (608(b)). Its Casebook ⚠️ **requires that ejection for any forceful check on a player standing along the boards with their back to the middle** (Rule 608, Situation 1). Hockey Canada 7.5(a) reaches a player who *"otherwise hits an opposing player from behind, anywhere on the ice"* — an inference here, since 7.5 never names clipping. The NHL and the PWHL are less clear rather than more lenient: their 43.3 names a player who *"cross-checks, pushes or charges"* from behind, and **their silence is not permission.**

  **Three of the books** ⚠️ **make a match mandatory for it** (the NHL and PWHL add one for an attempt to injure, 43.4). **CARHA 53(b)** (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only) and **Hockey Canada 7.5(c)** make it a mandatory match where a player hit from behind into the boards or goal frame (CARHA: goal net) is *"unable to protect or defend themselves"*. **USA Hockey 608(c)** is a match *"in all instances"* where a player *"recklessly endangers an opponent from behind with excessive force while the opponent is in a vulnerable or defenseless position"*; for any reckless check from behind, plan on a match (602(a)). A match penalty does not end tonight: CARHA **34(a)**, USA Hockey **405(c)** and Hockey Canada **4.10(a)** each bar you from further games (USA Hockey: games and practices) until your case has been dealt with. [Checking from behind](#checking-from-behind-and-what-the-onus-does-and-does-not-mean) sets out the ladder in all six.

- **Kneeing.** **NHL 50.1** makes kneeing *"the act of a player leading with his knee and in some cases extending his leg outwards to make contact with his opponent"*, **IIHF 50.1** is the same sentence in the plural, and **USA Hockey's Rule 628** Note reads *"leading with or extending their knee outwards for the purpose of making contact, or attempting to do so, with the opponent."* ⚠️ **Hockey Canada's kneeing is wider, and catches a knee on an otherwise legal hit:** Rule 7.8 includes *"making contact with an opponent's knee **on an otherwise legal hit**, leading into a hit with their knee, or deliberately extending their leg outwards to make contact with an opponent."*

**Neither clipping nor kneeing is capped at a minor, and for kneeing two books write no bare minor at all.** ⚠️ **Hockey Canada 7.8(a) is a double minor, and 7.8(b) lets the referee give a major plus a game misconduct** *"based on the degree of violence of the impact"* **with nobody hurt**, compulsory where a kneeing infraction that would otherwise be a double minor injures. **7.8(c)** is a match, and 7.8(d) bars a misconduct rung. **USA Hockey 628(a)** is a minor **plus a misconduct**, 628(b) a major plus a game misconduct for reckless endangerment, and 628(c) a match that *"may also be assessed"* — plan on one if it is reckless, since Rule **602(a)** says one *"shall be assessed"*. **The NHL and the IIHF start at a bare minor** (50.2): the NHL runs to a 50.3 major with an automatic game misconduct (50.5) and a 50.4 match, and the IIHF stops at **50.3**, a major plus an automatic game misconduct for reckless endangerment.

**Clipping runs the same shape** — NHL 44.2 minor, ⚠️ **44.3 a major mandatory *"[i]f an injury occurs as a result of this 'clipping' check"***, 44.5 the game misconduct with it, 44.4 a match; IIHF 44.2 and 44.3; Hockey Canada 8.7(a) to (c), with no misconduct rung; USA Hockey 639(a) to (c).

**Both bind your division** — no book scopes clipping or kneeing by age or classification. **Both are on USA Hockey's progressive-suspension list** (Rule 411(a) names Rules 628 and 639), so a third major in a season costs three further games there ([And what these fouls cost beyond tonight](#and-what-these-fouls-cost-beyond-tonight)). **CARHA** writes no clipping rule and puts kneeing with elbowing at **Rule 56(a)** — *"A Minor penalty or, at the discretion of the Referee, a Major penalty and a Game Misconduct penalty shall be assessed any player who fouls an opponent in any manner with their elbow or knee"*, and **56(b)** is a major where it injures. ⚠️ **It defines kneeing as nothing more than the knee, so its wording is wider than the NHL, IIHF, PWHL and USA Hockey wording — no leading or extending is needed**, and by Rule 30(a) any major there ends your game. The **PWHL** carries the NHL's Rules 44 and 50 almost word for word. 🇬🇧 **For a British reader the IIHF text is the whole of it**: the EIHL Casebook and IHUK's competition rules do not mention either foul, and the IIHF Situation Handbook prints *"No situation."* under Rule 50. The EIHL Casebook is revised in-season, so an Elite League player should check the current release.

### The late hit — and the narrow window for finishing a check

**In short:** Where checking is allowed, check the puck carrier now; once the puck has gone, pull up rather than decide to hit.

```facts
Rule: Not legal where checking is barred — USA Hockey 604(a) at 12U and below, all girls'/women's and non-check adult; Hockey Canada 7.3 at U13 and below, in female hockey, and in any other division a Member of Hockey Canada has approved; and in any CARHA adult league, whose 49(a) penalises a body check
Rule: IIHF women's play allows a check only with clear intent to play or gain the puck, never using the boards to push, pin or eliminate an opponent; a step or glide into one is at least a minor (Rule 101.1). Britain's age and the WNIHL's checking ban are in IHUK's Rules of Competition, not a rulebook
Action: Where you may check at all, check the puck carrier and check them now — never decide to deliver a check after the puck has gone; whether one already under way may be finished depends on your book
Rule: The NHL allows a check "rendered immediately following his loss of possession" — immediately, not a beat later; USA Hockey's Standard of Play penalises the same act under ROUGHING and calls it "finishing" the check
Rule: The IIHF writes the NHL's condition and adds a second on top of it — the check must also fall inside an "objectively reasonable window of opportunity" (IIHF 56.1), so a British reader's window is the narrower of the two
Rule: Being inside the IIHF’s late-hit window is not enough — a body check on an opponent who no longer controls the puck and is either unaware of the hit or unprepared for it is automatically reckless, however legal it would be on a non-vulnerable opponent (IIHF glossary, "Vulnerable Position")
Rule: A late hit is not capped at a minor — IIHF 56.1 makes it reckless endangerment and 56.5 a major plus a game misconduct with nobody hurt; Hockey Canada 8.3(b) reaches the same tier on the degree of violence of the impact, and mandatorily where it injures
Rule: USA Hockey 640(b) makes a minor of every avoidable check on a player who has released a shot or pass — but it is written "(except Adult Male Classifications)", and there 640 Note 2 directs a roughing penalty instead, with no classification exception
Rule: Finishing a check — the Casebook's unnumbered rule of thumb — needs four things: prior effort to gain possession, an arm and stick length when the puck went, the check already in delivery, and minimised force taking no advantage of a vulnerable or defenceless opponent. USA Hockey Situation 10
Never: Skate one or two more strides after the puck is gone and then decide to deliver the check — USA Hockey's Casebook rules that out in those words
Rule: USA Hockey 640(g), a major plus a game misconduct, and 640(h), a match penalty, reach only acts under 640(b–f); a late hit reaches them through 640(b), which excepts Adult Male Classifications, so whether they reach a late hit there the book does not say; plan on a match for a reckless one (602(a))
Rule: USA Hockey Rule 602(a) is limited by no classification and tied to no named foul at all — a match penalty for anyone who "recklessly endangers or attempts to injure any opposing player"
```

**Check the puck carrier, and check them now.** A check delivered after the player has passed or shot is a late hit. ⚠️ **Never decide to deliver a check after the puck has gone** — whether one already under way may be finished depends on your book, and where a book allows it at all the window is narrow. If they have not seen you coming, or are not set for it, pull up.

**The window, book by book.** The NHL allows a check "rendered immediately following his loss of possession" — immediately, not a beat later — and the PWHL carries the same condition at its **Rule 57.1**. USA Hockey's **Standard of Play and Rule Emphasis – Body Checking** penalises, under ROUGHING, "delivering a late check to a player who has already relinquished control of the puck by a pass or shot. This is oftentimes referred to as 'finishing' the check." ⚠️ **CARHA writes no window, because it never grants the hit — do not go looking for one there.** Its **Rule 49(a)** prices the body check itself, with no possession, timing or vulnerability limb; the major is mandatory *“[w]hen injury results from intentional body contact”*. **Any major there rules you off for the rest of the game** (Rule 30(a)), and 49(c)’s only relief is where *“accidental contact has taken place”* (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only).

🇬🇧 **The IIHF writes the NHL's condition and adds a second on top of it**, so a British reader's window is the narrower: the check must be *"rendered immediately following them 'loss of control' **and** their opponent still finds themself within an 'objectively reasonable window of opportunity' to deliver such a check"* (56.1). ⚠️ **And inside that window you still have to look at the player.** The IIHF's **Vulnerable Position** is a skater *“no longer in control or possession of the puck”* who is *“either not aware of an impending hit or they are not prepared for the hit”*, which is the late hit’s own fact pattern. And *“A bodycheck to an opponent who is vulnerable is automatically considered reckless even if that bodycheck would be considered legal to a non-vulnerable opponent.”* **If they have not seen you coming, or are not set for it, being inside the window does not save the hit.**

**USA Hockey's window is the narrowest of the five that write one — not the absence of one.** Outside adult men's hockey, 640(b) makes a minor of every *avoidable* check on a player who has released a shot or pass, and the Declaration of Player Safety calls *"finishing the check"* an *"unacceptable action"*. Its Casebook gives a *"general rule of thumb"* for when contact already under way is not late: the checker *"first makes an effort to gain possession of the puck and is an arm's length plus stick length away from the opponent when the puck was dumped"*, the check *"was in the process of being delivered when control of the puck was relinquished"*, and the checker is *"expected to minimize the force used in this instance **and not take advantage of a vulnerable or defenseless opponent**"* (Standard of Play, Situation 10). ⚠️ **You need all four to finish a check**, and lateness is an express test: *"It is not acceptable to skate one or two more strides after the puck is gone and then decide to deliver the check."*

A separate route to legal contact: *"Contact may also be legal provided the defending player maintains their normal skating lane and reasonable foot speed. In this instance, the obligation is on the attacking player to go around the defender"* — but *"if the defender initiates contact by stopping or changing their skating lane to cut off the opponent, an interference penalty would be the correct call."* 640(b) is written *"except Adult Male Classifications"*, but it is not a free hit there: 640 Note 2 directs a roughing penalty for *"an avoidable body check to an opponent … who is no longer in control of the puck"* with no classification exception.

**What it costs.** ⚠️ **A late hit is not capped at a minor.** IIHF 56.1 says a late hit *"constitutes reckless endangerment"* and **56.5 reaches a major plus a game misconduct with nobody hurt**. Hockey Canada 8.3(b) reaches the same tier on *"the degree of violence of the impact"* with nobody hurt, and makes it mandatory where it injures. Under USA Hockey, **640(g)** is a major plus a game misconduct for a player who *"recklessly endangers an opponent as a result of any actions falling under Rule 640(b, c, d, e or f)"*, and **640(h)** a match. Both reach the late hit through (b), so whether 640(g) reaches one in adult men's hockey the book does not say. But **Rule 602(a)**'s match for a player who *"recklessly endangers or attempts to injure any opposing player"* is tied to no named foul, and it carries a likely multi-game suspension. ⚠️ **And a 640(g) major outlasts the game:** Rule 640 is on Rule 411(a)'s aggressive-infractions list, so a third major costs three further games, a fourth five, and a fifth a suspension until a hearing ([And what these fouls cost beyond tonight](#and-what-these-fouls-cost-beyond-tonight)).

### Four fouls no division is exempt from

**In short:** Treat head contact, charging, boarding and checking from behind as things you never do, in every division, non-check included.

```facts
Rule: Boarding (7.2, 603), charging (7.4, 607) and checking from behind (7.5, 608) carry no division clause, and Hockey Canada's 7.6 and 7.7 both open by banning head contact outright — all four bind your division; 7.6(a) penalises it in minor and female hockey, 7.7 in Junior and Senior
Never: Make contact in an opponent's head, face or neck with any part of the body, equipment or stick — the onus is on the checker (USA Hockey Rule 620)
Rule: IIHF 48.1 opens "There is no clean check to the head or neck" and "supersedes all similar actions regarding hits to the head and neck" — an absolute the NHL's 48.1 does not write; both then bar a hit where the head was the main point of contact and it was avoidable
Never: Build speed into a check to punish rather than to win the puck — IIHF "multiple strides", USA Hockey and CARHA 52 more than two (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only), Hockey Canada two or more
Rule: Charging is speed built to punish or a jump into a check; no book caps it at a minor — NHL 42.2 minor to 42.4 match, 42.5 a game misconduct once a major is called for face or head injury; USA Hockey 607(a) has no bare minor, 607(b) a major plus game misconduct if reckless — plan on a match penalty
Rule: Hockey Canada prices charging this way — 7.4(a) is a minor, 7.4(b) is a major plus a game misconduct on the degree of violence with nobody hurt and mandatory on injury, and 7.4(c) a mandatory match penalty for a charge on a player unable to defend themselves or a deliberate attempt to injure
Rule: CARHA ejects you for any charging major — 52(a) is a minor or, at the referee's discretion, a major; 30(a) adds a game misconduct to it and 32(d) a one-game ban at least; 52(a) makes that major mandatory on injury, and 52(b) in a goalie's crease (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Rule: A CARHA stride count picks the charging tier there and never whether you may hit at all — that book has no checking division, and 49(a) makes a body check a minor or a major anywhere on the ice, a major where it injures, and a major ejects you by Rule 30(a) (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Rule: The IIHF writes no match penalty, so Britain’s charging ceiling inside the game is a major plus a game misconduct — unlike head contact at 48.3, 42.4 leaves that misconduct to the referee, except on a head or face injury in the EIHL and on any major in IIHF men’s junior and British women’s hockey
Rule: No book caps head contact at a minor: USA Hockey 620: no bare minor, a mandatory major and game misconduct if intentional or careless; if reckless, plan on a match (602(a)); Hockey Canada 7.6, 7.7 up to a match, a must if defenceless; NHL 48.2 minor, 48.5 match; PWHL 48.2 major, 48.5 game misconduct
Rule: USA Hockey 620(c) says a head-contact match "may also be assessed" — plan on one if reckless (602(a)); its Casebook makes it mandatory — a check using excessive force to intimidate or punish that contacts the head "must be assessed as a match penalty" (Rule 620, Situation 4)
Rule: Hockey Canada ejects for head contact with nobody hurt — a cross-check above the shoulders is a mandatory major plus game misconduct in Junior and Senior (7.7(b)), as is high violence; in minor and female hockey a deliberate one is (7.6(b), Interpretation 2), and the referee may give it on violence
Rule: NHL 48.5 and PWHL 48.4 need intent — a match where the player "attempted to or deliberately injured" the opponent, made mandatory by 21.1 in each — and the IIHF, Britain's book, writes no match penalty, so its ceiling inside the game is a major plus an automatic game misconduct (48.3)
Rule: The IIHF moves the sanction off the ice rather than removing it — 42.5 and 48.4 allow Supplementary Discipline above charging and head contact, 20.7 requires the Referee to report all majors, and 28.1 lets the Proper Authorities act "whether or not such offense has been penalized by the Referee(s)"
```

These are not ordinary penalties. Treat them as things you simply do not do: **never touch the head, never build speed into a check to punish, never put a player who cannot protect themselves into the boards, and never hit from behind.**

**All four bind every division, including yours if you play non-check.** Hockey Canada scopes only 7.3, the rule that bars body checking, and USA Hockey only 604. Boarding (7.2, 603), charging (7.4, 607) and checking from behind (7.5, 608) carry no division or classification clause at all. Hockey Canada's two head-contact rules both open by banning it outright: **7.6** *"There is no type of legal contact to the head, face, or neck"* and **7.7**, for Junior and Senior, *"There is no legal contact to the head, face, or neck"*. Each puts it on the players to avoid contact *"at all times"*. They split minor and female hockey from Junior and Senior so each is priced separately, not so that either is left out.

**Checking to the head: play the chest, and if the head is what is in front of you, do not hit.** ⚠️ **The IIHF states the principle outright — IIHF Rule 48.1: *"There is no clean check to the head or neck."*** NHL Rule 48.1 bars a hit where the head "was the main point of contact and such contact to the head was avoidable." USA Hockey Rule 620 reaches any contact to the head, face or neck with any part of the body, equipment or stick, and its Standard of Play puts the onus on the checker *"regardless as to size differential"*.

**Charging: win the puck, not the collision.** Charging is building speed into a check to punish rather than to win the puck, or a running or jumping approach; the stride thresholds are in [Delivering a Legal Body Check: Technique](#delivering-a-legal-body-check-technique). **Under Hockey Canada, two strides is already charging.**

**What it costs.** For head contact, the **NHL** is the lightest: an illegal check to the head is a minor, with no major or game misconduct available (48.3, 48.4), and a match only where the player *"attempted to or deliberately injured"* the opponent (48.5). **USA Hockey 620(a)** writes no bare minor: a minor plus a misconduct, or a major plus a game misconduct. **620(b)** makes the major plus game misconduct mandatory for intentional, careless or reckless contact, and for a reckless one plan on a match (602(a); 620(c) writes one too). ⚠️ **Its Casebook says *must* where the rule says *may*:** *"[a]ny check using excessive force for the purpose of intimidating or punishing the opponent that makes direct contact with the head area of the opponent must be assessed as a match penalty"* (Rule 620, Situation 4), and intentional or reckless head contact must be at least a major plus a game misconduct (Situation 3).

**Hockey Canada splits the rule by division.** In minor and female hockey (7.6(a)) accidental contact is a minor and intentional contact a double minor. In Junior and Senior (7.7) minimal violence may be called as *"a minor penalty for roughing, charging, or another appropriate penalty"*. Moderate violence is a minor and a misconduct, and so, at least, is a blindside, late, leaping or deliberate head hit, or one with a raised stick, elbow or shoulder (Interpretation 1 to 7.7(a)). ⚠️ **Both reach a major plus a game misconduct with nobody hurt:** at the referee's discretion on the violence of the impact under 7.6(b), compulsory where a minor-worthy contact injures; mandatory under 7.7(b) for high violence, or where an illegal hit makes a player strike their head on the glass, ice or boards and injures it.

Both Hockey Canada rules make a cross-check above the shoulders a major plus a game misconduct *"whether or not injury results"* (in 7.6, a deliberate cross-check rather than minimal accidental stick contact; §9 sets that out). Both reach a match at (c). ⚠️ **So under either Hockey Canada rule you can be ejected for head contact with nobody hurt.** In minor and female hockey, three head-contact penalties in one game are a Game Ejection (Rule 4.8(b)(ii)).

**The IIHF** runs from a discretionary minor (48.2) to a major plus an automatic game misconduct (48.3) — **no match penalty, because its book contains none anywhere.** ⚠️ **The PWHL calls it as an ejection from the start:** a major (48.2) with a mandatory game misconduct (48.5). It is reviewed automatically on video and reducible to a minor only on review, where the officials judge it accidental because the opponent's posture made head contact *"on an otherwise full body check unavoidable"* or they changed position just before the hit (48.3). **PWHL only** — a British women's player is under IIHF Rule 48.

**No book caps charging at a minor.** The NHL runs 42.2 minor, 42.3 major, **42.4 a match penalty**, and **42.5** makes the game misconduct mandatory once a major is called for an injury to the face or head. USA Hockey 607(a) writes no bare minor at all, and **607(b)** makes a major plus a game misconduct mandatory — *"shall be assessed"* — for a player who *"recklessly endangers an opponent as a result of charging"*. Plan on a match penalty too: general Rule **602(a)** says one *"shall be assessed"* for reckless endangerment, though **607(e)** says only that one *"may also be assessed"*. Hockey Canada 7.4(a) is a mandatory bare minor, but 7.4(b) lets the referee assess a major plus a game misconduct *"based on the degree of violence of the impact"* **with nobody hurt**, and makes that pair mandatory where a charge that would otherwise be a minor injures the opponent. **7.4(c)** is a match penalty that *"will be assessed"* for a charge *"in such a way that they are unable to defend themselves"*, or a deliberate attempt to injure.

**Under CARHA a charge that hurts somebody always ends your night.** **52(a)** leaves the minor-or-major choice to the referee, though a major there ejects you either way (Rule 30(a)). It adds *"If injury results, a Major penalty and a Game Misconduct penalty shall be assessed"*, and **52(b)** compels the same pair for charging a goalie in the crease or for any charge that injures (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only). 🇬🇧 **The IIHF, Britain's book, writes no match penalty anywhere**, so its ceiling *inside the game* is a major plus a game misconduct, with 42.5 allowing Supplementary Discipline above it. Unlike head contact at 48.3, its Rule 42.4 does not make that game misconduct automatic. In the **EIHL** it is mandatory once the major is for a foul causing an injury to the face or head; [Boarding](#boarding-and-the-player-who-cannot-see-you-coming) sets that out, with the two other populations where any major carries the game misconduct with it.

### Boarding, and the player who cannot see you coming

**In short:** Take the puck and let the player go when they cannot protect themselves, are racing for an icing, or are already battling.

```facts
Rule: NHL Rule 41.1 is boarding — checking or pushing a defenceless opponent so they hit the boards "violently or dangerously" — and the definition names the icing/offside case in terms
Rule: Under USA Hockey boarding is not only a hit — its Rule 603 Note reaches a player who "pushes, trips or body checks" an opponent dangerously into the boards, and its Casebook prefers the boarding call to tripping (Rule 603, Situation 3)
Rule: Boarding is not capped at two minutes — NHL 41.2 minor, 41.3 major, 41.4 match, and 41.5 adds a game misconduct whenever a major follows an injury to the face or head
Rule: USA Hockey writes no bare minor for boarding — 603(a) starts at a minor plus a misconduct, 603(b) makes a reckless board a mandatory major plus a game misconduct — and plan on a match penalty: 602(a) says one "shall be assessed" for reckless endangerment
Rule: Hockey Canada 7.2(b) reaches a major plus a game misconduct on the degree of violence of the impact with nobody hurt, and makes that pair mandatory where a board injures; 7.2(c) is a match penalty
Rule: Under the IIHF — Britain's book — boarding reaches a major plus a game misconduct with nobody hurt (41.4) and no match penalty above it, only 41.5; unlike checking from behind at 43.3, that game misconduct is not automatic — except in the EIHL, in IIHF men's junior and in British women's hockey
Rule: The EIHL supersedes the IIHF here, and its Casebook prints one sentence under boarding, charging and elbowing alike: where a major under that rule is for a foul "resulting in an injury to the face or head of an opponent, a game misconduct shall be imposed"
Never: Hit a player playing an obvious icing or offside puck into the boards — boarding in NHL, IIHF and PWHL 41.1 and Hockey Canada 7.2 alike; USA Hockey reaches only the icing half, at 624(d)
Never: Arrive as the third man and hit someone already engaged in a battle — USA Hockey deems both players defenceless
Rule: Under IIHF, a check on an opponent who no longer has the puck and is either unaware of the hit or unprepared for it is automatically reckless, however clean it would otherwise be (IIHF glossary, "Vulnerable Position")
```

**Never put a player who cannot protect themselves into the boards, never hit a player racing for an icing or offside puck, and never arrive as the third man on two players already in a battle.**

**Boarding.** NHL Rule 41.1: checking or pushing "a defenseless opponent in such a manner that causes the opponent to hit or impact the boards violently or dangerously." ⚠️ **Under USA Hockey, boarding is not only a hit.** Its Rule 603 Note reaches *"the action where a player pushes, trips or body checks an opponent causing them to go dangerously into the boards"*, and its Casebook prefers the boarding call to tripping for a trip into the end boards (Rule 603, Situation 3). With no bare minor in 603(a), the floor there is a minor plus a misconduct. **The NHL also names the icing race:** "Any unnecessary contact with a player playing the puck on an obvious 'icing' or 'off-side' play which results in that player hitting or impacting the boards is 'boarding'." The IIHF, Hockey Canada and PWHL print the same clause. USA Hockey reaches only the icing half, at 624(d), without naming boarding. **So on an icing race, take the puck and let them go.**

**Do not arrive as the third man and hit someone already in a battle — take the puck instead.** USA Hockey's **Declaration of Player Safety, Fair Play & Respect** says that "when two or more players are physically engaged for control of the puck along the boards, they are considered to be vulnerable or defenseless," and its **Rule 640(e)** Note says such players *"are not eligible to be body checked"*, naming no location. Its Glossary's **Physical Engagement** *"includes opposing players competing for the puck in front of the goal or along the boards."* 🇬🇧 The IIHF gets to the same place through its **Vulnerable Position**: *"A skater is considered to be in a vulnerable position when they are no longer in control or possession of the puck and they are either not aware of an impending hit or they are not prepared for the hit. A bodycheck to an opponent who is vulnerable is automatically considered reckless even if that bodycheck would be considered legal to a non-vulnerable opponent."* A player wrestling for a loose puck has neither possession nor any preparation for a third arrival.

**USA Hockey's boarding rule writes one narrow exception, and it is not a technique.** Inside 603(a): *"'Rolling' an opponent along the boards where they are attempting to go through too small an opening is not considered boarding."* It limits the word *boarding* only, for a player who tried to force a gap along the wall that was never there. No counterpart appears in NHL Rule 41, IIHF Rule 41 or Hockey Canada Rule 7.2. ⚠️ **It is not a licence to drive anybody into the wall:** it does nothing to checking from behind (608), head contact (620) or charging (607), nor to 603's own Note. A roll that recklessly endangers an opponent or is meant to injure them is still a match under 602(a). **Nothing about knowing this sentence should change how you play the wall.**

**What it costs.** ⚠️ **Boarding is not capped at two minutes.** The NHL runs minor (41.2), major (41.3) and match (41.4), and **41.5 adds a game misconduct whenever a major follows an injury to the face or head**. USA Hockey 603(a) has no bare minor, and **603(b)** makes a board that recklessly endangers the opponent a mandatory major plus a game misconduct. Hockey Canada 7.2(b) allows a major plus a game misconduct on the violence of the impact **with nobody hurt**, and makes it mandatory where a minor-worthy board injures. Under the IIHF book — the British one — 41.4 reaches a major plus a game misconduct with nobody hurt.

**Five of the six books reach a match.** The NHL and PWHL do at 41.4 for an attempted or deliberate injury, and **Hockey Canada 7.2(c)** for a deliberate injury or attempt. **CARHA** does through 49(b) and its general 48(a) for an attempt to injure (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only). **USA Hockey**, ⚠️ **where you should plan on one for a reckless board**, does because 602(a) says one *"shall be assessed"*, though 603(c) says only *"may also"*. **The IIHF stops at a major plus a game misconduct (41.4), and can give the major alone (41.3)** — unlike its checking-from-behind and head-contact rules at 43.3 and 48.3. The cost can outlive the game — see [And what these fouls cost beyond tonight](#and-what-these-fouls-cost-beyond-tonight).

**Where the misconduct is automatic.** ⚠️ **That IIHF discretion is not everyone's.** IIHF **201.1** makes the game misconduct automatic on any major in its Men's Junior U20 and U18 categories, and the **IHUK In-House Rules' Rule 101** does the same for **women's hockey in Britain**, *"in all cases"*, with no age limb. So in either, a major for boarding or charging ends your game ([If you play in Britain](#if-you-play-in-britain)). ⚠️ **The EIHL takes the discretion away for three fouls at once.** Its Casebook 26-27, under its boarding, charging and elbowing headings: *"When a major penalty is imposed under this rule for a foul resulting in an injury to the face or head of an opponent, a game misconduct shall be imposed"* — *shall*, where IIHF 41.4, 42.4 and 45.4 say *"may"*, and triggered by the injury rather than by reckless endangerment. The Casebook heads elbowing *"RULE 42 – ELBOWING"*, its own numbering (elbowing is Rule 45 in the IIHF books), so quote the words, not that number. **This binds the Elite League only.**

**For hitting an engaged player,** USA Hockey **640(e)** is a minor, ⚠️ **and it is not capped there:** **640(g)** is a major plus a game misconduct where the check *"recklessly endangers an opponent"*, and **640(h)** a match — plan on it, because 602(a) says one *"shall be assessed"* for reckless endangerment. Its Casebook (Rule 632, Situation 1) even has the referee stop play before the hit lands. Where a third player skates at two engaged players *"with the clear intent to deliver a check"*, *"The Referee must stop play the instant they realize that unnecessary contact will result"*; if contact is made, the referee penalises *"the third player who entered the corner and made no effort to legally play the puck"*. Under the IIHF, **recklessness is the trigger for the major-and-game-misconduct tier** in Rules 42.4, 43.3 and 48. In British women's hockey any major brings a game misconduct anyway (In-House Rule 101), and junior hockey is read the same way.

### Checking from behind, and what the onus does and does not mean

**In short:** If you can see their numbers, pull up and play the puck.

```facts
Never: Check from behind — no minor in NHL, IIHF, PWHL 43.2 (majors eject, 43.3, 43.5); none bare in USA Hockey 608(a), Hockey Canada 7.5(a), whose 7.6, 7.7 on the head reach a match, a must if defenceless
Rule: Hockey Canada 7.5–7.7 from behind: head first into boards or frame, minor and game misconduct, major if hurt; on the head, major and game misconduct for a high cross-check, in minor and female if hurt, in Junior and Senior if highly violent or head hurt off glass, ice or boards; match if defenceless
Rule: Checking from behind reaches a mandatory match at USA Hockey 608(c), Hockey Canada 7.5(c) and CARHA 53(b), and at NHL and PWHL 43.4 for intent to injure, which 21.1 in each makes mandatory; the IIHF writes none, but 20.4 rules its major and game misconduct "off the ice for the balance of the game"
Rule: USA Hockey’s Casebook makes it an ejection to check forcefully from behind an opponent "standing along the boards (back toward the middle of the ice)", or to send anyone "head first into the boards or goal frame" — a major plus game misconduct or a match penalty "must be called" (608, Situation 1)
Rule: A reckless USA Hockey check from behind is a major plus a game misconduct or a match "regardless of whether or not board contact is made" (Casebook 608, Situation 1); plan on the match (602(a)) — 608(c) makes it mandatory with excessive force on a defenceless player
Rule: The onus for checking from behind is USA Hockey Rule 608's — "The onus is on the player delivering the check to not hit from behind" — and Hockey Canada's, which puts it on the checker "in all circumstances"; NHL Rule 43 states no onus at all
Rule: "They turned at the last second" is no defence unless the turn was deliberate — a player who intentionally turns to create contact with their back draws the checker no penalty for checking from behind (NHL, IIHF and PWHL Rule 43.1; the IIHF adds that other penalties may still be assessed)
Rule: The turn-into-a-hit waiver is NHL, IIHF and PWHL only (43.1) — USA Hockey and Hockey Canada write none, and Hockey Canada's Interpretation 2 to 7.5(a) penalises the checker anyway: "[t]he responsibility is always on the player delivering the check to avoid making contact with the back"
Rule: An onus written into a boarding rule is not strict liability — NHL Rule 41.1, IIHF Rule 41.1, PWHL Rule 41.1 and Hockey Canada Rule 7.2 all let the referee weigh whether the opponent put themself in a vulnerable position, and whether the check was unavoidable
Rule: USA Hockey's boarding rule is the one that attaches no balance — "The onus is on the player delivering the check to avoid placing a vulnerable or defenseless opponent in danger", with no however-clause (USA Hockey Rule 603 Note; its Casebook weighs force against preparedness at 603 Situation 2)
Never: Rely on the vulnerability-and-unavoidability balance the boarding rules allow — the two checking-from-behind onus books attach no "however", and a spinal injury is not undone by a favourable call
Rule: No CARHA rung leaves you on the ice — an intentional hit from behind is a minor and game misconduct at the floor, a major and game misconduct on injury (53(a)), and a match (53(b)) for any hit from behind into the boards or goal net that leaves them unable to protect themselves; CARHA adult leagues
Rule: From behind, the instrument does not lower the tier — IIHF 43.1, Britain's book, reaches a hit "in any manner (i.e., high-sticking, cross-checking, etc.)" with no minor (43.2), so plan on 43.3's major and game misconduct, head or not
Rule: IIHF 43.1 names a destination as well as an act — "from behind into the boards, the goal frame, or on open ice" — and IIHF 5.1 makes "Player" cover goalies, so a goalie driven into their own net from behind is reached there, where that book writes no minor, not by charging
```

Never finish a check on a turned back, never let one ride up to the head, and play it as though the onus were entirely yours: you cannot know how a referee will weigh the hit, you are the one who chose to arrive, and a spinal injury is not undone by a favourable call. **And never turn your own back on purpose to draw the call** — it is the position that breaks necks.

**Checking from behind.** NHL Rule 43.1 defines it as a check "delivered on a player who is not aware of the impending hit, therefore unable to protect or defend himself," with contact on the back part of the body. **The NHL has no minor for it** (43.2): it is a major, with an automatic game misconduct (43.5). **Nor does the IIHF** — 43.2 says "there is no option to award a minor penalty" — and 43.3 makes it a major plus an automatic game misconduct where the check recklessly endangers the player. Its Glossary counts a check on a skater without the puck who cannot see it coming as reckless, so plan on the major and game misconduct. ⚠️ **Under the IIHF a stick driven high into a player's numbers from behind is checking from behind, with no minor rung, not a Rule 60 high stick:** 43.1 reaches a hit from behind *"in any manner (i.e., high-sticking, cross-checking, etc.)"*. It also names the goal frame, and IIHF Rule 5.1 makes *"Player"* cover *"both skaters and goalkeepers"*. So a goalie driven into their own net from behind falls under Rule 43 rather than charging — a reading of how the book is assembled rather than a sentence it prints.

**USA Hockey Rule 608** runs from a minor plus a misconduct or a major plus a game misconduct (608(a)), to a major plus a game misconduct where an opponent is recklessly endangered or sent head first into the boards or goal frame (608(b)), to a match penalty for reckless endangerment of a vulnerable opponent with excessive force (608(c)). ⚠️ **Its Casebook (Rule 608, Situation 1) says *"[t]he major plus a game misconduct penalty, or match penalty, must be called"* in three cases, and one does not need the boards:** a forceful check on an opponent standing along the boards with their back to the middle of the ice; *"[i]n every instance where a player is thrust head first into the boards or goal frame"*; and *"[i]n every instance where the player recklessly endangers the opponent from a check from behind, regardless of whether or not board contact is made."* **In those three cases the lightest call is an ejection, and for the reckless one plan on a match (602(a)).** With excessive force on a vulnerable or defenceless opponent the match is mandatory (608(c)).

The Casebook's own minor-plus-misconduct examples include a catch-up push in open ice, on an opponent *"skating ahead of them and not near the boards"*. **A forechecker who drives into a beaten carrier’s back at speed should not bank on that price** — the harder and faster the hit on a player who cannot see it coming, the closer it is to reckless endangerment, which is an ejection wall or no wall.

**Hockey Canada has a minor for it, but never on its own.** Rule 7.5(a) assesses "a Minor penalty **and** a Game Misconduct penalty" for a hit from behind anywhere on the ice. 7.5(b) lets the referee give a major plus a game misconduct on *"the degree of violence of the impact"* **with nobody hurt**, and makes that pair mandatory where a hit that "would otherwise call for a Minor penalty" injures. **7.5(c) is a mandatory match penalty** — *"A Match penalty **will** be assessed to any player who pushes, body-checks, cross-checks, or hits an opposing player in any manner from behind into the boards or goal frame, in such a way that the player is unable to protect or defend themselves"* — with no violence or injury threshold.

**The NHL has a match here too, and for intent it is not optional:** 43.4 allows one where the player *"attempted to or deliberately injured his opponent by checking from behind"*, and **Rule 21.1** says a match *"shall be imposed"* for a deliberate injury or an attempt. The PWHL writes both in the same words. **So no book above prices a hit to the back that stays off the head at two minutes: the NHL, the PWHL and Hockey Canada end your game every time, under the IIHF plan on the same, and USA Hockey forcefully at the boards, head first, or whenever it is reckless.**

**If the hit reaches the head, it never gets cheaper in the NHL or the PWHL**: it is priced at the harsher of Rule 43 and Rule 48, so there is still no minor. **Under the IIHF, plan on the major and automatic game misconduct (43.3, 48.3) — never plan on a minor** (43.2 writes none). Hockey Canada's answer is below.

**In adult recreational leagues.** ⚠️ **CARHA has no rung at all that leaves you on the ice for an intentional hit from behind** (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only). **Rule 53(a)** is *"[a] Minor penalty plus a Game Misconduct or a Major penalty plus a Game Misconduct"* on the degree of violence, for an intentional push, check or hit from behind anywhere on the ice. The major is compulsory *"[i]f a player is injured"*, and *"[a] Match penalty could also be assessed under this rule."* **Rule 53(b)** is that match written flat, for a player *"high sticked, cross-checked, body-checked, pushed, hit or propelled in any manner from behind into the boards or goal net, in such a way that the player is unable to protect or defend themselves"*. Its Note tells referees *"not to substitute other penalties when a player is checked from behind in any manner."*

Under CARHA, ⚠️ **a hit you did not mean is not free either.** **Rule 49(a)** penalises a player who *"does not avert body contact with an opponent"*, 53(b) names no intent, and where 53(b) does not reach the hit, **Rule 49(b)** makes a collision with the boards a double minor. A major there also brings at least a one-game suspension (Rule 32(d)), and a match keeps you out until the Chairman has dealt with your case (Rule 34(a)).

**Under Hockey Canada, a hit from behind that reaches the head is head contact (7.6 or 7.7) — and one that drives a player head first into the boards or goal frame is priced at the harsher of 7.5 and head contact.** Rule 7.6 *"supersedes any other rules, with the exception of Rule 7.10 – Fighting, and any foul that results in contact to the head must be penalized as Head Contact"*. But a hit that drives a player head first into the boards or goal frame is priced under Rule 7.5 whether or not it touched the head: at least a minor plus a game misconduct, a major plus a game misconduct if they are hurt, and a match if they could not protect or defend themselves (7.5(a), (b), (c) and (e)).

In minor and female hockey (Rule 7.6), head contact is a minor if accidental and a double minor if intentional, and a third such penalty in one game is a Game Ejection. ⚠️ **It must become a major plus a game misconduct if the player is hurt or it was a deliberate cross-check above the shoulders, and a match penalty if they could not protect or defend themselves or you tried to injure them** — 7.6(b) and (c), and the referee can give the major and game misconduct on the violence of the hit alone. In Junior and Senior (Rule 7.7), minimal violence may be called as *"a minor penalty for roughing, charging, or another appropriate penalty"*, and moderate violence is a minor plus a ten-minute misconduct. High violence, a cross-check to the head, or an illegal hit that drives their head into the glass, ice or boards and injures it is a major plus a game misconduct, with the same match above it (7.7(a) to (c)).

**Who carries the onus.** USA Hockey Rule 608: "The onus is on the player delivering the check to not hit from behind." Hockey Canada: *"In all circumstances the responsibility is on the player delivering the check to avoid"* contact with the back. **The NHL's Rule 43 states no onus at all** — its only onus on a checker is in Rule 41.1, boarding. "They turned at the last second" is no defence unless the turn was deliberate. NHL Rule 43.1 waives the penalty only when a player "intentionally turns his body to create contact with his back". IIHF Rule 43.1 does so for a player who "intentionally turns their back towards an opponent and puts themself in a vulnerable position", adding that "other penalties may still be assessed." **That waiver is the NHL's, the IIHF's and the PWHL's only.** USA Hockey writes none, and **Hockey Canada's Interpretation 2 to Rule 7.5(a)** gives the penalty to the checker even when the player turned, because *"[t]he responsibility is always on the player delivering the check to avoid making contact with the back."*

**Two things the word "onus" does not mean.** It is not strict liability. The NHL's boarding rule, 41.1, puts the onus on the checker to avoid a defenceless opponent, then says: "**However**, in determining whether such contact could have been avoided, the circumstances of the check, including whether the opponent put himself in a vulnerable position immediately prior to or simultaneously with the check or whether the check was unavoidable can be considered. This balance must be considered by the Referees when applying this rule." **IIHF Rule 41.1 — the British reader's — writes it almost word for word**, **Hockey Canada Rule 7.2** writes it with *"may be considered"*, and **PWHL Rule 41.1** writes it a fourth time. ⚠️ **That balance is the referee's to weigh, never yours to rely on.**

**USA Hockey's Rule 603 Note states the onus flat** — *"The onus is on the player delivering the check to avoid placing a vulnerable or defenseless opponent in danger"* — with no balancing sentence. **CARHA writes neither an onus nor a balance**: its Rule 49 turns on *"the intent and the violence of the impact"*. 49(c) gives no penalty for accidental contact, except to the head, face or neck, still a minor (50(a)), or from behind into the boards or net, a match (53(b)). ⚠️ **USA Hockey's Casebook weighs force against preparedness instead.** Rule 603, Situation 2 — scoped to *"USA Hockey youth games"* — says a penalty *"may be assessed when a player was not necessarily hit that hard, but was considered to be in a vulnerable or defenseless position when unnecessarily hit"*, and *"may not be warranted when a player is hit much harder, but their preparedness for the hit causes them to go only mildly into the boards"*. Standard of Play, Situation 19 excuses the harder hit only where the checker *"clearly attempted to gain possession of the puck"*.

**And every one of these balances sits in a boarding rule.** The two books that state an onus for *checking from behind* — USA Hockey 608 and Hockey Canada, "in all circumstances" — attach no "however" to it. **None of it is a licence.** Play it as though the onus were absolute; just do not tell a teammate the rulebook says it is.

### And what these fouls cost beyond tonight

**In short:** Play as though being thrown out costs the next game too, because in every book read here it can.

```facts
Rule: USA Hockey's Standardized Discipline Policy recommends six to ten games for a 602(a) reckless-endangerment match, four to eight for a boarding, charging, checking-from-behind or head-contact match, three to five for a body-checking match (604(e)), and a hearing may change it
Rule: A game misconduct can cost you the next game — USA Hockey 404(b) suspends you for the first one, and a second major in the same game costs two: the two games are 403(b)'s and are additional to any other suspension from the incident, and 404(b) calls them a minimum
Rule: USA Hockey 411(a) adds a three-game suspension on a third major of the season across its aggressive-infractions list — seventeen rules, boarding, charging, checking from behind and head contact among them, ending with 639 and Rule 640 Unnecessary Roughness (Roughing)
Rule: A 640(g) late-hit major is an aggressive infraction and counts on that tally, and USA Hockey 411(a) does not stop at three — a fourth major in the category is a further five-game suspension, a fifth a suspension until a hearing. Fighting is not on that list — 411's Note sends it to Rule 615(f)
Rule: NHL 23.6 suspends a second game misconduct in the Physical Infractions Category inside 41 consecutive regular-season games, and 23.5 does the same for stick fouls
Rule: Hockey Canada 4.8(c) suspends a game misconduct given in the last 10 minutes, in overtime or after the game — a minimum of the next game or seven days
Rule: The IIHF Rule Book writes no automatic next-game suspension for a player — 23.2 gives the Proper Authorities "full power to impose further disciplinary measures" and 28.1 lets them act "at their discretion"
Rule: The IIHF Rule Book is not the IIHF's whole discipline — Rules 23.3 to 23.6 refer its Stick and Physical Infractions categories to a Disciplinary Code; the current IIHF Disciplinary Regulations write no per-offence tariff, and their suspensions reach IIHF Events only
Rule: Hockey Canada also counts 7.6 head contact — in Minor and Female hockey a third Minor or double Minor in one game is a Game Ejection, not a game misconduct and with no automatic ban (4.8(b)(ii), Note 1); any one can instead be a major and game misconduct (7.6(b)) or a match (7.6(c))
Rule: A double Minor counts once towards a Hockey Canada Game Ejection — Interpretation 2 to Rule 4.8(b) reads "a double Minor penalty will be considered one infraction", so an intentional head-contact double Minor is one mark of the three, not two
Rule: CARHA counts a double minor as two where Hockey Canada counts it as one — Rule 32(a) ejects on three penalties in one game, and the Notes to Rule 49(b) boarding, 50(b) head contact and Rule 48 each make their double minor two penalties there (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Key: A game misconduct can always reach past tonight — every book here lets it reach the next game or beyond, CARHA 32(b) and PWHL 23.6 included, and your own league's supplementary discipline sits on top
```

Each book lets a game misconduct reach beyond the game it was given in, on its own trigger — USA Hockey, the NHL and Hockey Canada automatically, the IIHF at the Proper Authorities' discretion — and your own league's supplementary discipline sits on top of all of them.

**USA Hockey.** Under **Rule 404(b)** a player incurring a game misconduct *"shall also be suspended for the team's next game"*. **A game misconduct for a second major in the same game costs two games, and two is a floor:** **Rule 403(b)** suspends the player for the team's *"next two games"*, *"in addition to any other required suspensions incurred during the same incident"*, and 404(b) calls it *"a minimum two-game suspension."* **A match costs more:** USA Hockey's Standardized Discipline Policy recommends six to ten games for a 602(a) match, four to eight for a boarding, charging, checking-from-behind or head-contact match (603(c), 607(e), 608(c), 620(c)), and three to five for a body-checking, kneeing or tripping match (604(e), 628(c), 639(c)). A hearing may reduce, keep or increase it.

**Rule 411(a)** adds a three-game suspension on a player's third major of the season across its seventeen listed aggressive infractions — boarding, charging, checking from behind and head contact among them, ending with **Rule 639** (tripping, clipping, leg checking, slew footing) and **Rule 640 Unnecessary Roughness (Roughing)**, the rule the late hit lives in. A fourth major in the category is a further five-game suspension, and a fifth suspends the player *"until a hearing is conducted by the proper authorities"*. **Fighting is not on that list**: Rule 411's Note sends it to Rule 615(f). **And your majors reach your coach:** under **411(b)** a team taking three majors in one game, fighting majors included (Casebook 411, Situation 3), has its Head Coach suspended for the next game.

**The NHL and the PWHL** count by category: two game misconducts in the **Physical Infractions Category** before playing 41 consecutive regular-season games without one bring an automatic suspension under NHL **Rule 23.6**, and **Rule 23.5** does the same for stick fouls. The PWHL's **Rule 23.6** runs the same counter on a 12-game clock.
