Each further Physical Infractions game misconduct adds a game. ⚠️ **A third category counts majors and never resets:** three game misconducts in the **General Category** bring an automatic suspension in both books (each one's **Rule 23.3**), **and its list includes *"Third major penalty in a game"***.

**Hockey Canada Rule 4.8(c)** attaches to *when*: a game misconduct *"in the last 10 minutes of regular playing time, at any time in overtime, or after the conclusion of the game"* brings an automatic suspension of *"a minimum of the next regular league/play-off game or seven days."* ⚠️ **And Hockey Canada reports every major:** **Rule 4.4(a)** has the player *"ordered to the dressing room for the remainder of the game and will be reported to the appropriate Member or league for further action."* What that Member or league does with it is theirs.

🇬🇧 **The IIHF** writes no automatic trigger. **Rule 23.2** sends a player incurring a game misconduct to the Proper Authorities, *"which shall have full power to impose further disciplinary measures"*, and **Rule 28.1** lets them act *"at their discretion"*, *"whether or not such offense has been penalized by the Referee(s)"*. So a British reader's exposure is discretionary rather than tariffed, which is not the same as smaller. The IIHF's **Disciplinary Regulations** write no per-offence tariff and govern IIHF Events only. **In the Elite League** the Casebook's Rule 28 makes *"The Proper Authorities"* the EIHL Department of Player Safety and EIHL Hockey Operations.

**One Hockey Canada count runs the other way, and head contact is where it bites.** **Rule 4.8(b)(ii)** assesses a **Game Ejection** *"[w]hen a player, in Minor or Female divisions, is assessed three Minor or double Minor penalties for Rule 7.6 – Head Contact in the same game"*, and Rule 7.6(a) says the same from its own end. Junior and Senior head contact is **Rule 7.7**, and neither names it. **A double minor counts once** (Interpretation 2 to Rule 4.8(b)). **And a Game Ejection is not a game misconduct:** *"A Game Ejection is not equal to a Game Misconduct penalty and a player receiving a Game Ejection penalty does not trigger any automatic suspension"* (Rule 4.8, Note 1). So a third head-contact minor ends your night, the rulebook attaches nothing past the final whistle, and your league's own discipline may still act on it.

**CARHA counts the same thing the opposite way, and boarding is the foul that exposes it.** **Rule 32(a)** — **CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only** — rules a player *"assessed THREE PENALTIES in one game"* off for the rest of it, counting *"[a]ny combination of minor, bench minor, or major penalties"*. ⚠️ **There a double minor is two of those three, not one:** the Notes to **Rule 49(b)** (boarding), **50(b)** (intentional head contact) and **Rule 48** (attempt to injure) each count their double minor as two. **So a CARHA boarding call plus one more minor ends your night.** A minor you never serve still counts too: where a goal wipes out a delayed minor, **Rule 39(b)'s Note 1** keeps *"the infraction"* on the three-penalty tally.

**And CARHA does reach past tonight.** **Rule 32(d)** gives a game misconduct *"for receiving a Major penalty as a result of a Rule infraction, other than the Accidental High Stick Rule"* ⚠️ **at least a one-game suspension**: one game automatically, with *"further discipline action"* depending on the severity. **Rule 32(b)** has any game misconduct *"reported to the Chairman for further action."* A match keeps you out of every game until the Chairman has dealt with your case (**Rule 34(a)**).

---

## 6. Receiving a Hit Safely

**Read this section whatever league you play in** — unexpected contact happens in non-check hockey too, and the boards and the goal posts are just as hard.

### Keep your head up. Always — you cannot brace for a hit you cannot see.

**In short:** Head up, know where your opponents are, and when a hit is coming, see it and brace rather than duck.

```facts
Never: Skate with your head down — not "look up sometimes", never; USA Hockey's Rule One is to keep it up "especially when it looks like you're going to take a hit"
Key: You cannot brace for something you cannot see; a body prepared for contact absorbs it
Key: USA Hockey's Heads Up Hockey programme calls keeping your head up "the single most important thing to know and do to prevent head injuries" — a ranking of the habit rather than of any one moment
Rule: Keeping your head up is worth something in the rulebook too — IIHF Rule 48.1 states that where a player skates with their head up and may reasonably be expecting contact, "an opponent does not have the right to hit them to the head or neck"
Rule: The NHL bars an avoidable head hit at its own Rule 48.1 — a hit "where the head was the main point of contact and such contact to the head was avoidable" — and writes no flat head-down carve-out and no head-up sentence, but its Rule 48.1(ii) still weighs a head-down posture against you
Rule: The NHL and the PWHL are not silent on a head-down posture, though neither writes a flat carve-out — NHL Rule 48.1(ii) and PWHL Rule 48.3(i) both weigh whether you assumed "a posture that made head contact on an otherwise full body check unavoidable", and the IIHF lists that same factor too
Rule: Under the IIHF book only — a square check with no upward motion on a puck carrier skating head down toward the checker is not an illegal check to the head, the checker must still avoid your head, and the IIHF's Rule 48.1 attaches no penalty to the head-down puck carrier
Rule: The IIHF's head-down carve-out is a list of conditions and not a general licence — a head-down player hit from the side or the blind side, or hit without the puck, sits outside it entirely, and it reaches only a puck carrier skating toward the checker (IIHF Rule 48.1)
Rule: Hockey Canada gives a checker no Head Contact penalty when the opponent ducks into the hit — Junior and Senior divisions only, and never where the head is low as the result of a legitimate hockey play such as shooting or taking a pass (Interpretation 3 to Rule 7.7(a))
Rule: Below Junior and Senior, Hockey Canada's Interpretation 3 to Rule 7.7(a) — sparing the checker who catches a ducker's head — does not apply: Rule 7.6 governs in minor and female hockey, requiring head contact to be penalised regardless of the circumstances, so the checker is penalised too
Rule: Hockey Canada Rule 8.7 bars crouching to avoid a body check in every division, and the ducker's own penalty is not capped at a minor — 8.7(a) is a minor, 8.7(b) a major plus a game misconduct the referee may give on violence with nobody hurt and must give on injury, and 8.7(c) is a match penalty
Rule: The IIHF prices the ducker too, but only conditionally — its Rule 44.1 (Clipping) bars a player who crouches "down near the boards to avoid being bodychecked and as a result, causes an opponent to tumble over them" — do not duck in any book: a chin tucked to the chest straightens the cervical spine
Action: Learn to carry the puck without staring at it — it is a stickhandling problem, and it is solved off the ice
```

**Keep your head up on the ice, especially when it looks like you're going to take a hit.** That is Rule One of USA Hockey's *Heads Up Hockey* programme, whose guide calls it *"the single most important thing to know and do to prevent head injuries"* — a ranking of the habit, not of any one moment. Its companion instruction is *"Know where your opponents are"*, because players who do *"they'll be less likely to get checked without any warning."* And it names the reflex the rule is written against: *"most people duck when they see a hit coming, but doing so puts them in danger."*

Not "look up sometimes." Never skate with your head down. Two separate reasons, both serious:

1. **You cannot brace for something you cannot see.** A body prepared for contact absorbs it. A body that is surprised does not.
2. **Under the IIHF book, the rules protect you less when your head is down.** IIHF Rule 48.1 says a player who checks an opponent "who is skating with the puck with their head down in the direction of the Player and does not use an upward motion or drive their body up into the opponent, shall not be penalized for an 'illegal check to the head'." ⚠️ **All four parts are conditions** — a square hit, no upward motion, a puck carrier, skating **toward** the checker — so a head-down skater hit from the side or the blind side, or without the puck, is outside it. Even inside it, the checker must not target your head. The half in your favour comes just before it: *"When a Player is skating with their head up, whether they are in possession of the puck and may reasonably be expecting impending contact, an opponent does not have the right to hit them to the head or neck."*

**Other books weigh a head-down posture too, less flatly.** NHL Rule 48.1(ii) asks *"[w]hether the opponent put himself in a vulnerable position by assuming a posture that made head contact on an otherwise full body check unavoidable"*, the PWHL's 48.3(i) the same, and the IIHF lists that factor as well. No such exception was found in USA Hockey's Playing Rules or Hockey Canada's. Hockey Canada's Rule 7.5 tells referees to strictly enforce checking from behind on *"a player along the boards, with their head down looking for the puck or 'digging the puck out,'"*. USA Hockey's and CARHA's answers to the head-down hit are in [Common Mistakes](#common-mistakes).

Learning to carry the puck without staring at it is a stickhandling problem, and it is solved off the ice. See [Puck Handling](puck_handling.md).

### Do not turn your back to an oncoming checker near the boards

**In short:** Never turn your back; arrive on an angle, and if contact is coming, skates parallel to the wall, forearm and hip to it, head up.

```facts
Never: Turn your back to a checker near the boards. Spine exposed, head first into the wall, hit from behind. USA Hockey warns them: "Even a light hit from behind could inflict a severe head or neck injury"
Key: A chin tucked to the chest straightens the cervical spine; the head stops, the body keeps going, and vertebrae fracture
Risk: The spinal injury from a head-down, head-first collision with the boards or a goal post can occur at walking speed — no speed is slow enough to make one safe
Action: Case 1, best of all — arrive on an angle, not perpendicular
Action: Case 2, contact coming — skates parallel to the boards, forearm and upper thigh to the wall, feet apart and knees bent for a low centre of gravity, lean into the impact
Action: Case 3, already facing the wall — turn skates parallel to it before contact if at all possible; only if you cannot, spread the load, stick and gloves up on the glass, arms out as a shock absorber
Never: Take the wall on your shoulder — never the tip, and not the whole shoulder if you can help it. Forearm and hip meet the wall. Never crouch or duck to avoid a check
Rule: Turning into a hit deliberately to draw a call earns nothing — a player who intentionally turns to create contact with their back draws the checker no penalty for checking from behind (NHL, IIHF and PWHL Rule 43.1; the IIHF adds that other penalties may still be assessed)
Rule: The turn-into-a-hit waiver is the NHL's, the IIHF's and the PWHL's (43.1 in all three) — USA Hockey and Hockey Canada write no such clause, and Hockey Canada's Interpretation 2 to Rule 7.5(a) puts this exact case and gives the checking-from-behind penalty to the checker anyway
Key: Head up, chin off your chest — hit the boards or a post with an arm, a leg or anything but your head first
```

**Never turn your back to the checker — never turn to face the wall with them behind you — at any speed and in any league.** Turning away feels like protecting yourself; it is the opposite. It gives the checker your back and your spine, puts your head first into the boards, and **turns the collision into a hit from behind** — the one USA Hockey warns about most plainly: *"Even a light hit from behind could inflict a severe head or neck injury."* The warning is written to cover the light hit, so a slow checker is not the safe case. No source ranks this against the head-down collision below; both prohibitions are absolute.

**Do not turn on purpose to draw the call, either.** NHL Rule 43.1: "When a player intentionally turns his body to create contact with his back, no penalty shall be assessed." The IIHF and the PWHL say the same at their 43.1, the IIHF adding that "other penalties may still be assessed". USA Hockey and Hockey Canada write no such carve-out — Hockey Canada's **Interpretation 2 to Rule 7.5(a)** still gives the penalty to the checker. ⚠️ **None of that is a reason to turn: the turn is the position that breaks necks, and a penalty to the other player does not undo a cervical fracture.**

The standard teaching against it is the **"Heads Up, Don't Duck"** programme, begun in 1995 by Dr Alan Ashare and carried by the Hockey Equipment Certification Council and USA Hockey. Its whole content is one posture: head up, chin off your chest, whatever else is happening.

**Why the chin matters.** USA Hockey's *Heads Up Hockey* programme explains the mechanism: *"when the head is flexed (chin toward the chest), this normal curve is removed, and the cervical spine becomes straight"*, so hitting the boards or a post head-on stops the head while the body keeps moving, *"compressing the spine. This force can produce a shock greater than the neck's discs and muscles can cushion, resulting in a fracture or break of one [or] more vertebrae. And if one breaks, it can cause compression of the spinal cord, resulting in paralysis below the level of the fracture."* And the part players get wrong: **_"A player doesn't have to be going at full speed for this to happen — it can occur at walking speed."_** There is no speed slow enough to make a head-down collision with the wall safe.

USA Hockey also says *"almost all"* on-ice cervical spine injuries came from a slightly flexed head hitting the boards or a post, crediting research by Charles Tator. **Read that quantifier as USA Hockey's summary, not a measured proportion**: the paper it prints is a six-patient case series (*Spinal Injuries Due To Hockey*, Canadian Journal of Neurological Sciences 11:34–41) that does not carry it.

Larger work by the same author points the same way. A national survey found that *"[s]trikes from behind and collisions with the boards were common mechanisms of injury"*, with many burst fractures *"following a blow to the top of the helmet when the neck was slightly flexed"* (Tator and Edmonds, Canadian Medical Association Journal 1984;130:875–880). Of 355 Canadian cases from 1943 to 2011, **64.2% resulted from impact with the boards**, with a check or push from behind *"still the most common cause of injury"* at **36.0%** (Tator, Provvidenza and Cassidy, Clinical Journal of Sport Medicine 2016;26:232–238). Those papers were read in their published abstracts, not their full texts. **None of it changes the instruction: head up, chin off your chest, never duck and never turn your back to the checker.**

**Hockey Canada's Rule 7.5 tells officials to *"strictly enforce"* checking from behind in *"situations such as the following:"*** — an open list, not a count. Two are about the checked player's position: a player who "turns and, as a result, creates contact with the back," and "a player along the boards, with their head down looking for the puck or 'digging the puck out,'" who "is hit from behind and driven head-first into the boards." ⚠️ **The other two need no turn or bend by the player hit at all**: a checker who "swings the elbow and forearm into the opponent's back with the intent of throwing the opponent into the boards," and one who "kicks" or "pulls" an opponent's feet out from behind "and then pushes the player into the boards."

**What to do instead — USA Hockey's material, in three cases.** [Skating → Arriving at the boards](skating.md#arriving-at-the-boards--head-up-skates-parallel-take-it-along-your-side) covers Case 1, because choosing your angle is a skating decision made several strides out; this is the document with all three cases in one place.

**Case 1 — best of all: arrive on an angle, not perpendicular.** *"Skating into the boards at an angle means better puck control and less risk of injury… When players skate into the corners at an angle, the risk of hitting the boards with their helmets first is greatly reduced. A leg or side or an arm will absorb most of the impact"* (USA Hockey, *Introduction To Body Contact*, "Angling In"). Even better: **take the puck off the wall before you get there** — see "Get off the wall" below.

![](diagram:angle-into-the-corner)

The picture above draws Case 1 and only Case 1: the line, and where it is chosen. What happens once you arrive — skates parallel, forearm and hip to the wall, chin off your chest — is a posture rather than a position, and no rink diagram can show it.

**Case 2 — contact is coming and you can still choose how you meet the wall: skates parallel to the boards, and take it along your side.** The manual's teaching points for a player checked while skating parallel to the boards are *"[p]ress your forearm and upper thigh (hip area) against the boards"* and *"[k]eep a low center of gravity with your feet apart and knees bent"*. The angling-in list adds *"[l]ean into the impact"* and *"[a]bsorb the shock over the widest possible part of your body"*. **The manual names the opposite of that stance as a Common Error in its own words** — *"[s]tanding too upright with your feet together (not having a low center of gravity)"* — so a narrow stance is not a smaller version of the right one; it is the error. Your shoulder and hip turn toward the **checker**; your forearm and hip meet the **wall**.

- **Do not take the wall on the point of your shoulder — and along your side, keep the whole shoulder off it if you can.** The manual's flat prohibition is *"Never hit the boards or glass with the tip of your shoulder – it can cause injury"*. Its teaching point for this case is *"[a]void hitting the boards with the shoulder"*, and its checklist for taking a check says *"No shoulder contact on the boards, if possible"*. ⚠️ **Forearm and hip.** One drill in the same manual, *Partner’s Bump Along the Boards*, tells the player being checked to *"turn the far shoulder toward the boards to prevent shoulder injury"* without saying far from what. Far from the boards and far from the checker are opposite shoulders, so this document follows the manual's general checklist instead.

**Case 3 — you are already facing the boards and cannot turn: spread the load across everything you have.** *"If checked while facing the boards, distribute the force of the impact over as wide a surface as possible, including putting your stick and gloves on the glass to cushion the blow"*. The listed common error is *"[n]ot getting your stick and gloves up and spread out on the glass"*. The teaching drill has players *"make contact with the boards and glass with their extended arms, forearms, side of body and legs… but with no head contact at all"*, with the arms out forming a *"three-part 'shock absorber'."* **This is damage limitation, not the position you are aiming for.** USA Hockey's first teaching point for a player checked while facing the boards is *"[i]f at all possible, try to turn skates parallel to the boards before contact is made"*.

**In every case, and non-negotiable:** head up, chin off your chest, and *"[h]it the boards or goal posts with an arm, a leg or anything but your head first"* (Look Up Line, "Heads Up, Don't Duck"). Never turn your back to the checker, never duck. A tucked head is what turns a hit into a neck injury.

**Three pieces of rink-side advice the primary coaching material does not support.**

**"Take the wall on your shoulder."** USA Hockey's own material runs the other way — *"[a]void hitting the boards with the shoulder"*, and *"No shoulder contact on the boards, if possible"* — so take it on the forearm and hip. (Your shoulder does face the *checker*; it is the boards it should not meet.)

**"Get square to the boards."** No coaching material teaches chest-first squaring up as the orientation to aim for. It appears only as the Case 3 fallback for a player who is already facing the wall. The target orientation is **parallel**.

**"Get your hands off the boards — forearms, not hands."** USA Hockey teaches the opposite only for Case 3, where you are already facing the wall: get your **stick and gloves up and spread out on the glass**. For Case 2 its teaching points carry no stick instruction at all. The Hockey Equipment Certification Council's *Heads Up, Don't Duck* page asks a player to *"[i]f possible, use your hands, arms, shoulders or facemask to cushion the impact"*, naming the shoulders where USA Hockey warns off them. So follow USA Hockey's own hedge: ⚠️ **no part of the shoulder leads if you can help it, and the tip never.**

The warning that *"a locked, straight arm into the boards is how wrists and collarbones break"* is widely repeated, but no hockey safety authority was found behind it: treat it as unverified coaching craft. *(The nearest evidence is not about hockey. A laboratory study by Chou and colleagues dropped eleven volunteers onto force plates from 3 and 6 centimetres and found elbow shear force 68% larger with the arm extended, though axial force at wrist, elbow and shoulder did not differ. That is a reason to find the advice plausible, not a source for it.)* USA Hockey's own phrase reconciles it: arms out as a **progressive shock absorber that bends and gives**, rather than one locked strut taking your whole weight through one wrist.

**Do not duck, either, in any book** — the reason is the tucked chin above, not the penalty, but the penalty is real and the books differ. ⚠️ **Under the IIHF the ducker's offence is conditional:** Rule 44.1 says a player "may not crouch down near the boards to avoid being bodychecked **and as a result, causes an opponent to tumble over them**". **Hockey Canada's Rule 8.7 has no condition:** "Players may not crouch down to avoid being bodychecked", anywhere on the ice, whether or not anyone falls over you, and in any division. It is a minor (8.7(a)); a major plus a game misconduct the referee may give on the violence of the impact **with nobody hurt**, mandatory where the duck injures the checker (8.7(b)); and a match for an attempted or deliberate injury (8.7(c)).

**Under Hockey Canada,** ⚠️ **ducking can also cost you the protection.** Interpretation 3 to Rule 7.7(a): *"In Junior and Senior divisions, if a player ducks to avoid a check and, as a result, is hit in the head area with an otherwise legal check, this will not be considered Head Contact and no penalty will be assessed."* It *"does not apply to a player who is in a vulnerable position as the result of a legitimate hockey play (e.g. shooting, making or receiving a pass, etc.)"*, and in minor and female hockey Rule 7.6 penalises head contact *"regardless of the circumstances"*.

**Being hit from behind is a different problem, and what is taught for it is thinner.** For contact you can see coming, USA Hockey's instruction is to **skate through the check** — *"[s]kate through the check, this provides a better chance of maintaining balance and staying in the play"*. When you cannot, its *Heads Up Hockey* guide says *"players should keep their arms up, knees bent and, of course, their heads up"*, with skates parallel, *"arms/legs as shock absorbers"* and *"feet apart, knees bent, low center of gravity"*. No published treatment gives a technique for a hit with genuinely zero warning. Your real defence is awareness — *"[c]heck over your shoulder to see where your opponent is"* — because knowing pressure is coming turns it into Case 1 or Case 2 ([Scanning and Anticipation](../hockey-iq/scanning_and_anticipation.md)). On the other side of the collision, no book here prices a hit to your back at a bare minor, and Hockey Canada prices one that reaches your head as head contact (Section 5).

### Bracing and absorbing

```facts
Position: Wide base, knees bent, weight low — an athletic stance rides contact, a stiff upright one gets knocked over
Action: Skate through the check, which is USA Hockey's own instruction — meet contact with your legs moving rather than slowing or stopping; momentum you generate is momentum that does not go into you
Never: Skate at an oncoming opponent to meet a hit — you add your speed to theirs and neither controls the geometry. Keep moving on your line. In women's hockey it is an illegal hit: IIHF 101.1, PWHL 52.1
Position: Chest and shoulder first, hands and stick in front of your body, not out to the side
Technique: Absorb with your legs — let your knees and hips give rather than taking it all through a locked frame
```

- **Wide base, knees bent, weight low.** A stiff, upright player gets knocked over. A player in an athletic stance rides the contact.
- **Meet it, don't wait for it — but do not go and get it.** Skating into contact with your body prepared and your feet moving costs far less than standing still and being hit. USA Hockey asks players to *"keep their legs moving and concentrate on skating right through the impact"* — on your own line, not theirs. ⚠️ **Never turn into a closing opponent**: it adds your speed to theirs, and at that combined speed neither of you controls the collision. In women's hockey the move itself is an illegal hit: *"[a]ny move by a Player to step or glide into an opposing Player"* is at least a minor under IIHF Rule 101.1, and the PWHL writes the same at Rule 52.1. In any league the charging rules reach building speed into a collision ([each book's stride test](#delivering-a-legal-body-check-technique)).

  You may hold your ground instead: NHL and IIHF Rule 56.1 grant *"the ice they are standing on"*, and USA Hockey only *"as long as they are able to maintain their own foot speed and body position between opponent and puck"*. 101.1 lets women players *"'hold their ground'"* and the PWHL writes it at 57.1; CARHA grants only the standing half and only to defenders, and Hockey Canada writes no such sentence.
- **Chest and shoulder first, hands in front.** Keep your hands and stick in front of your body, not out to the side. This protects you and keeps you able to play the puck the instant contact ends.
- **Absorb with your legs.** Let your knees and hips give a little rather than taking it all through a locked frame.

### Protecting yourself along the wall

```facts
Priority: Arrive first if you possibly can — the player who arrives first controls the geometry of the collision
Position: Get low and get wide — a low centre of gravity along the boards is very hard to move
Technique: Skates parallel to the wall, forearm and hip to it — not the point of your shoulder, not the whole shoulder if you can help it. Head up, chin off your chest, never turn your back to the checker
Never: Stop and admire the puck — standing still, bent over, back to the play is the posture the checking-from-behind rules describe
```

When you go to the wall for a puck with a checker arriving:

- **Arrive first if you possibly can** — the player who arrives first controls the geometry of the collision.
- **Get low and get wide.** A low centre of gravity along the boards is very hard to move.
- **Get your skates parallel to the wall and put your forearm and hip to it — not the point of your shoulder, not the whole shoulder if you can help it.** Head up, chin off your chest, and never turn your back to the checker. (Case 2 above.)
- **Do not stop and admire the puck.** Standing still, bent over, back to the play is the exact posture the checking-from-behind rules describe.

### Get off the wall

```facts
Goal: The best protection is not being pinned. Avoid the collision entirely and keep possession
Options: Chip the puck along the boards, kick it to a teammate, or take it off the wall toward open ice before contact arrives
Action: Know what is around you before the puck arrives
Action: Have a plan for the puck before you get there — a chip, a rim, a reverse, or a spin off the wall with your head up, never turning your back to the checker
Key: Players who arrive at the wall with no plan get pinned; players who arrive with a plan get out
```

The best protection is not being pinned. If you can chip the puck along the boards, kick it to a teammate, or take it off the wall toward open ice before contact arrives, you avoid the collision entirely and keep possession.

Two habits make this possible: **know what is around you before the puck arrives** (see [Scanning and Anticipation](../hockey-iq/scanning_and_anticipation.md)), and **have a plan for the puck before you get there** — a chip, a rim, a reverse, or a spin off the wall, made with your head up and never by turning your back to the checker. Players who arrive at the wall with no plan get pinned. Players who arrive with a plan get out.

### Talk

```facts
Action: Call loud, immediate and specific for a teammate going back with their head down and pressure coming
Convention: "Time!" means they have space; "Man on!" or their name plus "behind you!" means they do not
Key: The warning is what lets a teammate prepare for contact they would otherwise take blind
```

If a teammate is going back for a puck with their head down and pressure coming, the correct call is loud, immediate and specific. "Time!" means you have space. "Man on!" or their name plus "behind you!" means you do not. A player cannot brace for a hit they cannot see, and the call is what gives them the chance to. See [On-Ice Communication](../foundation/on_ice_communication.md).

### If it happens anyway — a suspected neck injury on the ice

**In short:** Never help them up: call for medical help, keep them still except to clear their airway, and leave the helmet on.

```facts
Never: Help the player up — it is the most common and most damaging mistake after a spinal impact
Action: Call for immediate medical help first
Read: Symptoms after a spinal impact — pain at the injury, buzzing or tingling in arms or legs, loss of movement, radiating pain
Action: Ensure adequate airway and breathing, observing the ABCs — Airway, Breathing, Circulation
Action: Reassure the player, keep them warm, tell them not to move and keep them still — head and neck especially — except to keep their airway clear
Never: Take the helmet off — CRT6 writes that one flat, and unlike its instruction about moving the player it carries no exception
Never: Roll, lift or otherwise move the player beyond what is needed to keep their airway clear — unless you are trained to do more
Key: Do not wait for symptoms — CRT6 says assume a possible spinal cord injury in all cases of head injury
```

Everything above is prevention. This is what to do when prevention has failed, and it is short enough to memorise. **The most common and most damaging mistake is helping the player up.**

**Symptoms to look for after a spinal impact**, per USA Hockey: pain in the area of the injury; buzzing or tingling in the arms and/or legs; loss of movement in the arms or legs; radiating pain in the arms or legs.

**If you think a player may have sustained a spinal injury, your immediate role is to get help and keep the player calm and immobile.** After calling for immediate medical help, USA Hockey's instructions are:

- **Ensure adequate airway and breathing**, observing the ABCs — Airway, Breathing, Circulation.
- **Reassure the player.**
- **Do not allow the player to move. Instruct the player not to move and immobilize him or her to prevent motion, especially motion of the head and neck.**
- **Keep the player warm.**

**That list is USA Hockey's and it ends there — it says nothing about the helmet, and the helmet instruction is not theirs.** It is **CRT6's**, from the Concussion Recognition Tool's "Remember" box, and the two instructions in that box are not written the same way. Moving the player is qualified twice: *"Do not attempt to move the athlete (other than required for airway support) unless trained to do so."* ⚠️ **The helmet is written flat, with no exception attached to it at all**: *"Do not remove helmet (if present) or other equipment."*

**So do not roll or lift the player beyond what is needed to keep their airway clear — unless you are trained to do more — and do not take the helmet off at all.**

**Do not wait for these symptoms to appear before applying the rule.** CRT6 is the concussion tool that belongs on your phone, and the same "Remember" box says to *"[a]ssume a possible spinal cord injury in all cases of head injury"*. Neck pain or tenderness is itself a red flag that means an ambulance. CRT6's full red-flag list, and what to do in the hours afterwards, is in [Conditioning and Recovery → Concussion](../off-the-ice/conditioning_and_recovery.md). Head injury and neck injury are the same call at the rink: nobody moves them except to keep their airway clear, and somebody phones.

### And the case where they get up

**In short:** A suspected concussion means off the ice, no return that day, and assessed by a medical professional before playing again, however fine they feel.

```facts
Never: Assume a player is fine because they were not knocked out — loss of consciousness is not required for a concussion and is not typical of one
Action: A suspected concussion means off the ice, no return that day, and a medical professional before they play again
Never: Treat symptoms clearing up as the all-clear — CRT6 says no return to any activity risking head contact, a fall or a collision until assessed medically, "even if the symptoms resolve"
Never: Let the player decide whether they are fit to carry on
Never: Leave them alone for 24 hours (UK guidance; CRT6's floor is three hours). Stay with them: worsening symptoms "should lead to immediate medical attention"
Never: Let them drive home, and never send them home on their own — CRT6 says no driving until a healthcare professional clears them, and that they need to be with a responsible adult
Read: The red flags, the symptoms short of them, and what has to happen in the hours afterwards are in Conditioning and Recovery; Britain's adopted list differs — UK and England Rules carries it
Convention: Where CRT6's ten red flags and Britain's fourteen differ, act on whichever fires first — neither document writes that rule, it is what the two lists leave you with, and it can only ever make you act earlier
```

A player who takes a hit to the head and stands up is the far commoner case, and
the standard is the same everywhere it is written down: **a suspected concussion means off the ice,
no return that day, and a medical professional before they play again.** You do
not have to have been knocked out, and loss of consciousness is not typical of a
concussion. Do not let the player decide: judgement is one of the things a concussion impairs, and
a concussed player will tell you they are fine.

**And feeling better is not the all-clear.** CRT6 prints this in red, and it is the sentence that
answers the player who shakes it off and asks for the next shift: *"Any athlete with a suspected
concussion should be - IMMEDIATELY REMOVED FROM PRACTICE OR PLAY and should NOT RETURN TO ANY
ACTIVITY WITH RISK OF HEAD CONTACT, FALL OR COLLISION, including SPORT ACTIVITY until ASSESSED
MEDICALLY, even if the symptoms resolve."* Note how wide that is — it is not only the rest of the
game, and it is not only hockey.

**Two of CRT6's instructions are about the car park rather than the ice, and in adult hockey they
are the two most likely to be skipped.** CRT6 lists things an athlete with a suspected concussion
**should not** do, and among them: they should **not** *"[b]e sent home by themselves"* — *"[t]hey
need to be with a responsible adult"* — and they should **not** *"[d]rive a motor vehicle until
cleared to do so by a healthcare professional"*. Do not leave them alone for 24 hours (the UK guidance; CRT6's floor is *"at least for the first 3 hours"*). CRT6 adds that *"[w]orsening of symptoms should lead to
immediate medical attention."* So somebody drives them, somebody stays with them, and the decision
about when they drive again belongs to a healthcare professional rather than to them or to you.
These are CRT6's words, not medical advice from this document; the assessment is a professional's.

CRT6's ten red flags — the ones that mean an ambulance — are in
[Conditioning and Recovery → Concussion](../off-the-ice/conditioning_and_recovery.md), with the
symptoms short of them and what has to happen in the hours afterwards.

**But ten is CRT6's count, and CRT6 is not the only red-flag list.** England Ice Hockey has
adopted the **UK Concussion Guidelines for Non-Elite (Grassroots) Sport**, whose red-flag list runs
to **fourteen** and differs from CRT6's in both directions. ⚠️ **So if you play in Britain, learn that
list as well as this one, and where the two differ, act on whichever fires first.** **Neither CRT6 nor
the British guidance writes that rule** — it is what the two lists leave you with when you set them
side by side, and it can only ever make you act earlier, never later. Conditioning and
Recovery sets out where the two part company, and
[UK and England Rules](../foundation/uk_rules.md#what-medical-cover-you-can-expect-at-the-rink-and-what-to-ask-for) carries the British fourteen in full.

---

## 7. Board Battles

A **board battle** is a contest for a puck against the wall, usually between the goal line and the top of the circles. Most possessions in most games start or end with one — coaching emphasis rather than a counted share.

### Arrive first — and arrive with position, not just speed

```facts
Priority: The player who gets there first decides where the battle happens
Position: Body between the opponent and the puck at the moment of arrival, with the wall on the far side
Key: Arriving first with your body in the wrong place is worse than arriving second with it in the right one
```

The player who gets there first decides where the battle happens. But arriving first with your body in the wrong place is worse than arriving second with it in the right one. What you want at the moment of arrival is your **body between the opponent and the puck**, with the wall on the far side.

### The wide low base

```facts
Position: Feet wider than shoulders, knees bent deeply, backside low, chest up
Key: A centre of gravity below your opponent's is very hard to knock off the puck — your base is the variable you control, though a large enough strength gap still wins
Technique: Generate force with your legs — that is where leverage lives
Action: Stay mobile — a low wide stance can shuffle, pivot and step out; a stiff-legged stance can only fall over
Convention: Leverage often beats strength — but not always, and not against a large enough strength gap; coaching consensus, not a measured law
```

This is the whole technique in three words. Feet wider than shoulders, knees bent deeply, backside low, chest up. From that stance:

- You are very hard to knock off the puck, because your centre of gravity is below your opponent's — hard, not impossible, for the reason set out below.
- You can generate force with your legs, which is where leverage lives.
- You can move — a low wide stance can shuffle, pivot and step out. A stiff-legged stance can only fall over.

**Your base often beats their strength.** A stronger player standing upright will frequently lose to a weaker player who is properly down in their base. Not always — a big enough strength gap wins anyway — but your base is the variable you actually control, and the one most players give away for free. That is coaching consensus rather than a measured law: no ice-hockey study was found that quantifies it. The nearest evidence, from rugby scrummaging, is mixed. Green and colleagues report that *"[a] lower body height and wider foot stance are positively associated with the generation of individual scrummaging forces"*, while Wu and colleagues found the lowest of three stances produced the least force (J Strength Cond Res 2007;21(1):251–258). [Puck Handling](puck_handling.md) carries the same claim with the same caveat.

### Using the wall

```facts
Position: Puck between your skates and the wall, your body on the outside of it
Technique: Create a little space between you and the boards and use your body to protect that space
Goal: Halve the directions you can be attacked from — with the wall behind you it is only 180 degrees
```

The boards are a teammate that never gets tired. Blaine Buterbaugh, assistant hockey director at the Robert Morris University Sports Center, puts it well: "Nobody from outside of the rink is coming to steal your puck... if you can create a little bit of space between you and the boards and use your body to protect that space, you're only worried about 180 degrees, essentially, of people that are trying to steal your puck."

So: put the puck between your skates and the wall, put your body on the outside of it, and you have halved the number of directions you can be attacked from.

### Working the puck free with your feet

**In short:** When your stick is tied up, kick a puck you can see out of the pile, to a teammate or your own stick.

```facts
Key: In a real battle your stick is tied up and your hands are busy — your feet are free
Rule: You may kick the puck in all zones (NHL Rule 49.2), but you cannot score with a distinct kicking motion
Rule: A kicked puck that deflects into the net off a body is no goal; off a stick other than the goalie's it is a good goal (NHL 49.2(i) and 49.2(ii), and IIHF 49.2(I) and 49.2(II) word for word)
Rule: A kicked puck deflecting in off a stick can be a good goal only in the NHL, IIHF and PWHL books (49.2) — Hockey Canada 6.10(b): "no goal can be scored as the result of a kicking motion by an attacking player", and USA Hockey's Casebook (627, Situation 4) disallows even the kicker's own stick
Rule: Kick the puck, never a player — NHL 49.3 makes kicking or attempting to kick an opponent a match penalty *whether or not an injury occurs*, and USA Hockey 627(a) makes even a skate push-off a major plus a game misconduct
Rule: No book here makes kicking a player a two-minute mistake, and all six books read here penalise the attempt — match penalties under USA Hockey 627(b), Hockey Canada 7.1(c)(iii), PWHL 49.3 and CARHA 48(c), CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only
Rule: The IIHF writes no match penalty at all, so the ceiling inside the game under Britain's book for kicking or attempting to kick an opponent is a major plus an automatic game misconduct, 49.4 allowing Supplementary Discipline above — and IIHF 49.3 leaves that major to the referee's discretion
Action: Kick it out with the inside of your skate — back to a supporting teammate, along the wall, or out to your own stick
Action: Trap it — skates wide with the puck under you means nobody else can have it either
Never: Swing a skate at a puck you cannot see — kick only at a puck you can see, never blind into a pile of legs
Technique: Practise this deliberately — almost no amateur does, and it wins pucks every game
```

This is the part nobody teaches and everybody needs.

In a real board battle your stick is usually tied up, your hands are busy, and the puck is under a pile of skates. **Your feet are free, and kicking the puck is legal in every zone** (NHL Rule 49.2 permits it; you just cannot score with a distinct kicking motion).

- **Kick it out.** Use the inside of your skate to shovel the puck out of the pile — backward to a supporting teammate, along the wall to open ice, or out to your own stick. This is the highest-percentage way to win a puck that is genuinely stuck.
- **Kick it to your stick.** A short flick of the skate blade to pop the puck onto your own blade is the fastest way to turn a stalemate into a possession.
- **Trap it.** Standing over the puck with your skates wide, with the puck under you, means nobody else can have it either.

**Whether a kicked puck can ever count depends on your book.** Under the NHL, the IIHF and the PWHL, a kicked puck that deflects in off a *body* is no goal and one off a *stick* other than the goalie's is a good goal (49.2(i) and (ii) in each). **Hockey Canada** says the opposite outright — *"Kicking the puck cannot result in a goal, unless a defending player kicks the puck into their own goal"*, including a deflection off a teammate's *"skate, body or stick"* (Rule 6.10(b) and its Interpretation 1). **USA Hockey** disallows a kicked puck that *"deflects off any player, including goalkeeper, prior to entering the goal"* (617(c)(1); Casebook 627, Situation 4). **CARHA** permits kicking but says it *"cannot result in a goal being scored"* (Rule 69), and **Rule 68** makes a kick shot — kicking your own stick blade into the puck — a minor. Under those three the kick is a puck-recovery tool and nothing more.

Practise this deliberately. Almost no amateur does, and it wins pucks every single game.

**Kick the puck, never a player — keep the motion small and at the puck, and if you cannot see the puck, do not swing your blade at where you think it is.** NHL Rule 49.1 defines kicking as using the skate *"to propel the puck **or to contact an opponent**"*, and 49.3 makes kicking or attempting to kick a player a **match penalty** — ejection — *"[w]hether or not an injury occurs"*. ⚠️ **USA Hockey splits two acts:** Rule 627(a)'s major plus game misconduct is for a **push-off**, a skate used *"in a non-kicking motion"* (Note 2). **627(b) is a mandatory match penalty** for a player who *"kicks, attempts to kick or recklessly endangers an opponent by kicking."*

**The other four books get there by different routes.** **The PWHL** writes the NHL's match word for word at its **Rule 49.3**. **Hockey Canada** and **CARHA** reach it through their attempt-to-injure rules: **Hockey Canada 7.1(c)(iii)**, a match for *"Attempting to kick or deliberately kicking an opponent"*, and **CARHA Rule 48(c)** — *"[a] player who attempts to kick or deliberately kicks an opposing player shall be assessed a Match penalty"* (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only). 🇬🇧 **The IIHF writes no match penalty anywhere**, so its **Rule 49.3** is a discretionary major plus an automatic game misconduct, with no lesser tier for kicking an opponent. **There is no book here in which kicking a player is a two-minute mistake.**

### When to just pin it

**In short:** When nobody can win it, pin the puck to the boards and call for help, but never hold it for a whistle.

```facts
Read: Pin when you are outnumbered, tangled with an equally stuck opponent, or a turnover here is a chance against
Action: Pin the puck to the boards with your skate or stick for a second or two, long enough for a teammate to dig it out — never hold it for a whistle
Key: The pin buys time for your second player, not a faceoff — call for help, and when help arrives, move the puck
Rule: Under USA Hockey, holding the puck against the boards for three seconds is a minor whether or not you were pressured (Rule 632(b); Casebook Rule 632, Situation 6)
Rule: Outside USA Hockey a deliberate freeze is a minor — NHL 63.2(i), IIHF 63.2(I), PWHL 65.2(i); Hockey Canada 10.1 and CARHA 74(b) exempt a hold while "being checked by an opponent", but Hockey Canada 6.12(b) still penalises a freeze for a stoppage and CARHA 55(a) makes stalling a bench minor
Rule: A skater deliberately falling on or gathering in the puck is a minor in all six books (NHL 63.2(v), IIHF 63.2(V), PWHL 65.2(v), USA Hockey 614(a), Hockey Canada 10.2(a)(iv), CARHA 58(a)); in your crease a penalty shot, and with your net empty treat it as a goal against — most books award it outright
Never: Pin because a change is due or you are protecting a lead — that is the delaying purpose these rules describe
Rule: IIHF women's rules bar using the boards to push, pin or eliminate an opponent — pin the puck, not the player (IIHF Rule 101.1, and so in British women's hockey)
```

Sometimes you cannot win the battle and neither can they. If you are outnumbered, if you are tangled up with an opponent who is just as stuck as you are, or if a turnover here is a scoring chance against, **stop the puck moving: pin it against the boards with your skate or your stick blade, call for help, and hold it only until a teammate arrives to dig it out.** A puck going nowhere is a far better outcome than a turnover in the slot, and the pin is what gives your second player time to get there. ⚠️ **Never hold it for a whistle, and never fall on it or gather it into your body.**

**Pinning to wait for help also rules out two motives.** "A change is due" and "we are protecting a lead late" are precisely the delaying purpose the delay-of-game rules below penalise. They are reasons to get the puck out — rim it, chip it off the glass, or make the safe play up the wall — not reasons to sit on it and fish for a whistle. See **[Game Management](../systems/game_management.md)** for the legal ways to kill time and change lines.

Pinning is a legitimate tactic and a game-management decision, not a failure. Know the exception: **IIHF women's rules bar using the boards to push an opponent into them, pin her there or eliminate her from the play** (Rule 101.1) — there, you may pin the puck, but not the player. **That exception is live in Britain**, because women's hockey under England Ice Hockey and the SIHA runs Rule 101.1 unamended. [Defensive Zone Coverage](../systems/defensive_zone_coverage.md) carries the same "pin the puck, not the player" distinction and the same limits on holding it.

**Falling on the puck or gathering it in is a minor in every book**, and a penalty shot when the puck is in your own crease (NHL and IIHF 63.6, PWHL 65.6, USA Hockey 614(b), Hockey Canada 4.11(a)(v), CARHA 58(c)). Under Hockey Canada it is also one when you are inside your crease, wherever the puck is (10.2(a)(v)). **With your goalie pulled,** ⚠️ **it is a goal against instead:** four books award it outright (NHL and IIHF 67.5, PWHL 69.5, Hockey Canada 10.2(a)(vi)). USA Hockey (614(b)) awards it where it stopped an obvious and imminent goal, and CARHA writes it both ways (58(c) Note 1 flat, 36(b) conditioned), so treat any cover there as the goal.

**What it costs.** Under USA Hockey, a pin held for a whistle is itself the penalty: Rule 632(b) assesses a minor for delay of game to *"any player(s), including a goalkeeper, who holds or freezes the puck along the boards or goal frame in any manner causing a stoppage of play."* The Casebook applies it to exactly this play: once the puck *"is held against the boards for more than three seconds by one player, the Referee must stop play and assess the penalty for delaying the game. The fact that the player may or may not have been pressured by their opponent has no bearing on the call"* (Rule 632, Situation 6). Two of you sitting on it does not get you a free whistle either: officials *"cannot blow the whistle simply because two opposing players want to obtain a stoppage"* (Rule 632, Situation 4). Where both of you are waiting for one, the minors go to *"the player(s) most responsible for causing the stoppage"* (Rule 610, Situation 1). Move it inside three seconds, warned or not — the Casebook has the referee warn you first only when you are not being checked (Rule 632, Situation 5).

**The other five books stop play and face it off when the puck is frozen between opposing players** (NHL and IIHF 85.2, PWHL 87.2, Hockey Canada 6.12(c), CARHA 75(c)). What they penalise is the freeze done on purpose. **NHL Rule 63.2(i)** assesses a minor *"On any player, including the goalkeeper, who holds, freezes or plays the puck with his stick, skates or body in such a manner as to deliberately cause a stoppage of play"*, and the IIHF's 63.2(I) and the PWHL's 65.2(i) write the same sentence. **Hockey Canada 10.1** and **CARHA 74(b)** add a pressure test — a player who *"deliberately holds the puck against the boards or any part of the goal in any manner, unless they are being checked by an opponent."* **That exemption does not make a freeze for a whistle safe under pressure:** Hockey Canada 6.12(b) penalises a player who *"deliberately puts the puck out of bounds or makes it unplayable in order to gain a stoppage of play"*, with no being-checked exception. CARHA's Rule 55(a) is a bench minor for a team *"deliberately delaying the game in any manner"*, its Note 2 naming *"freezing the puck"*.

### Once you have it, move it

A won battle is only half a play: the puck has to go somewhere before the next opponent arrives. **The common approach is to move it at once to the support player you saw or heard.** In the offensive zone that is usually low to high to the point, behind the net, or to a teammate coming through the slot. In your own zone it is up the wall to your winger, or off the glass and out when nobody is open. The realistic alternative, when the support is not there yet, is to protect it on the wall and cycle it low until it is: puck between your skates and the boards, body on the outside, as in [Using the wall](#using-the-wall). Which your team prefers in each zone is a coaching choice, so ask your bench what they want. What every version shares is keeping your eyes up for the support while you work, so the decision is ready the moment the puck comes free.

---

## 8. Corner Battles

Corner battles are board battles with two differences that change everything.

### They are 2-on-1 by default

**In short:** First man in ties up the puck, not the player; second man stays out of the pile and takes it.

```facts
Key: Two players usually beat one in a corner — the first only has to tie the puck up rather than win it outright. That "usually" is the coaching account, not a counted result
Action: First man in engages the carrier, takes body position and seals — pin the puck, not the player. Keep the puck the thing you are going for
Rule: In women's hockey the puck has to stay the object of the contact — IIHF 101.1, a women's-hockey rule, allows pushing and leaning only while "possession of the puck" remains "the sole object" of the two players
Never: Make the player, not the puck, the object — where checking is barred, USA Hockey 604 Note 1 makes intentional overt hip, shoulder, forearm or torso contact an illegal check, even going for the puck
Rule: The seal has a limit in a checking league too — USA Hockey 622's Note makes it holding to wrap "one or both arms around the opponent along the boards in a manner that pins them", a minor under 622(a), in a rule carrying no category qualifier at all
Rule: Under USA Hockey a pin from behind on the boards is checking from behind — "minimal body contact" on an opponent "in close proximity", with board contact, is a minor plus a misconduct (Casebook 608, Situation 1); forceful, or head first into the boards, it is an ejection (608(b); Situation 1)
Rule: Forcefully checking an opponent standing facing the boards is a major plus a game misconduct or a match under USA Hockey, "in every instance" (Casebook 608, Situation 1); if reckless, plan on the match (602(a)) — 608(c) makes it mandatory with excessive force on a defenceless player
Rule: IIHF 101.1 bars two or more players competing for the puck from using the boards to eliminate, push or pin an opponent — so §7's women's exception is live in the corner too
Action: Second man in stays out of the pile and reads where the puck will squirt — that player recovers it
Never: Dive into the same scrum as your teammate — two tangled players and a third opponent skating away with the puck
```

The corner is the one place on the ice where support is closest and most reliable. **Two players usually beat one in a corner** — not because of strength, but because the first player only has to *tie the puck up* rather than win it outright. That "usually" is the coaching account rather than a counted result. What the first player occupies is the puck; the opponent is not a target in their own right. The second player just picks up the loose puck.

This is why the "second man in" is a defined role rather than an accident:

- **First man in** engages the puck carrier, takes body position, and seals them off it. Their job is to make the puck stop moving, not necessarily to win it — ⚠️ **and what you pin is the puck, not the player.** That is the safe standard everywhere. Hockey Canada writes one narrow exception, in Interpretation 3 to **Rule 7.5(a), checking from behind**: provided contact *"is marginal in nature and does not endanger the safety of the opponent"*, *"[p]inning a player to the boards at low speed and with minimal impact is permitted, even if contact is initiated from behind (except under Rule 7.3 – Body-checking, as applicable)"* — so not at U13 and below, in female hockey, or in any division a Member has approved as non-check, where an intentional bump, shove or push is a minor under 7.3(a).

  **USA Hockey penalises the same contact by name:** minimal body contact from behind that makes board contact *"should be called as a minor and a misconduct as the result of this 'pinch' against the boards from behind"*, and a forceful check on an opponent *"standing along the boards (back toward the middle of the ice)"* ⚠️ **must be a major plus a game misconduct or a match** (Rule 608, Situation 1). Same contact, same wall, opposite answers — so play the puck.
- **Second man in** stays **out of the pile** and reads where the puck will squirt. They are the one who actually recovers it. If they dive into the same scrum, you have two players tangled up and nobody with the puck.

**In women's hockey the first player in seals and goes for the puck; she does not pin the player.** IIHF Rule 101.1 bars players competing for possession from using the boards *"to make contact with an opponent to eliminate her from the play, push her into the boards, or pin her along the boards"* — in IIHF women's hockey and in British women's hockey at every age. An illegal hit there is a minor **or** a major (5') plus an automatic game misconduct, and In-House Rule 101 adds a game misconduct and ejection to any major *"in all cases"*. ⚠️ **And where body checking is barred rather than restricted** — USA Hockey 12U and below, all girls'/women's and all non-check adult classifications (Rule 604(a)); Hockey Canada U13 and below, female hockey and any other division a Member has approved (Rule 7.3) — a pin that becomes a bump, shove or push is a minor under **USA Hockey 604(c)** and **Hockey Canada 7.3(a)**, and neither book caps it there ([§5](#5-delivering-a-legal-body-check)). Seal, and take the puck.

**Under body-checking rules the seal still has a limit: body position seals, and arms wrapped around a player turn it into holding.** Every book reaches the act. USA Hockey's **Rule 622**, which carries no category scope, names *"wrapping one or both arms around the opponent along the boards in a manner that pins them against the boards and prevents them from playing the puck or skating"* — a minor under **622(a)**. **NHL 54.1** reaches *"[a]ny action by a player that restrains or impedes the progress of an opposing player whether or not he is in possession of the puck"*, and **IIHF 54.1** and **PWHL 55.1** say the same; **Hockey Canada 8.1(a)** reaches a player who *"holds an opponent in any manner."* The boards wording is USA Hockey's alone, so do not quote it into another book.

**The arm block.** Hockey Canada 8.1 and IIHF 54.2 allow a player to *"use their arm in a strength move, by blocking their opponent, provided they have body position and are not using their hands in a holding manner, when doing so"*, and the NHL and the PWHL write the same at 54.2 and 55.2. ⚠️ **USA Hockey writes no such permission, so keep your arm out of the block altogether there.** The one place it speaks, a skater may force a longer route to the puck *"so long as they do not use a hand or arm to hold or block the opponent"* — printed in its *Competitive Contact Category* section, and read here as the floor for every USA Hockey game because no wider permission exists above it (this document's reading, not the book's words).

**Under CARHA** ⚠️ **a seal that becomes a wrap can end your game.** **63(a)** gives the referee *"a Major penalty"* for a hold *"with the hands, stick or in any other manner"* with nobody hurt, **63(b)** makes the major mandatory where the hold injures, and **Rule 30(a)** rules a player off for the rest of the game on any major (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only). Play as though the call may not come either way.

The commonest amateur error in the corner is two teammates fighting the same opponent for the same puck while a third opponent skates away with it.

### You have less room and more escape routes to cover

```facts
Read: Boards on two sides help you seal, but give them the option to spin either way — up the wall or out behind the net
Action: Angle the carrier so the escape they have is the one you want them to take — toward the wall and up, away from the net
Never: Leave them an escape back toward the slot
```

The corner has boards on two sides, which helps you seal a player but also gives them the option to spin either way — up the wall or out behind the net. Angle the puck carrier so that the escape they have is the one you want them to take: **toward the wall and up, away from the net, never back toward the slot.**

![](diagram:corner-escape-routes)

The two escapes the corner gives them are drawn above. The one you are taking away is not drawn, because your body is where it would be.

### Whose corner is it?

```facts
Convention: Whose corner it is is a coaching choice, not a law of hockey. Find out which one your team plays
Convention: Under this guide's standard low zone collapse, the low corner belongs to the strong-side defenceman with the centre supporting
Convention: Under a low zone collapse the wingers stay high covering the points and do not come to the corner
Options: Under man-on-man, a winger follows their check into the corner and you get help low — but the point may be left open
Key: On the coaching account a defensive-zone breakdown is two players on different assumptions about who had whom, not an effort problem — the cause, not a ranking; nothing public counts breakdowns
Key: F1/F2/F3 are roles defined by order of arrival, not by position — the first forward to the puck is F1
```

**This is a coaching choice, not a law of hockey.** The default assumed here is a **low zone collapse** (zone-based defensive-zone coverage) with a **2-1-2 forecheck**. Under a low zone collapse, the low corner belongs to the strong-side defenceman with the centre supporting; the wingers stay high covering the points and do **not** come to the corner. Under **man-on-man** coverage, a winger follows their assigned check into the corner and you will get help low — but the point may be left open.

**Know which one your team plays.** When a breakdown happens down here, it is almost never that somebody failed to work hard — on the coaching account it is two players operating on different assumptions about who had whom, both skating hard in opposite directions. Nobody publicly counts defensive-zone breakdowns, so that is an account of the cause, not a ranking. [Defensive Zone Coverage](../systems/defensive_zone_coverage.md) sets it out; see also [Forechecking Systems](../systems/forechecking_systems.md).

**F1 / F2 / F3 are roles defined by order of arrival, not by position** — the first forward to the puck is F1 whoever they are.

---

## 9. Net-Front Battles

**Goals come from here, so both teams send bodies to it: attackers to screen the goalie, tip shots and bury rebounds, defenders to hold their ground beside them and take away their sticks.** Everything in this section is how to do one of those jobs without losing the goal or taking the penalty.

The area on top of the crease is the **densest scoring area on the ice**. Mike Corbett, head coach at the University of Alabama in Huntsville, said of his own team: *"Probably over 70 percent of our goals were scored within two stick-lengths of the net."* **At NHL level that number does not hold.** Computed for this document from the NHL play-by-play feed over 400 games of 2024-25 (2,498 goals with coordinates), and taking two stick-lengths as about **10.5 feet** (NHL Rule 10.1 caps a stick at 63 inches), **26.8%** of goals came from within two stick-lengths of the net, 30.9% within twelve feet and 51.6% within twenty; you have to go out to roughly **thirty feet** — the top of the circles — before you capture 70%. So keep the principle — a quarter of all goals from a patch a couple of stick-lengths wide — and do not repeat "70%" as a statistic. [Shooting](shooting.md) gives the slot-wide figure and its limits.

**Nor is it where most penalties are taken, whatever the coaching commonplace says.** In the same feed only **265 of 2,967** penalties fall inside the net-front box, and the ones that concentrate there are hooking (**2.10×**) and slashing (**1.76×**), not the cross-check ([The cross-check problem](#the-cross-check-problem)).

### Attacking the net front

**In short:** Get to the shot lane in front of the goalie, skates outside the crease, two hands on your stick for tips and rebounds.

```facts
Technique: Set up in the shot lane in front of the goalie, not beside them, off the red crease line, taking away their eyes — two hands on your stick, blade on the ice, to tip, to screen and to bury a rebound
Key: Screening stands you in the shot lane at close range and upright — know when the puck is about to be released, and go there with your head and face protection right rather than nearly right
Read: Track the puck while feeling the defender — eyes on the puck, body on the player
Technique: Know where the near post is before you plant — a defender walks you along a path with the goal frame at one edge and the goalie at the other, and you travel it sideways or backwards
Technique: Take the shove braced — wide base, knees bent, weight low, knees and hips giving rather than a locked frame — but never against the goalie
Never: Meet a goal post head first, and never duck. Head up and chin off your chest at the net front instead, because you can be moved by a defender you are feeling rather than watching
Rule: NHL and IIHF 69.3, PWHL 71.3 — once a goalie establishing position in the crease has made contact with you, give ground at once and re-set with both skates off the crease line; the rule glosses that duty as "give ground to the goalkeeper", and where you do not, "the goal will be disallowed"
Rule: NHL and IIHF 69.3 add a penalty "whether or not a goal is scored" for refusing to give ground — a minor in the NHL's words, "the appropriate penalty" in the IIHF's; their Situation 6 D, a table row, disallows the goal you scored, so refusing costs you the goal or the penalty, never nothing
Rule: Keep your skates off the red crease line, not just out of the blue paint. USA Hockey 625(b) and CARHA 66(b) bar a goal with an attacker in the crease, and both count the line as crease, as do the IIHF at 1.7 and Hockey Canada — but the NHL's and IIHF's Situation 5 E allows the goal on it
Rule: In the crease or privileged area, a defender who shoves you into the goalie need not cost you a penalty — USA Hockey's Casebook at 607, Situation 4: "no penalty should be assessed provided the attacking player has clearly made every attempt to avoid the contact"; the attempt saves you
```

- **Get there.** Corbett's first principle is willingness: "You have to have a commitment to getting there before you can play there."
- **Play in front of the goalie, not beside them — and in front of the blue paint, not in it.** "If you're going to get to that blue paint, you need to be able to play right in front of the goalie, and be able to take away the goalie's eyes... you need to play on the shot lane, because the goalie is finding that shot lane." Read "get to that blue paint" as the ice you are attacking, not the ice you stand on: the screen is set with both skates outside the crease and off its red line. ⚠️ **Standing in the shot lane is the job here, the reverse of what a shot blocker is taught — so know when the puck is about to be released, and go there with your head and face protection right rather than nearly right** ([Equipment](../off-the-ice/equipment.md)). [Playing Without the Puck](../hockey-iq/playing_without_the_puck.md) sets the screener and the blocker side by side.
- **Two hands on your stick, blade on the ice.** You are there to tip, to screen, and to bury a rebound. All three require your blade available. **When you go for that rebound, play the puck and make a clear effort to avoid the goalie** — contact you could have avoided is what the books penalise, and Hockey Canada puts the onus to avoid it *"always on the attacking player"*. **Under USA Hockey even contact you could not avoid goes under interference** — 607(d) Note 1: *"any accidental or unavoidable contact that occurs with the goalkeeper shall be penalized under the Interference rule"* — unless a defender forced you into them and you clearly made every attempt to avoid it (below).
- **Track the puck while feeling the defender.** Eyes on the puck, body on the player.
- **And that is what makes this the hardest place on the ice to take contact — so know where the near post is, and take the contact low with your head up.** Your eyes are on the puck, so a defender working under your arms walks you sideways along a path with the goal frame at one edge and the goalie at the other. Take the contact the way [Receiving a Hit Safely](#6-receiving-a-hit-safely) teaches it — **wide base, knees bent, weight low, knees and hips giving** — with ⚠️ **your head up and your chin off your chest, and never duck:** the flexed neck is what turns a collision with a post into a spinal injury. *"Hit the boards or goal posts with an arm, a leg, or anything but your head first"* (USA Hockey, *Introduction To Body Contact*). That holds in any league, because a defender can push you along that path whether or not the push is legal.
- **There is one body at the net front you must not take braced — the goalie's. Give ground the moment a goalie leans into you, then re-set the screen with both skates off the crease line, not merely out of the blue.** ⚠️ **Under the NHL and the IIHF, refusing to give ground costs you the goal or a penalty, never nothing** (Rule 69.3), and under the IIHF — Britain's book — the red line counts as crease (Rule 1.7). **If a defender shoves you into the goalie, clearly try to avoid them: being shoved is not a defence, the attempt to avoid is.** [What it costs to touch the goalie](#what-it-costs-to-touch-the-goalie) prices every book, and [Offensive Zone Play](../systems/offensive_zone_play.md) has Rule 69 in full.

### Where you may stand when you screen

**In short:** Screen with your body alone, outside the crease and off its red line, touching nobody.

```facts
Never: Put a hand, a glove or your stick blade up in front of the goalie's face. Hands and blade down, and let your body do the screening — a screen is your body in their sightline, nothing else
Key: Legal is not safe at the net front — screen with your chin strap done up and a full cage's chin cup in contact with your chin rather than hanging below it, not merely with the equipment on
Rule: A screen set outside the crease without contact keeps the goal (NHL and IIHF Situations 5 C and 5 E); contact, or a position that stops the goalie moving freely in their crease, can cost it (NHL and IIHF 69.1)
Rule: Rule 69 is "enforced exclusively in accordance with the on-ice judgement of the Referee(s)" (NHL and IIHF 69.1). The crease line splits the books: the IIHF, USA Hockey, Hockey Canada and CARHA count it as crease, the NHL's and IIHF's Situation 5 E allows a goal on it — so screen off the crease line
Rule: Neither USA Hockey nor Hockey Canada reaches a screen set from outside the crease — USA Hockey writes no goalie-vision provision in its playing rules or its Casebook, and Hockey Canada's two references to the goalie's view, both at 8.5, reach only a player standing in the crease
Rule: USA Hockey 625(b) bars the goal on position — "No goal may be scored with an attacking player in the goal crease" — unless the puck preceded you in, the goalie is out of the crease area, or a defender's actions put you there; that last relief reaches your position only, not what you do next
Rule: Hockey Canada 8.5 is not flat — a teammate's goal from outside the crease stands if you stay passive in it, and is disallowed once you play the puck, interfere with the play or obstruct the goalie's view or movements, and a screen obstructs the view by definition
Rule: A crease screen can cost the zone with no goal scored, and the books differ — USA Hockey 625(b) sends the faceoff out of the zone, but only with puck in the zone, possession yours and goalie in contact with the crease; IIHF 69.1 leaves it to the referee; NHL 69.6 only after a disallowed goal
Rule: One screening act is a minor with no contact and no crease — the IIHF Situation Handbook, at 69.3 and 75.4, treats a hand, glove or stick held in front of the goalie's "eyes/face with the obvious intent of impairing the ability to see the puck/play" as unsportsmanlike conduct under Rule 75
Rule: Under Hockey Canada a crease screen with no contact costs the goal and nothing else — Note 1 to its Interpretation 3 to 8.5(a): "No penalty would be assessed unless the attacking player's body or stick makes actual physical contact with the goaltender"
Rule: Not every skate in the paint stops play — USA Hockey's Casebook at 625, Situation 10: play "should not be stopped because a player momentarily places their stick and/or skates in the goal crease, unless they interfere with the goalkeeper", but a goal scored while either is in there is disallowed
```

- **Screening is normally legal — set it outside the crease and touch nobody.** Outside the crease, a screen with no contact keeps the goal even when it blocks the goalie's view: the NHL and IIHF reference tables allow it (**Situations 5 C and 5 E**). ⚠️ **What costs the goal there is contact, or stopping the goalie moving freely in their crease** — the NHL's Rule 69.1 disallows a goal where an attacker, *"either by his positioning or by contact, impairs the goalkeeper’s ability to move freely within his crease or defend his goal"*, as do the IIHF's 69.1 and the PWHL's 71.1. Under Hockey Canada (Interpretation 1 to 8.5(a)), CARHA 66(b) and USA Hockey 617(c)(4) (*"interfering with the goalkeeper"*), contact costs the goal wherever it happens. ⚠️ **Never stop a goalie who played the puck outside the crease getting back** — *"the deliberate actions of an attacking player"* that do so can be penalised (69.4).
- **Keep your skates off the red crease line, not merely out of the blue paint — a skate's width further out costs you nothing in any book.** ⚠️ **Four books count that line as part of the crease, and under three of them standing on it costs the goal.** The IIHF does at Rule 1.7, USA Hockey in the Note to Rule 625(b), Hockey Canada in its Glossary and CARHA in its definition of the creases. **USA Hockey 625(b)**: *"No goal may be scored with an attacking player in the goal crease"* — unless the puck got there first, the goalie is out of the crease area, or a defender put you there — with no impairment to judge. **Hockey Canada 8.5** lets the goal stand only while the crease player does not *"obstruct the Goaltender's view or movements"*, and a screen obstructs the view by definition. **CARHA 66(b)**: unless the puck is in the crease, *"a player of the attacking side may not stand in the goal crease"*, and a goal scored while one does *"shall not be allowed"*. The NHL's and IIHF's Situation 5 E allows the goal to an attacker who *"plants himself on the crease line"*, so the IIHF answers the same play both ways; [Rink Map](../foundation/rink_map.md) sets out the collision.
- **Do not linger in the paint even when no goal is scored.** ⚠️ **Two books attach a cost with no goal at all.** Under **USA Hockey 625(b)** the faceoff *"shall be conducted at the nearest neutral zone face-off spot"* where an attacker *"stands, holds their stick, or skates through the goal crease"* — when the puck is in the attacking zone, your team has it and the goalie is in contact with the crease. **The IIHF** leaves the same consequence to the referee at 69.1: *"If an attacking Player is in the goal crease and does not leave immediately, the Referee has the possibility to stop the game"*. The NHL's 69.6 moves the faceoff only after a goal has been disallowed. USA Hockey's Casebook (Rule 625, Situation 10) will not stop play for a skate or stick *"momentarily"* in the paint, ⚠️ **but a goal scored while your stick or skates are in the crease with the goalie in it is disallowed** — so a blade in the paint takes the goal off exactly as a skate does.
- **If a defender puts you in the crease, get out.** USA Hockey 625(b) allows a goal where *"the attacking player has been physically interfered with by the actions of a defending player that causes them to be in the goal crease"* — ⚠️ **but that excuses your position only, not playing the puck or touching the goalie once you are there** ([Shooting](shooting.md) carries the limit). **Hockey Canada 8.5** lets a teammate's goal from outside the crease stand *"provided that the player who was in the goal crease does not attempt to play the puck, interfere with the play or obstruct the Goaltender's view or movements"*; with no contact that costs the goal and nothing else — *"No penalty would be assessed unless the attacking player's body or stick makes actual physical contact with the goaltender"* (Note 1 to Interpretation 3 to 8.5(a)). The goal it takes off is your own team's, so stay out of the paint.
- **Keep your hands, glove and blade down — a screen is your body in the sightline, nothing else.** ⚠️ **Holding a hand, glove or stick in front of the goalie's face is a minor with no contact and no crease involved**: the IIHF Situation Handbook (Situations 69.3 and 75.4, both current editions) treats a hand, glove or stick *"directly in front of the goalkeeper's eyes/face with the obvious intent of impairing the ability to see the puck/play"* as unsportsmanlike conduct under Rule 75.
- **And none of that makes the spot a safe one.** ⚠️ **Legal is not safe.** The screen puts you in the shot lane at close range and upright — chin strap done up, and a full cage's chin cup in contact with your chin rather than hanging below it ([Equipment](../off-the-ice/equipment.md)).

![](diagram:screen-the-goalie-sightline)

### What it costs to touch the goalie

**In short:** A screen never needs contact; make an obvious attempt to avoid the goalie every time.

```facts
Never: Touch anybody to screen, or count on "I was shoved in" — where a defender puts you into the goalie, only clearly making every attempt to avoid them keeps you out of the box
Key: The books do not price contact with a goalie alike — find out which your league runs, because a braced shove runs from a minor to leaving the game
Rule: USA Hockey sends a braced shove straight to charging — 607(d) Note 1 penalises "deliberate body contact or check that is delivered to the goalkeeper" as charging, and 607(c) writes no bare minor for it "while the goalkeeper is within the goal crease or privileged area"
```
