```facts
Rule: USA Hockey's "privileged area" is the low zone between the dots, not a box around the paint (607(d) Note 2) — a net-front screener is inside it always, a corner forechecker never. 607(d) adds that a goalie outside it is NOT "fair game" — unnecessary contact is a penalty in every case
Rule: USA Hockey's charging ladder has a rung the referee does not weigh — 607(b): a major plus a game misconduct "shall be assessed to any player who recklessly endangers an opponent as a result of charging" — and plan on a match penalty, which 602(a) says "shall be assessed" for reckless endangerment
Rule: Hockey Canada 8.5(a) is a minor and 8.5(b) does not stop there — a major plus a game misconduct at the referee's discretion "based on the degree of violence of impact" with nobody hurt, and mandatory both "to any player who charges the goaltender" and where the contact injures
Rule: Hockey Canada 8.5(c) is a match penalty for a player who "attempts to injure or deliberately injures a goaltender by Interference", and 8.5(d) bars any misconduct; a goalie hit from behind takes the harsher of 8.5 and 7.5, whose 7.5(c) match is mandatory if driven defenceless into the frame
Rule: Hockey Canada puts the onus "always on the attacking player" — Interpretation 1 to 8.5(a) disallows the goal for contact you initiate "inside or outside of the goal crease", excepting only an attacker whom a defender's foul left unable to avoid it, and Interpretation 2 requires an effort even then
Rule: NHL and IIHF 69.1 allow the goal for incidental contact begun outside the crease if you "made a reasonable effort to avoid" it; 69.4, and 42.1 at the referee's call, permit it on that effort while the goalie plays the puck outside; USA Hockey 607(d) Note 1 files accidental contact under Interference
Rule: Out of the crease a goalie is not "fair game" — NHL and IIHF 69.4, PWHL 71.4: "The appropriate penalty should be assessed in every case" of "unnecessary contact"; and an attacker whose "deliberate actions" keep one who played the puck outside from "returning" to the crease "may be penalized" as well
Rule: NHL and IIHF 69.2 make deliberate contact in or out of the crease a minor or major; NHL 42.1 reaches a major plus a game misconduct for charging a goalie "within his goal crease", IIHF 42.4 at the referee's discretion if reckless; NHL 42.5 compels it once a major is called for face or head injury
Rule: A goalie put into their own net from behind is IIHF 43, not 42 — 43.1 names the goal frame, 43.2 “no option to award a minor penalty”, 43.3 a major plus automatic game misconduct if reckless — plan on it. A charge to the head is 48: a minor, or a reckless major and game misconduct (48.2, 48.3)
Rule: In the EIHL, expect contact in the paint to cost the goal even in a scramble — its Casebook adds to IIHF Rule 69 that "Contact in the blue paint area calls for disallowing a goal", without repeating 69.7's rebound allowance or saying how the two fit
Rule: CARHA 66(b) makes impeding a goalie "by actual physical contact" a minor, and deliberate contact, in or out of the crease, a minor or major; 52(b) compels a major and game misconduct for charging one in the crease or any charge that injures; a major ejects you (30(a)) (CARHA adult leagues only)
```

**Every book prices contact with a goalie differently, so find out which yours runs — a braced shove runs from a minor to leaving the game.** In all of them the instruction is the same: a screen never has to touch anybody, and **make an attempt to avoid the goalie every time, and make it obvious** — every relief below is bought with it.

**NHL and IIHF.** 69.2 in both books is a penalty *"(minor or major, as the Referee deems appropriate)"* for deliberate contact *"whether or not the goalkeeper is inside or outside the goal crease"*. ⚠️ **Only incidental contact is permitted (69.1)**, *"when such contact is initiated outside of the goal crease, provided the attacking player has made a reasonable effort to avoid such contact"*, and 69.4 the same while the goalie plays the puck outside the crease. But a goalie *"is not “fair game” just because he is outside the goal crease"*, and *"[t]he appropriate penalty should be assessed in every case where an attacking player makes unnecessary contact with the goalkeeper"* (69.4; 42.1 for any player). Under 69.3, once a goalie establishing position in the crease has made contact with you, an attacker who *"does not immediately vacate his current position in the goal crease"* — *"give ground to the goalkeeper"* — loses the goal, and the rule adds a penalty *"whether or not a goal is scored"*. The reference tables (Situation 6 D) take the goal off instead of the penalty where you did score.

**Charging a goalie, NHL and IIHF.** NHL **42.1** is *"[a] minor, major or a major and a game misconduct"* for charging a goalie *"while the goalkeeper is within his goal crease"*, and **42.5** makes the game misconduct mandatory once the major is for an injury to the face or head; IIHF **42.4** reaches a major plus a game misconduct for reckless charging with nobody hurt. ⚠️ **The route can remove the minor.** Drive a goalie into their own net from behind and the IIHF reaches it under **Rule 43**, where *"there is no option to award a minor penalty"* (43.2) and 43.3 adds *"an automatic game misconduct penalty"*, because 43.1 names the goal frame and Rule 5.1 makes *"Player"* cover goalies (a reading of how the book is assembled). The NHL and the PWHL do the same at their 43.2, 43.3 and 43.5, with a match above at 43.4.

Under the IIHF, a *charge* that reaches the head goes to Rule 48 (42.1): a minor (48.2), or a major plus an automatic game misconduct if reckless (48.3). For a hit from behind that reaches the head, plan on the major and game misconduct (43.3, 48.3), never on a minor. ⚠️ **The IIHF's ceiling, a major plus a game misconduct, is the whole game** — Rule 20.4 rules that pair *"off the ice for the balance of the game"* — with anything beyond it left to Rule 28 supplementary discipline.

**USA Hockey: a braced shove into a goalie is charging, not an interference minor.** Note 1 to **Rule 607(d)**: *"any accidental or unavoidable contact that occurs with the goalkeeper shall be penalized under the Interference rule"*, but *"[a]ny deliberate body contact or check that is delivered to the goalkeeper shall be penalized as charging."* **607(c)** writes no bare minor for it: *"A minor plus a misconduct or a major plus a game misconduct penalty shall be assessed to a player who body checks or charges a goalkeeper while the goalkeeper is within the goal crease or privileged area"*. ⚠️ **Charge a goalie recklessly and the major plus game misconduct is not the referee's to weigh**: 607(b) says it *"shall be assessed to any player who recklessly endangers an opponent as a result of charging"*. **Plan on a match penalty too**, because Rule 602(a) says one *"shall be assessed"* to anyone who *"recklessly endangers or attempts to injure"* an opponent (607(e) says only *"may also"*).

**USA Hockey's Casebook prices being shoved in, on three rungs** (Rule 607, Situation 4, for a goalie *"in their crease or privileged area"*): no penalty where a defender forced you in, *"provided the attacking player has clearly made every attempt to avoid the contact with the goalkeeper"*; a minor plus a misconduct where you *"makes an honest attempt to avoid the goalkeeper once it is realized contact is unavoidable"*; and *"the major plus game misconduct, or match penalty option"* where you made no attempt and *"recklessly endangers the goalkeeper by accelerating through the contact, or by extending the arms or dropping the shoulder to deliver the hit"*. Plan on the match there (602(a)). ⚠️ **So 625(b) keeps your goal with no avoidance condition; only clearly trying to avoid the goalie keeps you out of the box.**

**USA Hockey's privileged area is the whole low zone between the dots, not a box around the paint** — *"an area outlined by connecting the end zone face-off spots with an imaginary line and imaginary lines from each face-off spot running perpendicular to the end boards"* (Note 2 to Rule 607), extending behind the net to the end boards (Casebook 607, Situation 6) but not into the corners. **A net-front screener is inside it always; a forechecker in the corner is outside it.** ⚠️ **Outside it a goalie is still not fair game:** *"A penalty for interference or charging should be called in every case where an opposing player makes unnecessary contact with a goalkeeper"* (607(d)).

The Casebook (Rule 607, Situation 5) lets a goalie outside it be checked only to win a puck the goalie holds, and only in a game Rule 604 permits checking in — remembering 604(b)'s non-checking fixture. Where checking is barred that hit is a minor under 604(c), with 604(d)'s major plus game misconduct or 604(e)'s match above it if reckless (plan on the match, 602(a)), and 607(d) Note 1 still files deliberate body contact with a goalie as charging, at least a minor plus a misconduct (607(a)). ⚠️ **A check to intimidate or punish is charging anywhere.** [Language and Glossary](../foundation/language_and_glossary.md) defines the term.

**Hockey Canada starts at a minor and does not stop there.** **8.5(a)** is the minor; **8.5(b)** reaches *"A Major penalty and Game Misconduct penalty"* at the referee's discretion *"based on the degree of violence of impact"* with nobody hurt, and ⚠️ **mandatorily** — *"will be assessed"* — *"to any player who charges the goaltender"* and for any such foul that injures. No stride count is needed: Rule 7.4 counts a player who *"[v]iolently and unnecessarily checks an opponent in any manner"* as charging. **8.5(d)** rules out a misconduct, so there is no ten-minute rung beneath the ejection, and **8.5(c)** is a match for a player who *"attempts to injure or deliberately injures a goaltender by Interference."* **If the charge reaches the head, plan on the harsher of head contact (7.6; 7.7 in Junior and Senior) and 8.5(b): the major and game misconduct, which can be a match.**

**Under Hockey Canada, being fouled into a goalie is not the excuse; the attempt to avoid them is.** The onus is *"always on the attacking player"*, and **Interpretation 1 to 8.5(a)** takes the goal off for contact an attacker initiates *"regardless of whether the contact occurs inside or outside of the goal crease"*, excepting only an attacker a defender's foul left *"unable to avoid contact with the goaltender."* ⚠️ **Interpretation 2 conditions even that**: an attacker who is *"tripped, hooked, cross-checked, or otherwise interfered with, falls, and makes contact with the goaltender"* must make *"an effort … to avoid making contact"*, or *"they must be penalized for interference with the goaltender"* — and the defender's foul is penalised as well. **From behind, it is the harsher of 7.5 and 8.5:** the Glossary counts the goalie as a player, so a goalie driven from behind is priced at whichever costs more. **7.5**'s floor is a minor and a game misconduct, and **7.5(c)** is a mandatory match for a hit *"from behind into the boards or goal frame, in such a way that the player is unable to protect or defend themselves."*

**In the EIHL,** ⚠️ **go to the net expecting contact in the paint to cost the goal, even in a rebound scramble — and ask your officials how they call it.** The **EIHL Casebook 26-27**, under *“EIHL Addition to the IIHF Rule Book”*, sets *“the 3 main criteria”* for Rule 69 — whether the goalie can play their position and recover, who initiated the contact, and *“Was the contact made inside (or outside) of the crease? **Contact in the blue paint area calls for disallowing a goal.**”* That is harder than IIHF 69.1's relief for incidental contact *“initiated outside of the goal crease”*. **IIHF 69.7** still permits *“incidental contact”* *“[i]n a rebound situation, or where a goalkeeper and attacking Player(s) are simultaneously attempting to play a loose puck, **whether inside or outside the goal crease**”*, and the Casebook neither repeats nor repeals it; nothing in the books read here says how the two fit. Elsewhere in Britain IIHF 69.1 and 69.7 stand unamended, except that *“[o]n-ice video review is not available in EIH or SIHA competition”* (In-House Rules 2026-27).

**Under CARHA, never charge a goalie in the paint or arrive there carrying speed — and a charge that hurts anybody is a mandatory ejection anywhere on the ice.** **Rule 52(b)** assesses a major and a game misconduct — *shall*, not *may* — to *"any player who charges a goalkeeper while the goalkeeper is within the goal crease or who injures an opponent as a result of a charge"*. **52(a)**'s Note counts *"more than two steps or strides"* as a charge. ⚠️ **That is two limbs joined by an *or*, and only the first is keyed to the crease**: the injury limb reaches anywhere, and 52(a) writes the same into the general charging rule. ⚠️ **Nor does 53(b) spare a goalie:** a hit from behind *"into the boards or goal net"* on a player unable to protect themselves is a mandatory match.

**Under CARHA, contact that is not a charge costs too.** **Rule 66(b)** gives a minor for impeding the goalie *"by actual physical contact"*, and a minor or, at the referee's discretion, a major for an attacker who *"makes deliberate contact with a goalkeeper whether in or out of the goal crease"*; **Rule 66(e)** makes it a major where the interference injures. That book writes no privileged area, and 52(b)'s Note says *"[a] goalkeeper is NOT 'fair game' just because they are outside the goal crease area"*. Any major there costs you the rest of the game under **Rule 30(a)** plus at least the next one under **Rule 32(d)** (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only).

### Defending the net front

**In short:** Hold body position without leaning or pushing, lift or press their stick as the puck arrives, and never walk the screener out.

```facts
Priority: Body position first — inside shoulder ahead of theirs, hips between them and the puck, then hold it with no lean and no push
Action: Tie sticks, not cross-checks: lift theirs up, low on the shaft, well below the bottom hand, or press its blade lightly as the puck arrives, then let go. It kills the tip cheaply; held, it is a penalty
Rule: USA Hockey defines interference as impeding a non-puck carrier "with no effort to play the puck, maintain normal foot speed or maintain an established skating lane" (625 Note); Hockey Canada 8.3(i) and CARHA 66(a)(1) reach impeding one "not in possession of the puck" — a screener has none
Rule: Driving an opponent off an established spot is a push — a minor under USA Hockey 604(c) at 12U and below, in all girls'/women's and all non-check adult, and not capped there: 604(d) is a major plus a game misconduct for reckless endangerment — plan on a match too (602(a), 604(e))
Rule: Hockey Canada prices pushing an opponent off an established spot at U13 and below, in female hockey and in any division a Member approves — 7.3(a) a minor, 7.3(b) a major plus a game misconduct on violence, nobody hurt, mandatory on injury, 7.3(c) a match for deliberately injuring or trying to
Rule: In women's hockey it is an illegal hit under IIHF 101.1, where "[a]ny move by a Player to step or glide into an opposing Player" is at least a minor — and not capped there either: 101.1 provides a major (5') plus an automatic game misconduct
Convention: Leverage over strength — the lower player usually wins, though not always and not against a large enough strength gap. Coaching consensus, not a measured law: nothing found in the ice hockey biomechanics literature quantifies it
Read: Eyes on the puck, body on the player — you cannot beat them to a rebound you did not see leave the stick
Never: Walk a screener out in any game — it can be called interference — and least of all across your own goalmouth, where a moved body meets a goal post at one edge and your goalie at the other
Never: Back into your own goalie — you screen your goalie, and you arrive backwards onto a goalie who is down, square to the shot and unable to see you coming
```

The order of operations, and the order matters:

**1. Body position first.** Inside shoulder ahead of theirs, hips between them and the puck, box out — the same idea as boxing out for a rebound in basketball. If you are established inside, they have to come through you — so hold that ground, and do not lean on them or push to keep it.

**2. Stick ties, not cross-checks.** Corbett: "I'm more of a guy to take away sticks... You have to be able to, one, control sticks more than anything, and then wait for that shot to come and get physical." A stick lift or a light press on their blade, **made as the puck arrives, then let go**, removes the tip and the rebound at far lower risk than a cross-check. Lift it **up rather than sideways, low on the shaft, well below their bottom hand**, because at or near the hands it can be hooking ([Stick Checks](#3-stick-checks--legal-effective-and-under-used)). **The timing is the other half of the permission.** USA Hockey's *Casebook* calls *"a stick lift or stick press"* *"a good defensive play when the puck is in the vicinity"* (Standard of Play, Situation 5), but one that *"clearly impedes the opponent with no reasonable effort to play the puck"* is slashing, holding or hooking (Situation 3). **So a press kept on a screener's stick before the puck is anywhere near can be called holding** — under Hockey Canada 8.1 any action *"prohibiting their ability to pass, shoot, receive"*, and under CARHA 63(a) a hold *"with the hands, stick"*, a minor or a major that ejects you.

**3. Hold your position — never walk them out.** Once you have the inside position, stay there — low and wide, no lean, no push — and let them come through you. ⚠️ **Do not try to move a screener in any game, checking league or not.** The screener has no puck, so a body check is not what you would be delivering: what reaches you is **interference**, which is scoped to no division, and a violent or reckless walk-out can cost a major and, in some books, your game.

**4. Leverage over strength.** Same principle as the board battle, with the same caveats: the lower player usually wins, though not always and not against a large enough strength gap, and it is coaching consensus rather than a measured law.

**5. Eyes on the puck, body on the player.** Feel them; watch the shot. You cannot beat them to a rebound you did not see leave the stick, and a defender who turns to look at the player they are boxing out has stopped defending the dangerous thing.

**6. Do not back into your own goalie.** Being pushed into the crease screens your own goalie and turns saves into goals — you have done the attacker's job for them. ⚠️ **And the goal is not the only cost.** You arrive there backwards, on a goalie who is usually down, square to the shooter and unable to see you, with the post beside the pair of you — so fight the shove early rather than ride it into the paint. That second half is coaching caution; no source here counts injuries from it. See [Goaltender](../positions/goaltender.md) and [Receiving a Hit Safely](#6-receiving-a-hit-safely).

**Never move a body across your own goalmouth.** A net-front battle can move a body without anyone meaning to: a scrum, a lost edge, a push that went further than you meant. A body moved across the front of your own net travels sideways or backwards between a steel goal frame and your goalie, and it is the player being moved who meets the post. ⚠️ **USA Hockey puts the goal post in the spinal-injury mechanism alongside the boards:** with the chin tucked the cervical spine straightens, and *"when a player hits the boards or a goal post head on, the head stops suddenly, but the body's movement continues, compressing the spine"* — an injury that *"can occur at walking speed"* (*Heads Up Hockey Program Guide*).

**One more limit.** ⚠️ **Never take a player's skates off the ice at the net front.** A player whose edges are gone cannot brace and cannot choose what meets the post first. No book here writes a penalty for that lift on its own, so the reason is the collision. The NHL and the PWHL do list *"unnecessary extension of the body upward or outward"* among the signs that head contact was avoidable (48.1), so the tariff arrives if their head is what you reach.

![](diagram:net-front-walk-out-direction)

The picture above draws the job, not a move: hold the inside beside the screener, and move nobody into or across the shaded band over the goal frame and your goalie. **Whose job the net front is in the first place is a coaching choice, not a law of hockey.** The picture draws a defenceman because that is the commonest case. Under the standard low zone collapse it is the *weak-side* defenceman's; under **man-on-man** it belongs to whoever's check went there, which can as easily be a forward. Find out which your team plays; [Defensive Zone Coverage](../systems/defensive_zone_coverage.md) compares the systems.

**What it costs.** **Moving a screener is interference, in every game.** **NHL 56.2(iii)** writes a minor for a player who *"deliberately checks an opponent, including the goalkeeper, who is not in possession of the puck"*, and **56.2(i)** one for a player who *"interferes with or impedes the progress of an opponent who is not in possession of the puck"*. The PWHL's 57.2(iii) says the same, and the IIHF's 56.2(III) reaches one *"not or no longer deemed in possession"*.

**USA Hockey**'s Note to Rule 625 reaches a player using their body to impede *"an opponent (non-puck carrier) with no effort to play the puck, maintain normal foot speed or maintain an established skating lane"*, and **625(a)(4)** *"[a] defending player who changes their skating lane or foot speed in an effort to play the body of an opponent who is no longer in control of the puck"*. Rule 625 announces no Competitive Contact scope, so it reaches your checking league too. **Hockey Canada 8.3(i)** and **CARHA 66(a)(1)** reach any player who impedes an opponent *"not in possession of the puck"*. Interference is usually two minutes, but a violent or reckless play can cost a major, in some books with an ejection ([What actually gets called](#what-actually-gets-called)).

**What the books protect is the box-out itself.** NHL and IIHF 56.1 need *both* limbs — *"Moving laterally **and** without establishing body position, then making contact with the non-puck carrier"* — so holding a position you did establish is outside that clause, ⚠️ **though a push made from it is not outside 56.2.** USA Hockey's Standard of Play grants it in all age classifications: *"A player is entitled to use proper body position and competitive contact in all age classifications in order to gain a competitive advantage"*, and *"Players are entitled to the ice they occupy as long as they are able to maintain their own foot speed and body position between opponent and puck"*. Its Note to 604(c) blesses players *"simply maintaining established body position"*, most often *"in front of the goal or along the boards"*. The IIHF's 56.1 and the PWHL's 57.1 grant the ice you are standing on, and IIHF 101.1 lets women players *"'hold their ground'"*. CARHA grants defenders the standing half only (66(a) Note 2), with 49(a) still pricing a player who *"does not avert body contact"*.

**Under Hockey Canada,** ⚠️ **holding the spot itself can be called interference**, because that book writes no body-position grant and 8.3's minor reaches any player who impedes an opponent *"who is not in possession of the puck"*. ⚠️ **That is why step 1 comes first and is not optional.**

**Where checking is barred or restricted, driving a player off their spot is a check as well, and its minor is not the ceiling.** USA Hockey 604(c): *"Legal competitive contact shall not be penalized under this rule. However, deliberate physical contact with an opponent, with no effort to legally play the puck, shall be penalized"* — a minor in every Competitive Contact classification. Hockey Canada 7.3(a) writes a minor for a player who *"intentionally body-checks, bumps, shoves, or pushes any opposing player"* at U13 and below, in female hockey and in any division a Member has approved. USA Hockey 604(d) ⚠️ **is a major plus a game misconduct** for a player who *"recklessly endangers an opponent with a body check in a Competitive Contact category"*, and **604(e)** a match for the same reckless check, which you should plan on (602(a)). Hockey Canada **7.3(b)** reaches the same pair on *"the degree of violence of the impact"* — **nobody has to be hurt** — compulsory where it injures, with **7.3(c)** a match for a deliberate attempt to injure.

In women's hockey **IIHF Rule 101.1** ⚠️ **makes the walk-out an illegal hit** — *"at least a minor penalty (2')"* — with a major (5') plus an automatic game misconduct for the worse ones.

**Driving a player into the goal frame** from behind is a major plus a game misconduct under **USA Hockey 608(b)**, whose first limb — *"recklessly endangers an opponent"* — carries no board or frame condition. Plan on a match where it is reckless (602(a)); **608(c)** is a match *"in all instances"* for excessive force on a vulnerable or defenceless opponent. It is a **match** under **Hockey Canada 7.5(c)** where the player driven into the boards or goal frame could not protect or defend themselves — and if it reaches the head, whichever of that and head contact costs more (7.6; 7.7 in Junior and Senior). Under **IIHF Rule 43.1** it is checking from behind, which names *"the goal frame"* and writes no minor (43.2). The tiers are in [Checking from behind](#checking-from-behind-and-what-the-onus-does-and-does-not-mean).

### The cross-check problem

**In short:** At the net front, tie up the screener's stick instead of cross-checking their body, and never put your shaft into anybody's numbers.

```facts
Rule: Cross-checking is using the shaft of the stick between the two hands to forcefully check an opponent (NHL Rule 59.1)
Never: Believe a blade on the ice makes a shaft shove legal — that wording is USA Hockey Rule 609 only, not NHL, IIHF or Hockey Canada
Rule: USA Hockey's Standard of Play and Rule Emphasis – Body Checking gives the net-front shaft-and-arms shove to create space as the first of its two cross-checking examples (the playing rule itself, 609, gives none)
Key: Cross-checking is the smallest of the seven infractions broken out at the net front, and no likelier there than league-wide — the emphasis is real, the frequency ordinary
Key: Net-front penalty counts are computed for this document from the NHL play-by-play feed, 400 games of 2024-25, not a published finding — hooking and slashing are the fouls that concentrate there
Rule: A cross-check is not capped at a minor in any book, and no major needs an injury — NHL 59.3 on the severity of the contact, with an automatic game misconduct at 59.5 and a match penalty at 59.4; USA Hockey 609(b) a major plus game misconduct for reckless endangerment — plan on a match too (602(a))
Rule: Under the IIHF book, Britain's, a cross-check reaches a major plus an automatic game misconduct at 59.3 for recklessly endangering an opponent, nobody hurt — identical in the 2025/26 v1.1 and 2026/27 editions, and no match tier sits above it, only Supplementary Discipline (59.6)
Rule: Hockey Canada 9.2(b) makes a cross-check a major plus a game misconduct on violence with nobody hurt, and mandatory where it injures; 9.2(c) is a match; above the shoulders it is a mandatory major plus game misconduct, "whether or not injury results" — 7.7(b), and 7.6(b) for a deliberate cross-check
Rule: CARHA 54(b) is a major plus a game misconduct for a cross-check above the shoulders, "whether or not injury results", and 54(c) the same where you crosscheck a goalie in the crease or injure anyone; 54(d) is a match penalty (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Rule: A cross-check to the back is often not cross-checking at all — NHL 43.3, IIHF 43.1 and PWHL 43.3 name it inside checking from behind, with no minor rung (43.2); CARHA 53(b) and USA Hockey (on force) route it there too, Hockey Canada in motion or into the boards or goal (Interp. 1, 7.5(a))
Rule: A cross-check from behind into the boards or goal frame on a player who cannot protect themselves is a mandatory match at Hockey Canada 7.5(c) and CARHA 53(b) (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only); USA Hockey 608(c) makes it one for reckless excessive force, NHL and PWHL 21.1 for intent to injure
Action: Control the stick instead of the body — lift it up, low on the shaft, well below their bottom hand, or press its blade lightly, as the puck arrives, then let go. It does a cross-check's job
Action: Establish position early and quietly; lift or press their stick when the shot comes, not before, and never move the screener with your body — that can be called interference in any game
```

The stick tie does the job the cross-check was meant to do, and no book caps a cross-check at two minutes.

**The real defence is a different action: control the stick instead of the body.** Lift or lightly press it as the puck arrives, then let go — up rather than sideways, low on the shaft, well below their bottom hand ([Stick Checks](#3-stick-checks--legal-effective-and-under-used)). A press held on a screener with no play on the puck can be called holding ([Defending the net front](#defending-the-net-front)).

Corbett's warning about timing is worth repeating: "if you get physical before the puck is on its way, you're going to get a penalty." Establish position early and quietly, lift or press their stick when the shot comes, and keep your body to holding your ground. Moving the screener with your body risks a call every time — interference in any game, and in a non-check game the contact itself, which USA Hockey 604(c), Hockey Canada 7.3(a) and CARHA 49(a) all penalise.

**If you can see their numbers, do not hit or shove them.** Their back to you is what brings checking from behind into it, and the harder you push, the higher the price — see the last paragraphs of this section.

**Repeated cross-checking to the back is the penalty officials are specifically told to look for at the net front.** USA Hockey defines cross-checking at **Rule 609** as "using the shaft of the stick between the two hands to forcefully check an opponent with no portion of the stick on the ice", and its **Standard of Play and Rule Emphasis – Body Checking** gives exactly this scenario as its first cross-checking example: "When a player in front of the goal uses the shaft of the stick and extension of the arms to contact an opponent in a dangerous manner to create space." (The second is the same shove on a player protecting the puck along the boards.)

**What the call data actually shows.** Computed for this document from the NHL's public play-by-play feed (`api-web.nhle.com/v1/gamecenter/{id}/play-by-play`, games 2024020001–2024020400 of the 2024-25 regular season): **2,967 penalties**, of which **265** fall inside a net-front box 10 to 25 feet out from the goal line and within 10 feet either side of centre.

| Infraction | Share of net-front calls | League-wide share | Enrichment |
|---|---|---|---|
| Hooking | 25.3% | 12.1% | **2.10×** |
| Slashing | 15.1% | 8.6% | **1.76×** |
| Interference | 10.6% | 8.3% | 1.27× |
| Tripping | 10.2% | 16.6% | 0.61× |
| Roughing | 8.3% | 12.0% | 0.69× |
| Holding | 7.2% | 8.6% | 0.83× |
| **Cross-checking** | **5.7%** | **5.2%** | **1.08×** |

**Cross-checking is neither the most-called penalty at the net front nor over-represented there**: it is the smallest of the seven types broken out, called at almost exactly its league-wide rate. The penalties that concentrate there are **hooking and slashing** — stick fouls on a player trying to reach a puck. The seven figures rank one infraction against another and support nothing bigger. They cover 71.4% of calls, and tripping, the largest of them, is a stick foul and a leg foul alike, so they cannot say whether stick fouls as a group lead. Treat USA Hockey's net-front emphasis as what it is — **an instruction to officials about what to watch for**, not a claim about frequency.

**The rule itself is narrow and easy to break by accident.** NHL Rule 59.1: *"The action of using the shaft of the stick between the two hands to forcefully check an opponent."* **There is no blade-on-ice element in it.** You will hear that keeping your blade on the ice means it cannot be a cross-check. That is not the NHL, IIHF, Hockey Canada, PWHL or CARHA rule: under every one a two-handed shaft shove is a cross-check whatever your blade is doing. ⚠️ **The blade-on-ice phrasing is USA Hockey Rule 609's alone**, an officiating cue for one association, so under any other book you are exposed regardless of your blade.

**What it costs.** **A cross-check is not a two-minute foul in any book here.** Every one writes a major above the minor, and none of those majors needs an injury.

- **NHL.** 59.2 makes the minor discretionary *"based on the severity of the contact"*, and **59.3 makes a major discretionary on the same test** — the severity of the contact, not the consequence. **59.5 then attaches an automatic game misconduct to every cross-checking major:** *"When a major penalty is assessed for cross-checking, an automatic game misconduct penalty shall be imposed"*. **59.4** is a match penalty where, in the referee's judgment, *"the player attempted to or deliberately injured his opponent by cross-checking."* **The PWHL writes the same four rungs, renumbered to its Rule 60** — a discretionary minor at 60.2 and a discretionary major at 60.3, both *"based on the severity of the contact"*, an automatic game misconduct with the major at 60.5, and a match penalty at 60.4 written as a discretion. **In both books Rule 21.1 then takes the discretion away for intent**: a match *"shall be imposed"* on a player who deliberately injures an opponent or attempts to.
- 🇬🇧 **IIHF — your book in Britain.** **59.3** is headed *"Major Penalty and Game Misconduct Penalty"*, and the referee may assess *"a major penalty and an automatic game misconduct penalty if, in their judgment, the Player recklessly endangers their opponent by a 'cross-checking' and the Player would not be sufficiently sanctioned by imposing a minor penalty."* **Nobody has to be hurt.** The wording is word for word identical in the 2025/26 (Version 1.1) and 2026/27 editions, so the edition question that bites on high-sticking does not bite here. There is no tier above it on the ice, because the IIHF book writes no match penalty anywhere. 59.6 allows Rule 28 supplementary discipline above it, at the Proper Authorities' discretion.
- **USA Hockey.** **609(b)**: *"A major plus a game misconduct penalty shall be assessed to any player who recklessly endangers an opponent as a result of cross-checking."* **609(c)**: *"A match penalty for reckless endangerment may also be assessed for cross-checking."* Neither turns on injury either, and for a reckless cross-check plan on the match: general Rule **602(a)** says one *"shall be assessed"* for reckless endangerment.
- **Hockey Canada.** **9.2(b)** gives the referee a major plus a game misconduct *"based on the degree of violence of impact"*, and makes the same tier compulsory — *"will be assessed"* — for any player *"who injures an opponent by a cross-checking infraction that would otherwise call for a Minor penalty"*. **9.2(e)** requires the game misconduct with any such major, and **9.2(c)** is a match penalty for a player who *"attempts to injure or deliberately injures an opponent by cross-checking."* Its definition leaves out the word *"forcefully"* (CARHA's does too), and **Interpretation 1 to Rule 9.2(a)** has the referee *"consider whether the player is 'pushing' or 'striking' an opponent with the shaft of their stick. If a pushing motion is used, a penalty may not be necessary. However, a striking motion must be penalized."* A push is still a cross-check on the book's own definition, and still the referee's to call.
- **CARHA — CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only, and the book that takes the discretion away twice.** **54(a)** is a minor or, at the referee's discretion, a major plus a game misconduct. Above that it stops asking. **54(b)** assesses a major plus a game misconduct to any player who strikes an opponent *"above the normal height of the opponents shoulders with a cross-check"*, *"whether or not injury results"*. So the shove that rides up is priced in the cross-checking rule itself, not routed out to head contact the way Hockey Canada routes it. **54(c)** assesses the same pair *"to any player who crosschecks a goalkeeper while they are in the goal crease, or who injures an opponent as a result of a cross-check"* — ⚠️ **two limbs joined by *"or"*, so the injury limb carries no crease and no goalie in it at all.** **54(d)** is a match penalty for a player who *"deliberately attempts to or deliberately injures an opponent with a cross-check."* **Carry the crease limb to the net front:** under that book, shaft-shoving the goalie in their own crease is not a call the referee can keep at two minutes.

**And Hockey Canada takes the referee's discretion away for the cross-check that most concerns a net-front player.** Interpretation 2 to Rule 9.2(b): *"A Major penalty and Game Misconduct penalty for Head Contact **must** be assessed to any player who strikes an opponent with a cross-check above the normal height of the shoulders."* A shove that starts between the shoulder blades and rides up is a different and harsher foul there. ⚠️ **And the head-contact rules price it with nobody hurt:** **7.6(b)** for minor and female hockey and **7.7(b)** for junior and senior both say *"Any player who strikes an opponent above the normal height of their shoulders with a cross-check will be penalized with a Major penalty and a Game Misconduct penalty, whether or not injury results."* Interpretation 2 to Rule 7.6(b) narrows only what counts in minor and female hockey: it *"is not intended to penalize players who make minimal, accidental contact with an opponent's head with their stick"*, but is aimed at *"a deliberate cross-checking action"*. 7.7 writes no such softening.

🇬🇧 **In EIH and SIHA competition the same contact is labelled the other way, and it costs the same.** The In-House Rules' **Section 4**: *"If one player cross checks another player in the head or neck area, we want the penalty to be called Cross Checking, not Checking to the Head."* Under the IIHF book the two ladders are written alike — a discretionary minor, then a major plus an automatic game misconduct for recklessly endangering an opponent (**48.2** and **48.3** for the head, **59.2** and **59.3** for cross-checking) — so the label changes what the call is named, not the rungs.

**The shove into a player's numbers is not priced as a cross-check at all.** ⚠️ **Three books write the cross-check into their checking-from-behind rule with no minor rung:** **NHL 43.3** assesses a major to *"[a]ny player who cross-checks, pushes or charges an opponent from behind who is unable to protect or defend himself"*, *"anywhere on the playing surface"*, **43.2** says *"[t]here is no provision for a minor penalty for checking from behind"*, and 43.5 adds the game misconduct. **PWHL 43.3 and 43.2** say the same, and 🇬🇧 **IIHF 43.1** reaches a hit from behind *"in any manner (i.e., high-sticking, cross-checking, etc.)"*.

**The other three draw the line for you.** **Hockey Canada's Interpretation 1 to Rule 7.5(a)**: *"If the player is stationary and cross-checks an opponent from behind, then a penalty for Cross-checking should be assessed. If the player is in motion and cross-checks an opponent from behind, then a penalty for Checking from Behind will be called. If a player is cross-checked from behind into the boards or the goal, then Checking from Behind will be called"* — ⚠️ **and where that player cannot protect themselves, 7.5(c) makes it a match penalty, mandatorily.** **CARHA 53(b)** names the cross-check inside its flat match penalty for a player *"cross-checked… in any manner from behind into the boards or goal net, in such a way that the player is unable to protect or defend themselves"* (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only).

**USA Hockey draws it by force, on exactly the board battle this document is about.** Its *Casebook* at **Rule 609, Situation 2** has a player protecting the puck *"by having their back turned to center ice"* and an opponent who extends the arms to the back with the stick *"but not use enough force to be considered a check from behind. This action should still be considered a cross-checking violation."* So the light shove is the cross-check. The forceful one is **608(a)**, a minor *plus* a misconduct at the floor, or **608(b)**'s major plus a game misconduct for a player who *"recklessly endangers an opponent, or causes them to go head first into the boards or goal frame"*. Plan on a match where it is reckless (602(a)). ⚠️ **And for this player — on the wall, back to centre ice — a forceful hit takes that floor away:** a major plus a game misconduct, or a match, *"must be called"* *"[i]n every instance where a player forcefully checks an opponent who is standing along the boards (back toward the middle of the ice)"* (Rule 608, Situation 1).

---

## 10. Loose Puck Races and 50-50 Pucks

A **50-50 puck** is any puck that neither team possesses and both can reach — a rim around the boards, a dump-in, a rebound, a puck flipped out of the zone.

### Arrive with body position, not just with speed

**In short:** Take the inside lane on an arc, hips between opponent and puck, never holding; under Hockey Canada or CARHA, only a lane you already have.

```facts
Position: Skate the inside lane, between the opponent and the puck — half a step behind but inside usually wins it
Rule: USA Hockey: a skater is entitled to the ice they occupy so long as they maintain their skating speed and body position between an opponent and the loose puck — and, separately, is entitled to stand their ground and not move if an opponent wishes to skate through (Standard of Play)
Rule: The NHL attaches no speed condition — a player "is allowed the ice he is standing on (body position) and is not required to move in order to let an opponent proceed" (NHL Rule 56.1)
Rule: Hockey Canada and CARHA write no right to lengthen an opponent's path — both make impeding one without the puck interference (Hockey Canada 8.3, CARHA 66(a)), and CARHA 49(a) adds a duty to avert body contact: hold only a lane you already have, and never clear one for a teammate
Technique: Approach on an arc so you arrive already turned, and turn your hips so your backside is between opponent and puck
Position: Get low and wide, feet outside your shoulders, and keep your feet moving — a stationary player on the wall gets pinned
Never: Use a stick, arm, hand or skates to hold or obstruct, your free hand to hold, pull, tug or grab, or the position itself as cover for a check that would otherwise be illegal
Rule: The free hand may fend off an opponent or their stick, but may not hold their stick or body — NHL and IIHF Rule 56.1; Hockey Canada 8.1 permits blocking a stick but not holding it
Rule: USA Hockey writes the fend-off permission in its Casebook only, and conditions it — control the puck (Standard of Play Situation 6) or be reaching for it, engaged in a corner or in front of the goal (Situation 7), hold a position you "legally established", and never push off to create space
```

Winning the race is not the same as winning the puck. The player who arrives half a step behind but *on the inside*, between the opponent and the puck, usually ends up with it.

![](diagram:inside-lane-longer-route)

**So: skate the inside lane, make them go the long way round, and do not use your hands.** The mechanics, in the order they happen. **Approach on an arc**, not straight at the puck, so that you get there already turned. **Turn your hips** so your backside is between the opponent and the puck rather than arriving square. **Get low and wide**, feet outside your shoulders. **Keep your feet moving**: a stationary player on the wall gets pinned, while one with momentum can escape along the boards or spin out to the middle. And **fend with the free hand, never hold**: it may fend off an opponent or their stick but never hold, pull, tug or grab, and USA Hockey allows even the fend only on the conditions set out in [§4](#4-body-position-as-defence).

**Under Hockey Canada or in a CARHA adult league,** ⚠️ **hold only a lane you already have, and never lean or body to keep it.** Neither book writes a right to lengthen an opponent's path to the puck, and both make it interference to impede an opponent *"who is not in possession of the puck"* (Hockey Canada 8.3, CARHA 66(a)). CARHA goes further: Rule 49(a) reaches a player who *"does not avert body contact with an opponent"*, so there avoid the contact rather than take it. That is a minor, or a major that rules you off for the rest of the game (30(a)) and is mandatory where intentional contact injures. **And never hold a lane to clear the way for a teammate:** both books bar the side with the puck from running *"deliberate interference for the puck carrier"* (Hockey Canada's Rule 8.3 preamble; CARHA 66(a) Note 2, CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only).

**The NHL, IIHF, PWHL and USA Hockey books say explicitly that the inside lane is legal.** USA Hockey: "A skater is entitled to the ice they occupy so long as they maintain their skating speed and body position between an opponent and the loose puck," and "A skater can use their body position while maintaining their skating lane, to force an opponent to take a less direct route to the puck, so long as they do not use a hand or arm to hold or block the opponent." It states the stationary case separately: "A skater is entitled to stand their ground and is not required to move if an opponent wishes to skate through that area of the ice."

**The NHL attaches no speed condition at all** — Rule 56.1: a player "is allowed the ice he is standing on (body position) and is not required to move in order to let an opponent proceed," and "is always entitled to use his body position to lengthen an opponent's path to the puck, provided his stick is not utilized… his free hand is not used and he does not take advantage of his body position to deliver an otherwise illegal check." USA Hockey's body-checking guidance sets the same limit: "Two skaters who use competitive contact for position as they skate to a loose puck are within their rights to do so, unless one uses their stick, arm, or skates to obstruct the opponent's ability to skate to the puck."

### First to arrive, last to commit

```facts
Convention: Get there early, then decide late — a coaching heuristic in common use, not a law, with no named originator
Action: Arrive early, slow into a wide balanced stance, then read the pressure and chip, reverse, protect or turn up ice
Never: Arrive at speed already committed — a full-speed reach, a spin or a blind bank gives away all your options
Risk: Winning the race and immediately swinging at the puck turns a won race into a turnover
Read: The limit — in your own slot, or with an opponent bearing down on you, there is no beat to wait; get it out
Key: Upright, balanced and looking around is far harder to hurt than stretched out at full extension with your head down
```

The idea is this: **get there early, then decide late.** A player who arrives at speed with their body already committed to one play — a full-speed reach for the puck, a spin, a blind bank — has given away all their options and is easy to defend. A player who arrives early, slows into a wide balanced stance, and *then* reads the pressure can chip, reverse, protect, or turn up ice as the situation dictates.

The failure it is designed to prevent is specific: a player wins the race, immediately swings at the puck, misses it or shoots it into a shin pad, and turns a won race into a turnover. Arriving early and then taking body position and **waiting a beat** is very often the better play, because your support is still arriving and every tenth of a second improves your options. Its limit is equally specific: in your own slot, or with an opponent bearing down on you, there is no beat to wait. Get it out.

This is also the safest way to arrive. A player who is upright, balanced and looking around is far harder to hurt than one stretched out at full extension with their head down.

**Offered as a coaching heuristic, not as a law** — the phrase is in common use, with no named originator behind it.

### Protecting yourself on the boards

```facts
Action: Decide before you get there whether you are taking the puck off the wall, chipping it along, or protecting it
Never: Arrive bent over with your back to the play. Get side-on instead
Never: Turn your back to the checker in this race, and never duck. Puck races to the wall are where checking-from-behind and boarding injuries happen, so everything in Section 6 applies here
Rule: Unnecessary contact into the boards with a player playing an obvious icing or offside puck is boarding — NHL, IIHF and PWHL 41.1 and Hockey Canada 7.2 all write that clause; USA Hockey reaches only the icing half, at 624(d). That protects you, but do not rely on it
Technique: Get your skates parallel to the wall, head up and chin off your chest, and take the contact on your forearm and hip against the boards
Never: Meet the wall on the point of your shoulder. Take it on your forearm and hip instead — and not on the whole shoulder either, if you can help it
```

Everything in Section 6 applies at exactly this moment, because puck races to the wall are where checking-from-behind and boarding injuries happen. The specific points:

- **Decide before you get there** whether you are taking the puck off the wall, chipping it along, or protecting it. Arrive with the decision made.
- **Never arrive bent over with your back to the play.** Get side-on.
- **Remember the icing rule protection.** The NHL, the IIHF, the PWHL and Hockey Canada all class unnecessary contact with a player playing an obvious icing or offside puck, resulting in impact with the boards, as **boarding**. That protects you — but do not rely on it. Get your skates parallel to the wall, head up and chin off your chest, and take the contact on your forearm and hip against the boards — **not the point of your shoulder, not the whole shoulder if you can help it**, and never turn your back to the checker.

### Chipping and the escape

```facts
Technique: The one-touch — arrive and immediately put the puck somewhere useful
Options: Chip it past the defender to your own speed, bank it off the wall to a teammate, or rim it around
Goal: Convert a coin flip into a possession, and take yourself out of the collision
Read: Rim when a checker is sealing you against the wall; reverse when one has over-committed and skated past you
Action: Pick by the checker's momentum, and pick before you arrive
```

The highest-value skill in a 50-50 race is the one-touch: arriving and immediately putting the puck somewhere useful — chipped past the defender to your own speed, banked off the wall to a teammate, or rimmed around. It converts a coin flip into a possession, and it takes you out of the collision.

**Rim and reverse are opposite plays and they are chosen by where the pressure is, so do not treat them as the same escape.** A **rim** sends the puck *onward* around the boards, with the flow of play, and beats a checker who is sealing you against the wall. A **reverse** sends it *back against* the flow, into the ice the checker has just left, and beats one who has over-committed and skated past you. Pick by the checker's momentum, and pick before you arrive.

---

## 11. Shot Blocking

Blocking shots is genuinely useful and genuinely dangerous. Both facts matter.

### Shot Blocking: Technique

```facts
Position: Get close — the closer to the shooter, the more net you take away and the less the puck can accelerate or deviate; that getting close is also safer is coaching craft, not a measured finding
Technique: Stay on your feet where you can — a standing block leaves you in the play
Technique: Present your padded surfaces — shin guards front-on, thigh, hip, body square to the puck
Position: Head up and out of the shot lane, feet on the ice, hands behind your body with the backs of the gloves out — tucked in behind your shin pads is the going-down form, not a standing one
Never: Turn your back to the shot, turn sideways, or lift a leg — all three expose what armour does not cover and blind you to the puck
Never: Lead with your stick — a puck deflected off an outstretched blade goes upward, often into your own face. That mechanism is coaching craft rather than anything measured here
Never: Lunge. Close with your feet instead, because a slide that misses is a defender out of the play
```

**Get close.** The closer you are to the shooter, the more net you take away and the less time the puck has to accelerate or change direction. ⚠️ **Blocking from ten feet away is safer and more effective than blocking from three feet in front of your own goalie**, where a deflection is a goal. The geometry is plain; the "safer" half is coaching craft, since nothing held here measures injury against blocking distance and no governing body's safety material stands behind it.

![](diagram:shot-block-get-close)

**Stay on your feet where you can.** A standing block leaves you in the play, and a puck blocked on your feet often becomes a breakaway going the other way. A dropped block takes you out of the play whether it works or not, and if you miss, the shooter walks around you into a 2-on-1.

**Present your padded surfaces.** Shin guards front-on, thigh, hip. Keep your body square to the puck.

**Protect what is not padded.** Keep your head up and out of the shot lane, keep your feet on the ice, and keep your hands behind your body with the backs of the gloves out. ⚠️ **"Hands behind your shin pads" belongs to a block taken on the ice:** a player on their feet cannot get their hands there without folding forward at the waist, which brings the head down into the low shot lane. Going to the ice is set out in [Defender](../positions/defender.md); this section is written for the block you take standing. Never turn your back to the shot, never turn sideways, never lift a leg — all three expose the parts of you that armour does not cover, and turning away means you cannot see the puck.

**Do not lead with your stick.** A puck deflected off an outstretched blade goes upward, often into your own face — widely taught coaching craft, measured by nothing held here. ⚠️ **Take your stick out of the lane or keep it flat on the ice beside you.**

**Close with your feet, not by lunging.** A lunge is a slide, and a slide that misses is a defender out of the play with no way to recover.

### When *not* to block

**In short:** Commit fully or get out of the lane, and never block the shot on a 2-on-1 or from a screening position.

```facts
Never: Block the shot on a 2-on-1 — your job as the lone defender is the pass, and the goalie has the shooter
Options: A timed slide at the pass, where that is your team's 2-on-1 method, is a different act from going to the ice at the shot — head up either way, and ask your coach how they want it done
Rule: USA Hockey Casebook 639, Situation 4 — a slide that brings an opponent down is tripping "regardless of who gains possession of the puck", and the sole exemption is a knee-drop shot block whose "momentum carries them into the player shooting the puck"
Rule: USA Hockey 639(b) makes "a major penalty plus a game misconduct penalty" mandatory for tripping a referee judges to recklessly endanger an opponent, plus a match to plan on (602(a)) — nobody has to be hurt for it to be called
Rule: Under Hockey Canada a slide that trips the carrier is 8.6 tripping unless it plays the puck first — a minor, an ejection if it injures, no match; one that takes them low can be 8.7 clipping, up to a match if meant to injure
Never: Block from a screening position — a deflection off a body the goalie could not see is one of the most common ways soft goals are conceded, on the coaching account rather than a count
Priority: Either commit fully and take the shot, or get out of the lane — never stand half in the way with your eyes closed
Never: Dive to block a low-danger point shot while an unmarked opponent stands at the goalmouth
Read: Stay on your feet until the puck is actually released — committing early is what a shot fake is designed to draw
Convention: How much your team blocks is a coaching choice — find out which yours plays, and ask your goalie what they want
Key: Blocking puts your shins, feet and hands in the puck's path deliberately — shin guards that cover shin and ankle, gloves not worn through in the palm
```

This is the part that gets skipped, and it is where blocking costs goals.

- **On a 2-on-1.** Your job as the lone defender is the pass, not the shot — the goalie has the shooter. Sliding to block on a 2-on-1 takes you out of the passing lane and turns a manageable situation into a tap-in. Some teams coach a timed slide at the *pass* as their 2-on-1 method, and where that is the system the slide is the plan. It is a different action on a different axis, and nothing here transfers to it ([Defending the Rush](../systems/defending_the_rush.md) sets out both systems). ⚠️ **A misjudged slide is not free:** under USA Hockey a player who leaves their feet and slides into an opponent, bringing them down, is tripping *"regardless of who gains possession of the puck"*, the only exception being *"when a player drops to their knees to block a shot and their momentum carries them into the player shooting the puck, causing them to fall"* (Casebook, Rule 639, Situation 4). 639(b) makes *"a major penalty plus a game misconduct penalty"* mandatory where it recklessly endangers an opponent, with nobody hurt; plan on a match too (602(a)).

  Under Hockey Canada, ⚠️ **a slide that trips the carrier is tripping (8.6) unless you touch the puck before the player**: a minor, a major plus a game misconduct if it injures, and never a match (8.6(c)). One that takes them low can be clipping (8.7): a minor at the floor, a major plus a game misconduct the referee may give on the violence of the impact and must give on an injury, and a match if meant to injure.
- **When you are screening your own goalie.** If your goalie cannot see the puck because you are in front of it, do not add a deflection to the problem. A blocked shot that changes direction off a defender the goalie could not see is one of the most common ways soft goals are conceded — the coaching account, not a counted breakdown. Either commit fully and take the shot, or get out of the lane and let your goalie see it — **the one thing you cannot do is stand half in the way with your eyes closed.**
- **When you would leave a better scoring chance unguarded.** Diving to block a low-danger point shot while an unmarked opponent stands at the goalmouth is a losing trade.
- **When the shooter is faking.** Committing early to a block is exactly what a shot fake is designed to draw. Stay on your feet until the puck is actually released.

**How much your team blocks shots is a coaching choice.** Some systems ask defenders to block aggressively and collapse in front of the goalie; others ask them to take away the lane while staying on their feet and let the goalie see everything. Neither is right in the abstract. Find out which your team plays and, in particular, ask your goalie what they want — see [Goaltender](../positions/goaltender.md) and [On-Ice Communication](../foundation/on_ice_communication.md).

**Blocking puts your body in the puck's path on purpose, so your equipment is being asked to do its actual job.** Shot blockers should have shin guards that actually cover the shin and ankle, and gloves that are not worn through in the palm. See [Equipment](../off-the-ice/equipment.md).

---

## 12. Playing Physical Without Taking Penalties

### The discipline point

**In short:** If you lose position, skate to get it back instead of reaching, and never retaliate after the whistle.

```facts
Key: Most amateur penalties are not dirty — they are late, frustrated or lazy; the coaching account, since no published study codes why a penalty was taken
Rule: Illegal means are acts that establish, maintain or restore body position other than by skating (NHL Rule 56.1)
Action: If you lost position, the only legal fix is your feet — skate more, reach less
Never: Retaliate after the whistle — retaliating converts one penalty into two
Rule: Under USA Hockey Rule 615(a) only, a player who does not retaliate after being struck shall not be penalised for fighting — neither current IIHF edition writes that protection, so do not assume it under the British book
Convention: That the retaliator is the one who gets caught is dressing-room lore — the written standard aims the extra penalty at the instigator
```

**Most amateur penalties are not dirty — they are late, frustrated or lazy.** That is the standard coaching account, and a lens for your own game rather than a measured fact. No published study codes *why* a penalty was taken; what is measured is only that amateur penalties are mostly aggression-type infractions rather than obstruction fouls ([Game Management, "Frustration penalties"](../systems/game_management.md#frustration-penalties)).

- **Late** — you were beaten, so you reached. Hooking, holding, tripping, and interference are overwhelmingly penalties of a player who has lost position. NHL Rule 56.1 names the mechanism precisely: "Illegal means are acts which allow a player or goalkeeper to establish, maintain or restore body position other than by skating." If you lost position, the only legal fix is your feet.
- **Frustrated** — the retaliation after the whistle, the extra cross-check in the scrum, the shove back. USA Hockey tells officials that "any avoidable contact after the whistle shall be penalized strictly, including scrum situations around the goal or along the boards," with an **additional** penalty for whoever instigates. Its Rule 615(a) says *"[a] player who does not retaliate after being struck shall not be assessed a penalty for fighting under this rule."* ⚠️ **That protection is USA Hockey's alone, and a British reader does not have it**: the 2025/26 IIHF book says *"A Player who retaliates to being punched will be assessed at least a minor penalty (“Roughing”) or a major penalty (“Fighting”)"* (46.6), and the 2026/27 book writes no protection either. Under every book, retaliating turns one penalty into two.
- **Lazy** — the stick that stays on an opponent's hip because you cannot be bothered to skate.

**Skate more, reach less** — the fix is unglamorous and entirely within your control. That better conditioning cuts penalty minutes, because a tired player reaches, is reasoning rather than a measured effect. The one study found, by Haugen and colleagues, tested Norway's top men's league every pre-season from 2008 to 2017. It found no link across players between endurance tests and penalty minutes per game (correlations near zero; the largest with any test, bench-press strength, a small **positive** 0.23), and it did not test whether *you* reach more at the end of a long shift. So the instruction stands without a measurement behind it. See [Conditioning and Recovery](../off-the-ice/conditioning_and_recovery.md) and [Mental Game](../off-the-ice/mental_game.md).

### What actually gets called

**In short:** Skate to the puck, not to the player, and keep your hands and forearms out of every check.

```facts
Rule: Stick-to-stick is exempt from hooking in the NHL, the IIHF and the PWHL only, and only while checking (NHL and IIHF 55.1; PWHL 56.1) — USA Hockey 623, Hockey Canada 8.2 and CARHA's Glossary make the opponent's stick hookable (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Rule: The shaft above your upper hand used to hold or hook is a minor under NHL and IIHF 55.2, PWHL 56.2 and Hockey Canada 8.2 — and a double minor at the referee's discretion under CARHA 48(f), which files it under Attempt to Injure rather than hooking (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Rule: USA Hockey's hooking rule reaches only your own stick's blade (623's Note), so the shaft above your upper hand is butt-ending under Rule 606 instead: a major plus a game misconduct, which is what the book prints and not a forecast of the call
Rule: Slashing is a forceful chop on the body, on their stick, or near their hands that is not an attempt to play the puck (NHL and IIHF 61.1, PWHL 63.1) — USA Hockey 634 drops that let-out, so there the force of the chop decides; Hockey Canada 9.3 exempts only a tap on the stick to gain possession
Rule: Cross-checking has no blade-on-ice element outside USA Hockey Rule 609 — it is absent from NHL and IIHF 59.1 and PWHL 60.1
Rule: Charging thresholds differ — IIHF "multiple strides" (Rule 42.1), USA Hockey and CARHA 52(a) more than two, Hockey Canada two or more, the NHL and PWHL 42.1 no stated number
Rule: Interference is not capped at a minor — NHL 56.4, PWHL 57.4 a major on violence, a game misconduct on injury; Hockey Canada 8.3(b) the pair on violence or injury, 8.3(c) a match; CARHA 66(a) the pair, 66(e) a major on injury; if reckless, IIHF 56.4 a major or 56.5 the pair, USA Hockey 602(a) a match
Rule: Kneeing and clipping carry no division clause in any book that writes them and neither is capped at a minor — Hockey Canada 7.8(a) is a double Minor and 7.8(d) bars the misconduct rung, USA Hockey 628(a) is a minor plus a misconduct, and both 628 and 639 are on Rule 411's suspension list
Rule: USA Hockey makes a reckless knee, trip, clip or leg check a mandatory major plus a game misconduct (628(b), 639(b)), and slew footing starts at that pair; if it is reckless plan on a match too, as 602(a) says one "shall be assessed"
```

| Infraction | The test the official applies |
|---|---|
| **Hooking** | Using the stick to restrain. Stick-to-stick is exempt in the NHL, the IIHF and the PWHL only, and only while checking (55.1; PWHL 56.1). |
| **Slashing** | A forceful chop on the body, on their stick, or on or near their hands. The "not an attempt to play the puck" let-out is NHL and IIHF 61.1 and PWHL 63.1 **only** — not USA Hockey 634 or CARHA 80. |
| **Holding** | Using the free hand to hold, pull, tug or grab. You may fend off (NHL and IIHF 56.1); the arm strength move is NHL and IIHF **54.2**, PWHL 55.2 and Hockey Canada 8.1, the same but for pronouns. |
| **Interference** | Impeding an opponent who does **not** have the puck; checking a player who has already released the puck; a "pick" on a player unaware of the hit. |
| **Cross-checking** | The shaft between two hands used forcefully to check an opponent (NHL 59.1). The blade-on-ice element is USA Hockey 609's alone — NHL and IIHF 59.1 and PWHL 60.1 lack it. |
| **Roughing** | A punching or slamming motion, normally at the head or face. USA Hockey 640 reaches wider — a careless hand, arm or stick in a check, and late checks. Hands are no legal check surface. |
| **High-sticking** | Stick contact above an opponent's shoulders. ⚠️ **The waist proviso (NHL 60.1, IIHF 2026/27) is no defence over a crouching opponent — 60.2 writes none.** |
| **Tripping** | Using a stick, knee, foot, arm, hand or elbow to take an opponent's feet away. ⚠️ **No fall is needed under USA Hockey (639 Note 1) or Hockey Canada (8.6(a)); NHL and IIHF unresolved.** |
| **Slew-footing** | A leg or foot taking an opponent's feet from under them (NHL and IIHF 52.1; PWHL 53.1). ⚠️ **Four of the six eject you at the floor; Hockey Canada and CARHA start below that.** |
| **Charging** | Distance travelled, speed built up, or jumping in. ⚠️ **Two strides is already charging under Hockey Canada** — over two in USA Hockey and CARHA, "multiple" in IIHF 42.1, no count in the NHL. |
| **Clipping** | Lowering your body to check at or below the knees (NHL and IIHF 44.1). ⚠️ **Under Hockey Canada stay above the hips (8.7); in the NHL an injury makes it a major plus game misconduct (44.3, 44.5).** |
| **Kneeing** | Knee as the contact point, even an opponent's knee on an otherwise legal hit (Hockey Canada 7.8, from a double minor). ⚠️ **Keep knees in: USA Hockey 628 ejects a reckless knee, plan on a match.** |

**Two more of them catch players who were not trying to foul anybody. Interference** is impeding an opponent who does **not** have the puck, checking a player who has already released it, or setting a *pick* on a player who is unaware of the hit — so skate to the puck, not to the player. **In every book here but one, a minor is the floor rather than the ceiling.** The NHL and PWHL allow a major on the degree of violence, with a game misconduct where that major is for an injury (NHL 56.4–56.5, PWHL 57.4–57.5). CARHA lets the referee go straight to a major plus a game misconduct, mandatory where it injures (66(a), 66(e); CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only). Hockey Canada 8.3(b) reaches that tier on *"the degree of violence of the impact"* with nobody hurt, mandatory where it injures, and 8.3(c) is a match for an attempted or deliberate injury. The IIHF allows a major for interference that recklessly endangers (56.4) and ⚠️ **a major plus a game misconduct with nobody hurt for a *late hit*** (56.1, 56.5).

**USA Hockey caps interference itself at a minor,** ⚠️ **but not what surrounds it.** Rule 625 is not on Rule 411's list, but 602(a)'s match for a player who *"recklessly endangers or attempts to injure any opposing player"* is tied to no named foul. Where checking is barred, a pick thrown as a body check is **604(c)**'s minor and, if reckless, **604(d)**'s mandatory major plus a game misconduct. And a **late hit** there is not interference at all but 640(b), which 640(g) and 640(h) take to a major plus a game misconduct and a match. 640(b) is written *"except Adult Male Classifications"*, so their reach in adult men's hockey is unstated.

**Roughing** is a punching or slamming motion, normally at the head or face — **keep your hands and forearms out of every check.** ⚠️ **Under USA Hockey a careless hand, arm or stick in a check is roughing too.** Note 2 to Rule 640: a roughing penalty *"should also be assessed to a player who uses their hands, arms or stick in a careless or reckless manner while delivering a check, or delivers an avoidable body check to an opponent after the whistle or who is no longer in control of the puck."* Hands and forearms are no legal contact surface anywhere: USA Hockey's Glossary and Declaration of Player Safety say *"The use of the hands, forearm, stick or elbow in delivering a body check is unacceptable and not within the guidelines of a legal body check"*, and the **IIHF glossary** allows a check *"only with the trunk of the body (hips and shoulders)"* ([§5](#what-a-legal-check-is-precisely)).

**Roughing is the low end of what a hand or forearm in a check can cost.** Under USA Hockey, 611(a) elbowing and 604(c) are each a bare minor, against roughing's minor or double minor at 640(a), and head contact starts at 620(a)'s minor plus a misconduct. ⚠️ **But 611(b) and 620(b) both reach a major plus a game misconduct on recklessness, 620(b) on carelessness too.** USA Hockey's adult-male carve-out in 640(b) does not switch the rest of Rule 640 off: 640(c), avoidable contact after the whistle, and 640(d), a check with no effort to gain the puck and the blade above the knees, carry no such exception, and Note 2 directs a roughing penalty with none of its own.

**And in this group too, a minor is usually the floor rather than the ceiling** — [the cross-check ladder](#the-cross-check-problem) is the worked example. A **hooking** major comes with a game misconduct under the NHL, IIHF, PWHL, USA Hockey, Hockey Canada and CARHA books. It needs an **injury** under the NHL (55.3), the PWHL (56.3), Hockey Canada (8.2(b)) and CARHA (64(b)), while under the IIHF (55.3) and USA Hockey (623(b)) reckless endangerment is enough, with nobody hurt. ⚠️ **And the stick-on-stick exemption is a three-book rule, with three books running the other way.** It is the NHL's and the IIHF's 55.1 and the PWHL's 56.1, and it holds only while you are checking the puck carrier. **USA Hockey 623, Hockey Canada 8.2 and CARHA's Glossary make the opponent's stick hookable**, so under those three a stick that pulls or tugs on theirs is the offence itself ([§3](#3-stick-checks--legal-effective-and-under-used)).

**Butt-end hooking: the shaft above your upper hand is a restraining tool in no book.** ⚠️ **The same act is two minutes under the NHL, IIHF, PWHL and Hockey Canada, and an ejection under USA Hockey.** NHL 55.2, IIHF 55.2 and PWHL 56.2 give a minor, and Hockey Canada calls it *"[b]utt-end hooking"*, a hooking minor under 8.2(a). Under USA Hockey it is not hooking but **Rule 606**, butt-ending, whose Note reaches a player who *"uses the shaft of the stick, above the upper hand, to check an opposing player in any manner"* — no jab needed. **606(a) is a major plus a game misconduct**, with a match to plan on if it is reckless (602(a)). The game misconduct costs the team's next game (**404(b)**), and Rule 606 is on Rule 411's progressive-suspension list. CARHA files it under attempt to injure: Rule 48(f), a double minor at the referee's discretion (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only).

**NHL 61.1 writes what is *not* called** as slashing: *non-aggressive* contact to the pants or the front of the shin pads. A **slashing** major, and with it a game misconduct, is reachable with nobody injured in every book read here: NHL 61.3 with 61.5, IIHF 61.3, PWHL 63.3 with 63.5, USA Hockey 634(b) and Hockey Canada 9.3(b). Under **CARHA 80(a)** a slash is a minor or a major at the referee's discretion, and **Rule 30(a)** rules any major off for the rest of the game, its only exception an accidental high stick that injures nobody (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only). ⚠️ **Hockey Canada's slashing rule forecloses the "I was playing the puck" defence in terms.** Its only exemption is for the stick — *"Tapping the stick of the puck-carrier is not considered slashing if it is for the sole purpose of gaining possession of the puck"* — so a chop on the body or hands has no let-out, and 9.3 also penalises a player *"who, on the pretext of playing the puck, makes a wild swing at the puck with the object of intimidating their opponent."*

#### Holding, and the arm you are allowed

```facts
Rule: Holding is the free hand used to hold, pull, tug or grab — you may fend off, not hold (NHL and IIHF 56.1); Hockey Canada 8.1 permits a hand to block an opponent's stick, never to hold it
Rule: USA Hockey writes the fend-off permission in its Casebook only — control the puck (Standard of Play Situation 6), or be reaching for it while physically engaged in a corner or in front of the goal (Situation 7); keep a position legally established, and never push off to create space
Rule: Only Hockey Canada 8.1 and CARHA 63 take holding itself above a minor — a major that ejects in both (8.1(e), CARHA 30(a)), mandatory on injury, and CARHA's open to the referee without one; 8.1(c) bars a match penalty; NHL and IIHF stop at Rule 54, PWHL 55, USA Hockey 622(a) for a plain hold
Rule: CARHA 63(a) leaves a major to the referee for a hold of any kind with nobody hurt, and 63(b) makes the major mandatory on injury — and a CARHA major for holding ends your night under Rule 30(a), plus at least a one-game suspension under Rule 32(d) (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Rule: NHL and PWHL 21.1 and USA Hockey 602(a) need no named foul — 21.1 is a match penalty for deliberately injuring, or attempting to, "in any manner"; 602(a) one for recklessly endangering or attempting to injure; IIHF 21.1 a major plus automatic game misconduct for an endangering act no rule covers
Never: Touch an opponent's facemask — PWHL 51.3 and 51.5, USA Hockey 622(b): a major and game misconduct; in USA Hockey a match if reckless (602(a)); NHL and IIHF 75.2 a minor a referee can take to Rule 21
Rule: Hockey Canada 7.1 and CARHA 48(d) (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues) file a facemask grab under attempt to injure — a double minor at least, and a match where used for advantage, punishment or injury; Hockey Canada adds a major and game misconduct, mandatory on injury, and a match for a violent grab
```

**Use your free arm only as your book allows it — and never to hold.** NHL and IIHF 56.1 let a free hand *"fend off"* an opponent or their stick, but it *"may not be used to hold an opponent's stick or body."* NHL 54.2, IIHF 54.2, PWHL 55.2 and Hockey Canada 8.1 share the strength-move arm word for word: *"A player is permitted to use their arm in a strength move, by blocking their opponent, provided they have body position and are not using their hands in a holding manner, when doing so."* Hockey Canada 8.1 also lets players *"block an opponent's stick with their hands, arms, or body"* — but not hold it. ⚠️ **USA Hockey and CARHA write no strength-move sentence at all.**

**USA Hockey writes its fend-off in the Casebook, in two situations.** Standard of Play **Situation 6** lets a player *"in control of the puck"* use a free hand to *"maintain body position that they legally established"*, but *"if they grab the stick, sweater or arm of the opponent, or use the free arm to push off and create space, a penalty should be assessed."* **Situation 7** needs no puck: where two players are *"physically engaged in the corner or in front of the goal"*, a player may take a hand off the stick *"when reaching for the puck and using the free arm to steer the opponent or maintain position"* — until *"the free arm is used to impede the opponent, pushes off with enough force to knock the opponent off of the puck or create space."* It is written for the corner and the goal front, not open ice. The holding rules themselves are **Rule 54** (NHL and IIHF), **622** (USA Hockey, which names clutching or holding the stick outright), **55** (PWHL) and **8.1** (Hockey Canada).

**What it costs.** Of the NHL, IIHF, PWHL, USA Hockey and Hockey Canada, only Hockey Canada takes holding itself above a minor: **8.1(b)** makes a major plus a game misconduct **mandatory** where holding injures, and **8.1(c)** bars a match. ⚠️ **CARHA reaches the major without an injury:** **Rule 63(a)**: *"A Minor penalty or, at the discretion of the Referee, a Major penalty shall be assessed any player who holds an opponent with the hands, stick or in any other manner"*. **63(b)** makes the major mandatory where holding injures, and a major there ends your night (**Rule 30(a)**) and costs at least the next game (**Rule 32(d)**) (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only).

**In four books** ⚠️ **a rule's own ceiling is not the game's.** **NHL Rule 21.1** imposes a match on *"any player who deliberately attempts to injure or who deliberately injures an opponent in any manner"*, and **USA Hockey 602(a)** on *"any player or team official who recklessly endangers or attempts to injure any opposing player or team official"*. Both are tied to no named foul, so a hold that deliberately injures can be a match under both; the PWHL's 21.1 and CARHA's 48(a) open the same route. Hockey Canada's 8.1(c) closes it. 🇬🇧 **The IIHF writes no match penalty at all:** its Rule 21, *Dangerous Actions*, is a major plus an automatic game misconduct, and reaches only *"an action that is not covered by the playing rules"*.
