**The facemask: never put a hand on an opponent's cage.** ⚠️ **It is a different offence from holding, and no book leaves it unpriced.** USA Hockey **622(b)** is a major plus a game misconduct for a player who *"uses their hand to rub, grab or hold the facemask of an opponent"*, and 622(c) a match that *"may also be assessed"* — plan on it for a reckless grab (602(a)).

**Hockey Canada** files the facemask grab under **Rule 7.1 – Attempt to Injure or Deliberate Injury**: a double minor for a grab of *"the facial protector, helmet, chin strap, or throat protector of an opponent"* with *"a minimal degree of violence"* (7.1(a)). A major plus a game misconduct follows on the degree of violence, **mandatory** where it injures (7.1(b)), and a match for *"a moderate or high degree of violence"*, or one used *"to gain an advantage or to inflict punishment or injury"* (7.1(c)). **The PWHL files it under roughing — a major even *"with a minimal degree of violence"* (51.3), with a game misconduct that *"must also be imposed"* (51.5)** — and its 21.1 match reaches a deliberate attempt to injure. **CARHA** prices it under 48(d), a **double minor**, and a **match** where the player *"uses this as an advantage or to inflict punishment or injury"*. The NHL and IIHF price it at **75.2**, where *"grabbing hold of a face mask"* is an unsportsmanlike minor the referee can take to **Rule 21** — *"[i]f warranted, and specifically when injury results"* in the NHL's words, and in the IIHF's where it is *"serious and severe, based on the degree of force"*.

#### Tripping, and how narrow the relief is

```facts
Rule: Tripping requires no fall under USA Hockey Rule 639 Note 1 ("lose balance or fall") or Hockey Canada 8.6(a); the NHL and IIHF books are unresolved — 57.1 in each is "trip or fall" and 57.2, the clause imposing the minor, "trip and fall" — play it as though the stumble is enough
Rule: A stick through an opponent's skates is tripping exactly as a leg is — 57.1's list names the stick first in both books, the NHL's "stick, knee, foot, arm, hand or elbow" and the IIHF's "stick, or any part of their body"
Rule: Playing the puck first saves the penalty shot, not the minor (NHL 57.3, IIHF 24.8) — USA Hockey's Note to 639(a) relieves only a player "clearly hook-checking or poke-checking the puck", and no book's relief reaches slew-footing
```

**Tripping is the most-called of the seven infractions counted in [§9](#the-cross-check-problem)**, at 16.6% of all calls in that 400-game sample (a ranking of those seven, not the whole book). **The relief you think you have is narrower than it sounds.** ⚠️ **Play it as though the stumble is enough.** USA Hockey's Casebook says the fouled player need not go down (Rule 639, Situation 1: *"No"*), and Hockey Canada 8.6 defines tripping as causing an opponent *"to trip or fall"*.

The NHL and IIHF books leave it unresolved: 57.1 in each bars causing an opponent *"to trip **or** fall"* — the NHL naming *"the stick, knee, foot, arm, hand or elbow"*, the IIHF *"the stick, or any part of their body"* — while 57.2, which imposes the minor, reads *"trip **and** fall"*. ⚠️ **Playing the puck first does not save you the minor in the NHL or the IIHF: it saves the penalty shot only** (NHL 57.3, IIHF 24.8). USA Hockey's relief (the Note to 639(a)) covers only a player *"clearly hook-checking or poke-checking the puck"*, a stick check, not a leg through the skates. Hockey Canada's 8.6 says in terms that it does not reach clipping or slew-footing ([Slew-footing](#slew-footing--the-foul-that-grows-out-of-an-ordinary-battle-position)).

#### The stick above the shoulders

```facts
Rule: The windup and follow-through carve-out is the NHL's, the IIHF's and the PWHL's only (60.1; PWHL 61.1 — all three exclude a wild swing at a bouncing puck) — USA Hockey writes none in its playing rules or its Casebook
Rule: Hockey Canada's follow-through rule is junior and senior only — Interpretation 1 to 9.5(a) is written for those divisions alone. In minor and female hockey the same contact is Rule 7.6 Head Contact, with no stick height in the test
Rule: Under Hockey Canada an accidental follow-through to the head in minor and female hockey is a minor at 7.6(a), a major and game misconduct on violence, mandatory if it injures (7.6(b)), and a match if they could not protect themselves (7.6(c))
Rule: The waist is the struck player's, not yours, and no book's waist floor licenses a stick on a crouching opponent — "above the height of the opponent's waist" is NHL 60.3's wording, IIHF 60.3 has it in the 2026/27 book only, and 60.2 bars contact above the shoulders with no proviso at all
Rule: USA Hockey's playing rule and its own Casebook disagree on what a high stick is — 621's Note requires contact above the opponent's shoulders; Casebook 621 Situation 2 says contact is neither required nor need be above shoulder height, and penalises a stick raised carelessly or to intimidate
Rule: A high stick is not capped at a double minor — IIHF 60.4 reaches a major plus an automatic game misconduct for reckless endangerment, USA Hockey 621(b) the same pair — plan on a 602(a) match too — and NHL 60.4 and PWHL 61.4 match penalties for a deliberate or attempted injury
Rule: Hockey Canada 9.5 is "High-Sticking – Junior/Senior", and it bars its own major and match — 9.5(b) and 9.5(c) send them to 7.7(b) and 7.7(c); minor and female hockey goes to 7.6 Head Contact. Neither 7.6(b) nor 7.7(b) needs an injury; 7.6(c) and 7.7(c) are match penalties
Technique: Take the stick lift under the shaft and low against an opponent bent low over the puck — the shaft is nearer their face than it looks, and a lift that misses rides up into it
Priority: Where USA Hockey's playing rule and its Casebook disagree, take the wider reading — it is the only one safe under both, and a stick landing below the shoulders can still be high-sticking
```

**Keep your stick below every opponent's shoulders, whatever their height.** The NHL book now tests what the stick *touched*: its 60.1 reads *"A 'high stick' is one which contacts an opponent above the shoulders, provided their shoulders are at waist level or higher"*, and 60.3 adds *"(above the height of the opponent's waist)"*. The **IIHF Official Rulebook 2026/27** writes the same, but the **2025/26 (Version 1.1)** book tests a stick *carried* above the opponent's shoulders, with no waist wording anywhere in Rule 60. 🇬🇧 **Which is yours in Britain is worth asking**: the IHUK In-House Rules 2026-27 name *"the latest IIHF Official Rule Book (Version 1.1, published July 2025)"*, which is the 2025/26 book, while the EIHL Casebook names the 2026/27 one. **USA Hockey 621 and Hockey Canada 9.5 write no waist floor at all.**

**And the waist that counts is the waist of the player you hit, not yours.** The **IIHF Situation Handbook 2026/27** answers the bent-over case at **Situation 60.13**, which [§3](#where-the-stick-lift-becomes-a-penalty) sets out: *"The key here is that the opposing player was hit in the neck/face/head with the opponent's stick, at or above the height of the waist of the player being struck."* Under the 2025/26 IIHF book a bent-over opponent is caught more simply still, because Rule 60 there sets no floor. **So a stick below your own waist is a defence in no book** — which is why the [§3](#the-stick-lift) lift on a bent-over opponent goes under the shaft and low, or not at all.

**60.2 is word-for-word identical in the NHL book and in both IIHF editions** — *"Any contact made by a stick on an opponent above the shoulders is prohibited and a minor penalty shall be imposed"* — and it is the clause that actually imposes the minor, so the waist floor is no basis for planning a stick on a crouching opponent. Under USA Hockey, Hockey Canada and the 2025/26 IIHF book there is no waist floor at all. ⚠️ **The one exception is printed inside the definition itself.** NHL and IIHF 60.1 excuse *"accidental contact"* in a normal *"windup or follow through"* of a *"shooting motion"*, and *"accidental contact on the opposing center who is bent over during the course of a 'face-off'"* — confined to accidents and to the centre at a faceoff, not a general licence over a bent-over player. USA Hockey's 621 writes no such sentence, and Hockey Canada runs the other way: its Interpretation 1 to Rule 9.5(a) gives junior and senior players *"a Minor penalty for High-sticking"* for exactly that follow-through.

**Shoulder height.** ⚠️ **No waist floor is not full protection either.** USA Hockey's 621 Note measures against *"the normal height of the opponent's shoulders"*, and Hockey Canada's 9.5 against *"the normal height of the shoulder[s]"*, and neither says whether "normal" means where the shoulders are or where they would be standing upright. USA Hockey's playing rule and its Casebook also differ: 621's Note needs a stick carried above the shoulders **and** contact, while the Casebook (Rule 621, Situation 2) needs no contact — *"[w]henever a player raises their stick above their shoulders in an attempt to intimidate an opponent or is careless in their actions, high sticking must be called."* ⚠️ **Play the wider reading, the only one safe under both:** a stick raised above your shoulders can be a minor with no contact at all, and a stick that catches an opponent *below* their shoulders can still be high-sticking. One of carelessness or intimidation has to be there, so this is not a bar on carrying a stick high in open ice.

**Head contact.** ⚠️ **What reaches a crouching opponent under those two books is head-contact law, and it does not turn on stick height at all.** USA Hockey's Casebook routes a stick that reaches the head *"during the process of delivering a check"* to Rule 620 Head Contact rather than high-sticking (Rule 621, Situation 1), which raises the price. Rule 620 reaches contact *"with any part of the player's body, equipment or stick"*, starts at a minor **plus a misconduct** (620(a)), and makes intentional, careless or reckless contact a mandatory major plus a game misconduct (620(b)). Hockey Canada Rule 7.6 opens *"There is no type of legal contact to the head, face, or neck."* **Its high-sticking rule, 9.5, is titled "High-Sticking – Junior/Senior".** In minor and female hockey, contact above the shoulders falls to 7.6 instead, which is **easier to trigger, not harder**: a minor for accidental contact and a double minor for intentional (7.6(a)), with no injury needed and nothing turning on how high the stick was carried. It also has a higher ceiling through 7.6(b) and (c).

**And a high stick is not capped at a double minor.** IIHF 60.4 reaches a major plus an automatic game misconduct for reckless endangerment, and USA Hockey 621(b) a major plus a game misconduct, and there plan on a match, because Rule 602(a) says one *"shall be assessed"* for reckless endangerment. NHL 60.4 and PWHL 61.4 write a match for a deliberate or attempted injury. ⚠️ **CARHA's high-sticking rule writes no double minor at all** (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only): **62(b)** makes contact above the normal height of an opponent's shoulders a **major** from the first instance, accidental or intentional, *"whether or not injury results"*. Its Note adds a game misconduct where the contact was intentional, or accidental and injuring, and 62(c) is a match. ⚠️ **And 62(a) is a minor with no contact at all**, for a player who *"checks an opponent or intimidates an opponent while carrying their stick above the waist height of their opponent"* — their waist, not yours. CARHA's head-contact Rule 50 sends stick contact to Rule 62.

**The majors.** ⚠️ **Under Hockey Canada the majors live in head-contact law, and neither waits for an injury.** In junior and senior, 9.5(b) and (c) — *"No Major penalty may be assessed for high-sticking"* — send the major and the match to 7.7(b) and (c). In minor and female hockey, where 7.6 *"supersedes any other rules"* bar fighting, the same contact is 7.6(b), a major plus a game misconduct, or 7.6(c), a match. **7.6(b)** allows the major *"based on the degree of violence of the impact"* and makes it compulsory where a head contact worth a minor injures; **7.7(b)** assesses it *"for any Head Contact infraction with a high degree of violence"*, and also where an illegal hit makes a player strike their head on the glass, ice or boards — that limb only where a head injury results. Both (c) clauses are match penalties for a hit to the head of a player *"unable to protect or defend themselves"*, or a deliberate or attempted injury by head contact.

### Slew-footing — the foul that grows out of an ordinary battle position

**In short:** Win the battle with body position, base and skates on the puck, and never put a leg behind an opponent.

```facts
Rule: Slew-footing is a leg or foot used to take an opponent's feet from under them, classically while an arm or elbow pushes their upper body backward at the same time — NHL and IIHF 52.1, PWHL 53.1, USA Hockey 639 Note 4, Hockey Canada 8.8
Rule: The leg alone is slew-footing in every book read here but CARHA, whose 86(b) wants it "from behind" — USA Hockey 639 Note 4 opens "Slew Footing is the act of a player using their leg or foot to knock or kick an opponent's feet from under them", and PWHL 53.1 writes the same, 53.2 a mandatory match
Never: Put a leg behind an opponent in a battle. Keep your leg in front of you instead, and win the puck with your hips and your skates
Rule: NHL Rule 52.2 is a mandatory match penalty with no minor or major tier beneath it — "Any player who is guilty of slew-footing shall be assessed a match penalty"
Rule: IIHF Rule 52.2 — the British book — reads "The Referee shall, at their discretion, assess a major penalty and an automatic game misconduct penalty"; Rule 52 writes no lesser tier, and no match penalty anywhere in the book — 52.3 allows Supplementary Discipline instead
Rule: USA Hockey 639(a) excludes slew footing from the minor and 639(b) sets the floor at a major plus a game misconduct; its Casebook at Rule 639, Situation 5 says "[a] minor penalty is NOT an option for slew footing"
Rule: USA Hockey's floor is not its ceiling either — plan on a match penalty for reckless endangerment: 639(c) writes one "under this rule", and the same Casebook Situation 5 closes that "a match penalty must be considered" any time one of these actions is deemed to recklessly endanger the opponent
Rule: Hockey Canada 8.8(a) is a double minor and 8.8(b) bars a major — but that is a floor, not a ceiling: 8.8(c) reaches a match penalty on the degree of violence, and mandatorily where the foul injures; meant to injure, plan on the match — this guide's reading of Appendix D Note 1
Rule: Hockey Canada defines it widest, reaching a leg that knocks, drags or sweeps the feet away (8.8), and its Interpretation 1 to 8.8(a) urges "[s]erious consideration for a Match penalty" where the offender is in motion
Rule: "I played the puck first" is a defence to slew-footing in none of the six books read here — every puck-first relief there sits inside a tripping rule: USA Hockey 639(a) is written "except slew footing", Hockey Canada 8.6 excludes 8.8 in terms, and NHL and IIHF Rule 52 and PWHL Rule 53 write none
Rule: CARHA files slew-footing inside its tripping rule, as USA Hockey's Rule 639 does — 86(b)'s Note ignores the trip where a player already falling or sliding knocks the puck clear before body contact, which is not the act 86(b) describes (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Rule: CARHA 86(b)'s match for slew-footing is discretionary — its casebook keeps a kicking slew-foot from behind there — but 48(a) makes one mandatory for a deliberate attempt to injure
Never: Move a player who has gone down backward and is not getting up, except to keep their airway clear, and never take their helmet off. Somebody calls for immediate medical help instead
```

**Keep your leg in front of you.** Slew-footing starts from ordinary battle play: [§4](#4-body-position-as-defence) puts your body between an opponent and the puck, and slew-footing is what that becomes when **a leg goes through their skates**, with or without an arm pushing their upper body back. Win the battle with your hips, your base and your skates on the puck — [§7](#7-board-battles) is the whole method — and if you have lost position, get it back with your feet. ⚠️ **And "I played the puck first" is not a defence.** Every puck-first relief in the six books sits inside a *tripping* rule and stops there: USA Hockey's 639(a) is written *"except slew footing"*; Hockey Canada's Rule 8.6 says *"This rule does not apply to actions defined under Rule 8.7 – Clipping or Rule 8.8 – Slew-Footing"*; and NHL and IIHF Rule 52 and the PWHL's Rule 53 write no relief of any kind ([Risk Management](../hockey-iq/risk_management.md#slew-footing-the-one-the-carve-outs-never-reach)).

**The leg alone is enough.** NHL and IIHF Rule 52.1 write two forms — the leg alone, *or* the leg with an arm push — each ending on a player made to *"fall violently to the ice"*. Hockey Canada's Rule 8.8 writes both and widens the verb: a player who *"uses a leg or a foot to knock, drag, or sweep an opposing player's feet from under them"*. PWHL 53.1 writes the first form. USA Hockey's Rule 639 Note 4 opens *"Slew Footing is the act of a player using their leg or foot to knock or kick an opponent's feet from under them"*, and its Casebook (Rule 639, Situation 5) makes the arm push an aggravation, not a requirement — *"especially when used in conjunction with"* a push to the chest. CARHA writes the leg alone but adds *"from behind"* (below).

**What it costs.** **No book prices it at a bare minor.** A player whose feet leave in front of them goes down backward with nothing to break the fall (this guide's reading; the books write *"fall violently to the ice"*), and **four of the six eject you at the floor**:

- **NHL Rule 52.2 — a mandatory match penalty**, no discretion, no lesser tier: *"Any player who is guilty of slew-footing shall be assessed a match penalty."*
- **🇬🇧 IIHF Rule 52.2 — your book in Britain:** *"The Referee **shall**, at their discretion, assess a major penalty and an automatic game misconduct penalty if a Player is guilty of 'slew-footing' on an opposing Player"* — *shall*, where neighbouring rules say *may*, and no lesser tier beneath it. That is an ejection; the IIHF writes no match penalty, and 52.3 allows Supplementary Discipline above it.
- **PWHL Rule 53.2 — a match penalty** for any player *"guilty of slew–footing"*, with no lesser tier.
- **USA Hockey Rule 639** takes it out of the minor: 639(a) covers the tripping family *"except slew footing"*, and 639(b) sets the floor at *"a major plus game misconduct penalty"*. For reckless endangerment plan on a match (639(c), 602(a)). Its Casebook (Rule 639, Situation 5): *"[a] minor penalty is NOT an option for slew footing"*, and ⚠️ **it is stricter than the rule**: *"Any time one of these actions has been deemed to recklessly endanger the opponent, a match penalty must be considered."*
- **Hockey Canada starts small, and the double minor is a floor rather than a ceiling.** 8.8(a) is *"a double Minor penalty"* and 8.8(b) says *"[n]o Major penalty may be assessed for slew-footing"* — but ⚠️ **it goes straight past the major to a match penalty (8.8(c))**, at the referee's discretion on *"the degree of violence of impact (including the impact with the ice, goal, or boards)"*, and **mandatory** where the foul injures. Interpretation 1 to 8.8(a): *"[s]erious consideration for a Match penalty should be paid when an offending player is 'in motion' (skating) while committing this foul."* **Meant to injure, plan on the match even if no one is hurt:** Appendix D Note 1 says one *"shall be assessed for any deliberate attempt to injure"*. That is this guide's reading: the Note cites Rule 7.1, whose match, 7.1(c), reaches only acts *"not otherwise covered within the playing rules"*, and 8.8's own Interpretation 1 weighs intent inside its discretionary match.
- **CARHA Rule 86(b)** — CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only — assesses *"[a]t the discretion of the Referee, a Match penalty … to any player who uses their feet to knock an opponent's skates out from under them with a kicking or leg dragging motion from behind ('slew footing')"*, with no arm push in the sentence and the words *from behind* that the other books' rules do not carry. ⚠️ **It files the foul inside its tripping rule, but the puck-first Note under 86(b)** covers only a player *already falling or sliding along the ice* who knocks the puck away *"prior to making actual body contact"* — not a leg through somebody's skates. **Meant to injure, do not count on the discretion:** 48(a) makes a match mandatory for a deliberate attempt to injure. 48(c) makes one mandatory for a player who *"attempts to kick or deliberately kicks"* an opponent, though CARHA's own casebook rules a kicking slew-foot from behind under 86(b), at the referee's discretion.

**If someone goes down backward and is not getting up,** ⚠️ **nobody moves them except to keep their airway clear, nobody takes the helmet off, and somebody calls for immediate medical help** ([If it happens anyway](#if-it-happens-anyway--a-suspected-neck-injury-on-the-ice)).

### The habits that keep you out of the box

```facts
Action: Blade on the puck, never on the player — USA Hockey penalises a stick in front of the legs to impede them "even if on the ice", with no effort to legally play the puck
Rule: Hands and forearms are no part of a legal body check — USA Hockey's Glossary and Declaration bar "the hands, forearm, stick or elbow in delivering a body check", and the IIHF permits the trunk only; a careless one is roughing under 640 Note 2, but the check was already illegal
Action: Move your feet instead of reaching — a stick that has to reach is a stick that hooks, holds or trips
Priority: Take the puck, not the player — if your first thought is the body, your stick is somewhere it should not be
Never: Stay in a scrum. Play the whistle and then stop instead; retaliating turns their penalty into two
Never: Put a hand on an opponent's cage or facemask — USA Hockey 622(b) is a major plus a game misconduct — plan on a match if reckless (602(a)) — and Hockey Canada files it under Rule 7.1 Attempt to Injure
Rule: The NHL and the IIHF price the facemask grab too, outside Rule 54 — 75.2 in each names "grabbing hold of a face mask" as unsportsmanlike conduct and a minor, and the referee may apply Rule 21 where warranted — the NHL names injury, the IIHF serious force. No book here leaves it unpriced
Rule: The PWHL makes a facemask grab a roughing major even at minimal violence, plus a game misconduct (51.3, 51.5), and 21.1 a match for intent to injure; CARHA 48(d), for CARHA-affiliated adult leagues, a double minor, and a match where used as an advantage or to inflict punishment or injury
Never: Take a penalty out of frustration at a referee — it is the cheapest goal you will ever give up
Action: Know your league's standard — watch how the first five minutes are being called and adjust
```

- **Blade on the puck, never on the player.** Almost every stick penalty starts with the blade off the ice — but a blade *on* the ice is no safe harbour either: USA Hockey's Standard of Play penalises placing the stick in front of an opponent's legs to impede them "even if on the ice", where there is "no effort to legally play the puck".
- **Hit with your trunk, never with your hands or forearms.** A hand or a forearm in a check is not a lighter version of a legal one; it is outside the definition altogether ([What a legal check is, precisely](#what-a-legal-check-is-precisely)), and it can be called roughing, elbowing or head contact.
- **Move your feet instead of reaching.** A stick that has to reach is a stick that hooks, holds or trips. Coaches name reaching as the biggest driver of penalty minutes in amateur hockey — **coaching emphasis, not a counted ranking**.
- **Take the puck, not the player.** In a battle, aim for the puck. If your first thought is the body, your stick is somewhere it should not be.
- **Play the whistle, then stop.** Nothing good happens in a scrum. Both the instigator and the retaliator can be penalised, and retaliating turns their penalty into two.
- **Never put a hand on an opponent's cage or facemask.** It is not a scrum reflex you can afford: ⚠️ **under USA Hockey it is a major plus a game misconduct, under the PWHL a major at the lowest violence, under Hockey Canada and CARHA up to a match** — no book leaves it unpriced ([The facemask](#holding-and-the-arm-you-are-allowed)).
- **Never take a penalty out of frustration at a referee.** It is the cheapest goal you will ever give up.
- **Know your league's standard.** Beer leagues, youth associations and international play call the same rulebook very differently. Watch how the first five minutes are being called and adjust.

### Fighting — and why the amateur rules are much harsher than the NHL's

**In short:** Never throw a punch: keep gloves and helmet on, skate away, and leave breaking it up to the officials.

```facts
Rule: Fighting in the NHL is a five-minute major (Rule 46.14); an instigator takes that major plus an instigating minor and a ten-minute misconduct (46.11); an aggressor a major plus a game misconduct (46.2); outside the periods, a major and game misconduct (46.9), just the major if the other instigated
Rule: The third man into an altercation faces a game misconduct at the referee's discretion — except where a match penalty is being imposed in the original altercation (NHL Rule 46.16)
Rule: USA Hockey counts an attempted punch, contact or not, as fighting — a major plus a game misconduct (Rule 615)
Rule: A player who does not retaliate after being struck shall not be penalised for fighting under USA Hockey Rule 615(a) — neither current IIHF edition writes that protection, and under both, punching back can reach a major for fighting (2025/26 Rule 46.6; 2026/27 Rules 46.1 and 46.12)
Never: Drop your stick or remove your gloves in an altercation you were not part of — a minor under USA Hockey 615(a), plus a game misconduct if you are judged the instigator of a subsequent altercation
Rule: Under IIHF Rule 46 an instigator takes 2+5+game misconduct and the third player in can take a game misconduct — 46.3 and 46.7 in 2025/26, 46.10 and 46.14 in 2026/27. Outside the EIHL (2026/27) the British edition is unresolved, so plan on 2026/27's narrower third-man exception
Rule: In NIHL (all leagues), SNL and BUIHA Varsity, fighting is a five-minute major — and in NIHL, NIHL1 and NIHL2 a fighting major in the last five minutes of regulation or in overtime also carries an automatic game misconduct (In-House Rules, Section 6)
Rule: The British fighting major carries a relief neither IIHF edition writes in those words — in NIHL (all leagues), SNL and BUIHA Varsity an unwilling combatant may be given, at the referee's discretion, "a minor OR a major penalty (2 OR a standalone 5 without a game misconduct)"
Rule: In British women's hockey at every age, adult included, any major brings an automatic game misconduct — a fighting major ends your game (In-House Rule 101's ejection bullet applies "in all cases" and states no age scope)
Key: Junior hockey at every age reads the same way, on In-House Rule 201's scoping of the IIHF junior rules — an inference, since 201 does not name 201.1, so treat a fighting major as ending your game
Key: Rec and beer leagues are commonly harsher again — a general observation about how such leagues are run, not a sourced survey. Read your own league's sheet, the only version that binds you
Rule: CARHA 59 gives a fight's instigator or aggressor a match, may give one to the player who fights back, suspends any fighter (59(d)) and gives a peacemaker a game misconduct (59(b)) (CARHA adult leagues only); Hockey Canada 7.10(e)(ii) also catches the peacemaker
Rule: USA Hockey refuses the peacemaker defence by name: 615(d) is a mandatory game misconduct for the first to intervene in an altercation, and Casebook Situation 24 applies it "[r]egardless of the reason"
Action: If a scrum starts, hands down, gloves on — skate away and let the officials sort it out
```

**Do not throw a punch — even one that misses — keep your gloves and helmet on, skate away, and never go in to break it up.** This document is about legal contact, and fighting is not it. But the moment a battle turns into a shove-fest, the rules that apply are ones most players know only from watching the NHL — and **they are not the rules you play under.** The NHL is the lightest book here; the amateur books are much harsher. Skating away is not passivity: it is the only outcome that leaves you on the ice.

A team that plays hard, wins battles and takes no penalties is far more physically dominant than a team that hits everything and spends nine minutes a game shorthanded. See [Risk Management](../hockey-iq/risk_management.md).

**What it costs.**

- **NHL (Rule 46).** Fighting is a **five-minute major** — 46.14: *"A major penalty shall be imposed on any player who fights."* ⚠️ **Outside the periods — warm-up, intermission, after the buzzer — 46.9 makes it a major plus a game misconduct** (the game misconduct is waived if your opponent was the instigator). An **instigator** *"shall be assessed an instigating minor penalty, a major penalty for fighting **and a ten-minute misconduct**"* (46.11). A second instigator penalty in the same game turns that misconduct into a **game misconduct**, and a third in a season brings one automatically. An **aggressor** — one who *"continues throwing and landing punches"* at an opponent *"no longer in a position to defend himself"* — *"shall be assessed a major penalty for fighting and a game misconduct"* (46.2). ⚠️ **The third man into an altercation faces a game misconduct** *"at the discretion of the Referee"* (46.16), also catching *"subsequent players who elect to intervene"*. Its one exception — *"except when a match penalty is being imposed in the original altercation"* — is one you cannot know about from outside the fight.
- **USA Hockey (Rule 615) is far stricter, and the threshold is much lower.** Its Note defines fighting as *"[a] punch, **or an attempted punch**, thrown by any player in the direction of an opponent, **regardless as to whether contact is made**"*. 615(a) assesses *"a major plus a game misconduct penalty… to any player who engages in fighting"*, so one thrown punch is **an ejection**, plus a further minor if you instigated it, plus the suspensions that follow a game misconduct. A player who *"drops their stick and/or removes their glove(s) during an altercation and is not a participant in the original altercation"* takes a minor, and ⚠️ **a game misconduct is added if the referee deems that player *"the instigator of a subsequent altercation."*** And 615(a) protects the player who does nothing: *"[a] player who does not retaliate after being struck shall not be assessed a penalty for fighting under this rule."*
- **Going in to break it up is not a defence in any of the six books, and three price the peacemaker by name.** ⚠️ **Under USA Hockey the first player in takes a mandatory game misconduct.** 615(d): *"A game misconduct penalty shall be assessed to any player or goalkeeper who is the first to intervene in an altercation then in progress. This penalty is in addition to any other penalty incurred in the same incident."* An *altercation* there is *"[a]ny physical interaction between two or more opposing players where at least one penalty assessed"* (Casebook 615, Situation 21), so a shoving match somebody gets two minutes for is enough. The Casebook refuses the intent defence: *"[r]egardless of the reason, the first player to enter any altercation shall be assessed a game misconduct penalty"* (Situation 24).
- **CARHA and Hockey Canada name the act rather than the order of arrival.** CARHA 59(b) ⚠️ **gives a game misconduct** to *"[a]ny player joining in a fight, acting as a peacemaker, or taking part in another fight during the same stoppage of play"*, and **Hockey Canada 7.10(e)(ii)** to a player who *"joins in a fight or acts as a peacemaker"*. So under those two the fourth player in is caught as squarely as the third, though Hockey Canada's Interpretation 6 applies it *"only to situations where at least one player has been assessed a fighting penalty"*. Its 7.10(f) makes intervening *"as a peacemaker in a fight involving a team official"* a gross misconduct. The NHL's 46.16 and the PWHL's 46.15 write no intent limb; the only peacemaker waiver in any of the six is the 2026/27 IIHF book's (46.14, below). **There is no rule set here in which stepping in to separate them is free.**
- 🇬🇧 **IIHF (Rule 46) — the British base, recently rewritten.** ⚠️ **A player in a fight takes a major plus an automatic game misconduct** (46.1 in 2026/27; the 2025/26 book says a fighter *"may be ejected"*). The other tiers are the same in the 2025/26 and 2026/27 editions; the numbers are not, so quote a number with its edition. An **instigator** takes a minor plus a major for fighting plus an automatic game misconduct (46.3 in 2025/26, 46.10 in 2026/27); an **aggressor** the same (46.4, 46.2); the **third player in** a game misconduct at the referee's discretion (46.7, 46.14).
  - **Under the current book,** ⚠️ **skating in to break it up is more exposed, not less.** The 2025/26 book waives the third-man game misconduct *"except when a game misconduct penalty is being imposed in the original altercation"* (46.7); the 2026/27 book waives it only *"[i]f an aggressor penalty is being imposed in the original altercation"* **and** the referee judges you came in *"with the purpose of intervening to stop the ongoing altercation with an 'unwilling combatant' and not to start a secondary altercation"* (46.14).
  - **Neither edition makes punching back safe.** The 2025/26 book prices a player who retaliates at *"at least a minor penalty ('Roughing') or a major penalty ('Fighting', without a game misconduct penalty)"* where the *"few punches"* of an unwilling combatant are exceeded (46.6). The 2026/27 book drops that, and lets a referee waive only the **game misconduct**, not the fighting major, for a player who *"'defends' themselves against an aggressor or an instigator"*.
  - **Which edition binds you outside the Elite League is not answerable from the British documents.** The EIHL Casebook names the 2026-2027 book; the IHUK In-House Rules 2026-27 adopt *"the latest IIHF Official Rule Book (Version 1.1, published July 2025)"*, which is the 2025/26 book. ⚠️ **Outside the EIHL, take the harsher reading of each point.**
- **The IHUK In-House Rules amend it.** In **NIHL (all leagues), SNL and BUIHA Varsity**, *"[f]ighting shall be penalised with a 5-minute Major Penalty"*, and *“An unwilling combatant may be awarded (at the referee’s discretion) a minor OR a major penalty (2 OR a standalone 5 without a game misconduct)”* — discretionary, so not something to count on. In **NIHL, NIHL1 and NIHL2**, *"Any fighting major assessed in the last 5 minutes of regulation time (including overtime) will also carry an automatic Game Misconduct."* ⚠️ **In British women's hockey at every age, any major already ejects you** (In-House Rule 101, *"in all cases"*), so a fighting major is an ejection there. Read junior hockey the same way, as an inference from In-House Rule 201. See [UK and England Rules](../foundation/uk_rules.md).
- **The Elite League runs its own fighting rule, set out below** — lower on the sheet, not lower in cost, and not your rule unless you are in that league.
- **Rec and beer leagues are commonly harsher again** — many eject and suspend for any fight regardless of who started it. That is a general observation, not a survey: read your own league's sheet.
- **CARHA and Hockey Canada price the punch itself high.** Under CARHA a fight is a match penalty for whoever the referee names as instigator or aggressor, the player who fights back can take one too, and any fighting penalty brings an automatic suspension (59(a), 59(d)). Under Hockey Canada any player who fights takes a major plus a game misconduct (7.10(b)), and a punch to the head short of a fight is head contact (7.6, via 7.9). CARHA can call it head contact as well (50(b)).

### The EIHL's fighting rules — lower on the sheet, not lower in cost

**In short:** The Elite League's lower on-ice fighting penalties are not a lower price, and outside that league they are not your rules.

```facts
Rule: The EIHL Casebook supersedes IIHF Rule 46 for the Elite League alone — its 46.1 penalises willing combatants with "at least a Major Penalty", noting that "for their 2nd Major Penalty an Automatic Game misconduct will be assessed", where IIHF 46.1 attaches that ejection to the first
Rule: The EIHL instigator is "2 minor + 5 major + 10 misconduct" under Casebook 46.10 where the IIHF's is 2+5+game misconduct — rising to "2 minor + 5 major + 20 Game misconduct" in the final five minutes of regulation, at any time in overtime, or on a second instigator penalty in the same game
Rule: Two EIHL provisions pull the other way — Casebook 46.2 imposes a game misconduct on any player assessed a fighting major "after the original altercation has started", waivable only where the opposing player was clearly the instigator of the altercation
Rule: Every EIHL game misconduct assessed under Casebook 46.2 is "reviewed and subject to further game suspensions through the EIHL Department of Player Safety" — the ejection starts a review rather than ending the matter
Rule: The EIHL Casebook keeps a season fighting count outside Rule 46.1, in its Automatic Supplementary Discipline section — a 10th fighting major in the regular season brings an automatic one-game suspension, a 14th "an automatic two game suspension for each additional fighting major"
Rule: An EIHL altercation outside the periods draws "all appropriate penalties that may be imposed at the time" plus automatic supplementary discipline, and its instigator at least one game; the Casebook does not replace IIHF 46.8: plan on its major plus automatic game misconduct if a referee sees it
Rule: An EIHL instigator who is also the aggressor takes both tiers stacked — "an instigating (2) minor penalty, a (5) major penalty for fighting, a (10) ten-minute misconduct (instigator) and a (20) game misconduct penalty (aggressor)". The Casebook prints that rule and the altercation rule both as 46.2
Rule: Casebook 46.13 gives an unsportsmanlike minor to "[a] player who is involved in an altercation and does not have his sweater properly tied down" — involvement, not fighting, is the trigger, and the IIHF book the EIHL otherwise adopts writes no such limb
Never: Read the EIHL's lower on-ice tier as a lower price — what the Casebook takes off the penalty sheet it hands to the Department of Player Safety
```

**This is one British competition, and it is the one on television — which is exactly why its rules get carried into rinks they do not govern.** The **EIHL Casebook 26-27** supersedes IIHF Rule 46 for the Elite League, and on the penalty sheet alone it departs *downward* — but it moves part of the cost off the sheet. **None of it is your rule unless you are in that league.**

- **The on-ice tiers are lower than the IIHF's.** The Casebook's **46.1**: *"Willing combatants will be penalised with at least a Major Penalty ('Fighting'). Note for their 2nd Major Penalty an Automatic Game misconduct will be assessed."* Its **46.10** prices an instigator at *"an instigating minor penalty, a major for fighting and a ten-minute misconduct. (2 minor + 5 major + 10 misconduct)"*, rising to *"2 minor + 5 major + 20 Game misconduct"* in the final five *"minutes of regulation time or at any time in overtime"* or for a second instigator penalty in a game. A player *"deemed to be both the instigator and aggressor"* takes *"an instigating (2) minor penalty, a (5) major penalty for fighting, a (10) ten-minute misconduct (instigator) and a (20) game misconduct penalty (aggressor)"* — stacked, and reviewed by the Department of Player Safety. ⚠️ **The Casebook prints 46.2 twice, for two different rules**, so quote the heading as well as the number.
- **Do not do arithmetic on 46.1's "2nd Major Penalty".** The Casebook does not say whether it counts within the game or across the season, and its separate DOPS season count excludes a non-instigator's fights. ⚠️ **A second fight in the same game ends it either way:** IIHF Rule 20.4 still applies an automatic game misconduct to *"any Player who has been assessed a second major penalty"*.
- **Being second into the night's fighting** ⚠️ **is an ejection there.** **Casebook 46.2 Altercation** imposes *"a game misconduct penalty… on any player who is assessed a major penalty for fighting after the original altercation has started"*, waivable only *"if the opposing player was clearly the instigator of the altercation"*, and *"All fighting penalties that involve a Game Misconduct penalty assessed under this rule will be reviewed and subject to further game suspensions through the EIHL Department of Player Safety."*
- **There is a season ladder you cannot see from the box.** A fighting major *"for the 10th time during the EIHL regular season"* brings an automatic one-game suspension, a 14th *"an automatic two game suspension for each additional fighting major"*, and an instigator above the tenth draws a further game. The count resets for the play-offs. An altercation outside the periods of the game brings *"all appropriate penalties"* plus automatic supplementary discipline, and an instigator there *"shall be suspended for a minimum"* one *"game pending a review of the incident"*. The Casebook does not replace IIHF 46.8, so plan on a major plus an automatic game misconduct if a referee sees it ([the warm-up rules](#before-the-puck-drops--the-warm-up-is-not-outside-the-rules)).
- **And tie your jersey down before you leave the room.** Casebook **46.13 Jerseys** gives *"a minor penalty for Unsportsmanlike Conduct"* to *"[a] player who is involved in an altercation and does not have his sweater properly tied down (the sweater is not removed)"* — being *involved*, not fighting, is the trigger. Removing your jersey before an altercation, wearing a modified one, or fighting and losing it off your torso is that minor **plus a game misconduct**, unless the opponent or a linesman took it off or the tie-down held.

### Leaving the bench or the box during an altercation

**In short:** Stay put until the officials wave you out; leaving the bench or box during an altercation is itself the offence.

```facts
Rule: USA Hockey 629(a) is a major plus a game misconduct to any player who leaves either bench "during an altercation", "in addition to any other penalties" — and a USA Hockey major is served with "no on-ice substitute" permitted (403(a)), so the team kills it shorthanded
Rule: USA Hockey's Casebook puts that on every player who leaves, "not just the first player to do so" (Rule 629, Situation 2) — so "only the first man off the bench gets it" is not USA Hockey's rule
Rule: In USA Hockey Casebook 629, Situation 1 a player whose penalty had expired left the penalty box during an altercation, stayed out of it and skated to their own bench — "Each player would be assessed a major plus game misconduct penalty"
Rule: The IIHF rules that pattern the same way — a player with 0:45 left who leaves the box during a fight and "just skates around the altercation" takes "an additional minor penalty plus a game misconduct" (Situation Handbook 70.1, on Rule 70.4)
Rule: The prohibition is in every book, in wording close but not identical — NHL 70.1 bars leaving "the players' or penalty bench at any time during an altercation or for the purpose of starting an altercation"; IIHF 70.1 names the two places separately, "the Players' bench or penalty box"
Rule: "The first or second player to leave" is the NHL's and the IIHF's PLAYERS'-bench tier (NHL 70.3 and 70.6; IIHF 70.3 and 70.6) — out of the penalty box both books catch every player, and USA Hockey 629(a) catches every player off either bench
Rule: Hockey Canada 10.4(e): "A Game Misconduct penalty will be assessed to any player who leaves the players' or Penalty Benches while a fight is occurring on the ice or for the purposes of starting a fight" — double minor plus game misconduct first off, game misconduct for a later player penalised
Rule: CARHA 70(b) is the first player off either bench, but 70(c) adds an automatic game misconduct to any other player penalised for what they did — including one out of the penalty box, who "would not have to be the first to leave a bench". CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only
Rule: A penalised player who leaves the box during a stoppage and during an altercation takes a minor plus a game misconduct under the NHL (70.4, 70.6) and the IIHF (70.4), and 629(a)'s major plus game misconduct under USA Hockey
Rule: USA Hockey 629(a) treats the altercation as concluded once the referee enters the referee's crease or, with no penalties, signals a faceoff location — until then, leaving the bench is the offence
Rule: The EIHL adds a suspension the IIHF does not, the night’s penalty unchanged — leaving either bench "to join an altercation" draws a 20 or a 2 + 20 and "shall be automatically suspended for the minimum of three" games, "pending a review of the incident" (Casebook, Fines and Suspensions (2))
Never: Leave the bench or the penalty box while an altercation is going on — not to help a teammate, not to change, and not even to skate to your own bench once your penalty has expired
```

**If an altercation starts while you are on the bench or in the box, stay there until the officials wave you out — even if your penalty has just expired.** This is the one place where skating away is itself the offence: the rule is about leaving the bench at all, not what you do once you are out. **It is scoped to no division and no age.** ⚠️ **Wait for the altercation to be over** — USA Hockey 629(a) defines that as *"when the referee enters the referee's crease or, in the absence of penalties, signals a face-off location"* — and do not take the stoppage that released you as permission, because the stoppage may be the fight.

**What it costs.**

- **USA Hockey is the strictest on the night.** Rule 629(a): *"A major plus a game misconduct penalty shall be assessed to any player who leaves the players' bench or the penalty bench during an altercation or for the purpose of starting an altercation. These penalties are in addition to any other penalties that may be assessed during the incident."* The major is served on the ice with *"no on-ice substitute"* (Rule 403(a)), so your team kills it a skater short while you are in the dressing room. Its Casebook catches every player who leaves, *"not just the first player to do so"* (Rule 629, Situation 2). A player whose penalty expired at a stoppage *"because of an altercation"*, who *"does not enter the altercation, but skates to their players' bench"*, takes the same major plus game misconduct: *"No player may leave the players' or penalty bench at any time during an altercation"* (Situation 1). A game misconduct there also costs *"the team's next game"* (404(b)). Only a change already under way is protected: *"Substitutions made prior to the altercation shall not be penalized under this rule provided the players so substituting do not enter the altercation."*
- **The NHL and the IIHF** bar the same act: NHL 70.1, *"No player may leave the players' or penalty bench at any time during an altercation or for the purpose of starting an altercation"*, and the IIHF's 70.1 in the same terms. Both carry an automatic game misconduct for the first **or second** player off the players' bench (70.3, 70.6). ⚠️ **Out of the penalty box they catch everybody:** *"[a]ny penalized player leaving the penalty bench during a stoppage of play and during an altercation shall incur a minor penalty plus a game misconduct penalty"* (NHL 70.4 and 70.6; the IIHF's writes *"penalty box"*), with no first-or-second limit.

  The NHL adds ⚠️ **automatic suspensions of ten games for the first player off and five for the second**, and five for *"[a]ny player who leaves the penalty bench illegally during an altercation and is not the first player to do so"* (70.10). The IIHF's Situation Handbook rules a player who leaves the box during a fight and *"just skates around the altercation"* gets *"an additional minor penalty plus a game misconduct"* (Situation 70.1). 🇬🇧 The IIHF writes no suspension tariff of its own (70.10 refers it to Rule 28), and its 2026/27 book adds that the player's coach *"shall also be subject to potential supplementary discipline"*.
- **The PWHL** writes the NHL's bar at **72.1** and its first-or-second game misconduct at 72.6, but suspends the first player off four games and the second three (72.10); the penalty-box clause matches the NHL's five games.
- 🇬🇧 **The EIHL adds an automatic three-game minimum.** Its *Fines and Suspensions* section: *"A Player who leaves the players’ or penalty bench to join an altercation will receive either a 20 (Game Misconduct) penalty or 2 + 20 (Game Misconduct) penalty and shall be automatically suspended for the minimum of three"* games, *"pending a review of the incident"*. The suspension is scoped to leaving *"to join"* one; the player who leaves and skates around it is still exposed to IIHF 70.4 on the night. Outside the EIHL, nothing British adds a tariff.
- **Hockey Canada** lets a player off the bench only *"for the purposes of a legal line change or at the end of a period"*, and **10.4(e)** opens: *"A Game Misconduct penalty will be assessed to any player who leaves the players' or Penalty Benches while a fight is occurring on the ice or for the purposes of starting a fight"*. That is a **double minor plus a game misconduct** for the first player off (10.4(e)(i)), a game misconduct for a later player who leaves and *"is subsequently assessed a penalty"* (10.4(e)(ii)), and no major (10.4(b)). Its Interpretation 6 gives the first player off who comes from the penalty box *"three minor penalties, plus a Game Misconduct penalty"*. ⚠️ **You do not have to leave at all to be ejected:** Interpretation 8 prices a player who *"intervenes as a peacemaker in the fight while still on the bench"* at a bench minor plus a game misconduct under Rule 8.4, **plus a further game misconduct** under 7.10(e)(ii).
- **CARHA 70(a)**: *"No player may leave the players' bench or penalty bench at any time during a fight on the ice or for the purpose of starting a fight"*. It gives the first player off either bench a double minor plus a game misconduct (70(b)), and an automatic game misconduct to any *other* player off who is assessed *“a Minor, Major, or Misconduct penalty for their actions”*, including from the penalty box (70(c), 70(d)) (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only).

**Keep this rule well away from the one it can be confused with.** A player lying on the ice after a hit is a medical situation, not a penalty-sheet one: ⚠️ **leave them where they are except to keep their airway clear, leave the helmet on and get help** ([If it happens anyway](#if-it-happens-anyway--a-suspected-neck-injury-on-the-ice)).

### Helmets in an altercation — keep yours on, and never touch theirs

**In short:** Do your chin strap up properly before every shift, never take your own helmet off in a scrum, and never touch anyone else's.

```facts
Rule: USA Hockey 615(c) is a match penalty "(for all age classifications)" for deliberately removing your own helmet/facemask "prior to an altercation", and a second one for removing an opponent's "prior to or during" one
Rule: USA Hockey's Casebook applies that match penalty to your own helmet removed during an altercation too (Rule 615, Situation 14), and adds "Fighting penalties do not have to be assessed when applying Rule 615(c)"
Rule: USA Hockey counts as an altercation "[a]ny physical interaction between two or more opposing players where at least one penalty assessed" (Casebook, Rule 615, Situation 21) — a post-whistle shove drawing one minor is enough
Rule: USA Hockey 615(c) also assesses a game misconduct with an express two-game suspension to a player "whose actions during an altercation causes the removal of an opponent's helmet and facemask"
Rule: USA Hockey 615(c) assesses a further game misconduct where your own helmet comes off because it was not worn as designed — Casebook Situation 19 measures a loose chin strap at more than one finger between strap and jaw
Rule: Hockey Canada makes it a Gross Misconduct — ordered to the dressing room and reported to the league (Rule 10.6(f), with 4.9(a)) — to intentionally remove your own or another player's helmet before or during a fight, or to undo either chinstrap so it comes off
Rule: The NHL, the IIHF and the PWHL are lenient — a minor for unsportsmanlike conduct for removing your own helmet before a fight, and no penalty for helmets that come off in the course of it (NHL and PWHL 46.6; IIHF 46.12 in 2025/26, 46.6 in 2026/27)
Rule: The roughing minor for removing an opponent's helmet is the NHL's, the IIHF's and the PWHL's and is written for removal "during play" (NHL 51.1 and 51.2 via Rule 9.6; IIHF 51.1 and 51.2; PWHL the same via its own Rule 9.6) — it is not the rule for a helmet that comes off in a fight
Rule: The PWHL makes a grab at an opponent's helmet or chin strap a roughing major even at a minimal degree of violence, plus a mandatory game misconduct (51.3, 51.5)
Rule: A helmet or chin-strap grab is attempt to injure under Hockey Canada 7.1 and CARHA 48(d) (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues) — a double minor, a match if used for advantage, punishment or injury; Hockey Canada adds a major and game misconduct, mandatory on injury, and a match for a violent grab
Rule: Hockey Canada 10.6(d) reaches the gesture on its own — a Misconduct penalty for a player who "undoes their chinstrap to incite or challenge an opponent but does not actually participate in a fight"
Never: Take your own helmet off, or reach for an opponent's, in an altercation. Gloves on and helmet on instead — skate away and let the officials sort it out
```

Most players know the televised version — helmets come off in NHL fights and nothing happens — and that is three books' rule only: the NHL's, the IIHF's and the PWHL's. Under USA Hockey the same helmet is an ejection, and it does not need a fight.

- **USA Hockey starts at a match penalty, for every age group.** Rule 615(c) assesses one *"(for all age classifications)"* against *"any player who deliberately removes their helmet/facemask prior to an altercation"*, and against *"any player who deliberately removes their opponent's helmet/facemask prior to or during an altercation."* It adds **a game misconduct with an express two-game suspension** for *"any player whose actions during an altercation causes the removal of an opponent's helmet and facemask"*, and a game misconduct for *"any player who doesn't wear their helmet/facemask as designed and the helmet/facemask is removed during an altercation."* A match penalty suspends you from all USA Hockey games and practices until your case has been dealt with (405(c)).
- **Its Casebook** ⚠️ **widens it to ordinary players.** Removing your own helmet *during* an altercation is a match too: *"any time a player involved in that altercation deliberately removes their own helmet, a match penalty must be assessed"*, and *"[f]ighting penalties do not have to be assessed when applying Rule 615(c)"* (Rule 615, Situation 14). And *"an altercation does not need to be considered a fight"*: one penalty is enough (Note to Situations 14-20; Situation 21). **A chin strap that lets the helmet come off is a game misconduct with no deliberate act by anyone** (Situations 12 and 16), and the measure is *"If the space between the player's lower jaw and the chin strap allows for more than one finger to be inserted into that space, then the chin strap is not being properly worn"* (Situation 19). Do it up.
- **Hockey Canada files it under equipment and still ends your night.** **Rule 10.6(f)** is a **Gross Misconduct** for a player who *"intentionally removes their helmet before or during a fight OR undoes their chinstrap before or during a fight and that helmet subsequently comes off"*, and the same for *"another player's"*. That means the dressing room for the rest of the game and a report to the league (4.9(a)), with an automatic suspension on top late in the game (4.9(b)). **10.6(d)** gives a misconduct for undoing your chinstrap *"to incite or challenge an opponent"*. Interpretation 15 lets a genuinely incidental removal go, makes grabbing a helmet a double minor under Rule 7.1, and a **match penalty** under 7.1(c)(iv) where it is used *"to gain an advantage or to inflict punishment"*.
- **The NHL, the IIHF and the PWHL price it lowest.** NHL Rule 46.6, IIHF 46.12 (46.6 in 2026/27) and PWHL 46.6 make removing your own helmet before a fight an unsportsmanlike-conduct minor, and add the sentence behind the folklore: *"Helmets that come off in the course of and resulting from the altercation will not result in a penalty to either player."* That sentence has no counterpart in USA Hockey's book. Separately, removing *an opponent's* helmet **during play** is a roughing minor in all three (51.1, 51.2), ⚠️ **but the PWHL's 51.3 makes a grab at an opponent's helmet or chin strap a roughing major even *"with a minimal degree of violence"*, with a game misconduct (51.5).**
- **Hockey Canada and CARHA price the grab as attempt to injure.** Hockey Canada's **Rule 7.1**: a double minor at minimal violence, a major plus a game misconduct on violence and mandatorily where it injures, and a **match** for moderate or high violence or one used *"to gain an advantage or to inflict punishment or injury"*. **CARHA 48(d)**: a **double minor**, and a **match** where it is used *"as an advantage or to inflict punishment or injury"* (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only).

**None of this is about a player lying on the ice after a hit.** There the instruction is medical: ⚠️ **leave the helmet on, do not move them except to keep their airway clear, and get help** ([If it happens anyway](#if-it-happens-anyway--a-suspected-neck-injury-on-the-ice)).

### Head-butting — and the net-front push that counts as one

**In short:** Keep your head out of theirs — in a scrum, at the net front, after a whistle.

```facts
Rule: Head-butting is no scrum technicality under USA Hockey — 619(a) assesses "a major plus a game misconduct penalty" for it, and for reckless endangerment plan on a match penalty: 619(b) says one "may also be assessed for head-butting", and 602(a) that one "shall be assessed"
Rule: IIHF Rule 47.1 calls head-butting "making deliberate contact, or attempting to make contact, with an opponent by leading with their head and/or helmet, whether contact is made or not" — a double minor for an attempt (47.2); if reckless a referee may give a major and automatic game misconduct (47.3)
Rule: The NHL prices head-butting in three steps — a double minor for an attempt (47.2), a major for a completed head-butt with a mandatory game misconduct (47.3, 47.5), and a match penalty where it injures (47.4)
Rule: Hockey Canada files head-butting under attempt to injure — a double minor at 7.1(a)(iii) for a minimal degree of violence, and a match penalty at 7.1(c)(i) for "[h]ead-butting an opponent with force, or injuring an opponent with this action"
Rule: Hockey Canada writes the scrum case into its head-butting double minor — Interpretation 1 to Rule 7.1(a) gives "helmet-to-helmet or facial-protector-to-facial-protector contact, without the use of excessive force" as its example
Rule: CARHA Rule 48(b) — CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only — is a flat match penalty for head-butting, with no lower tier beneath it, and Rule 34(a) then bars the player from any further games "until their case has been dealt with by the Chairman"
Rule: In the PWHL the attempt alone ends your game — Rule 47.2 is a major for a player who "head–butts or attempts to head-butt an opponent", 47.4 makes the game misconduct mandatory, and 47.3 is a match penalty where the head-butt injures. PWHL only
Rule: EIHL Casebook Rule 47 gives the head-butting double minor two examples of its own, Elite League only — a double minor for "a player who fakes a head butt at an opponent", and for one who "forces his head, helmet or visor and pushes his head with forces against an opponent’s head, helmet, or visor"
Never: Put your head or helmet into an opponent’s at the net front or in a scrum — the EIHL reaches a faked head-butt, and under USA Hockey head-butting is an ejection rather than four minutes
```

The act the books describe is not a fight move but **a push delivered with the head by two players chest to chest**, which is the net front in §9, the corner in §8 and the shove after a whistle. You do not have to swing anything or drop a glove to arrive at it, and in no book is it the cheap option.

**What it costs.**

- **USA Hockey has no low tier at all.** **Rule 619**'s Note defines head-butting as *"the physical use of one's head in the course of delivering a body check (head first) in the chest, head, neck or back area or the physical use of the head to strike an opponent"*, and **619(a)**: *"A major plus a game misconduct penalty shall be assessed for head-butting an opponent."* **619(b)** adds that a match *"may also be assessed"* — plan on it, because Rule 602(a) says one *"shall be assessed"* for reckless endangerment.
- 🇬🇧 **The IIHF's definition does not need contact.** **Rule 47.1**: *"The act of head-butting involves a Player making deliberate contact, or attempting to make contact, with an opponent by leading with their head and/or helmet, whether contact is made or not."* **47.2** is a double minor at the referee's discretion *"to any Player who attempts to 'head-butt' an opponent"*, and **47.3** a major plus an automatic game misconduct where the player *"recklessly endangers their opponent"*, the same in both editions. ⚠️ **For a completed head-butt the IIHF writes no double-minor tier at all**; what the referee has is 47.3 — so do not read the missing double minor as a free pass.
- **The NHL's double minor (47.2) is for an attempt**; a completed head-butt is a major there, with 47.5 making the game misconduct mandatory and 47.4 a match where it injures.
- **Hockey Canada files it under Rule 7.1, Attempt to Injure.** **7.1(a)(iii)** is a **double minor** for a player who *"head-butts or attempts to head-butt an opponent"* *"with a minimal degree of violence and without using such an action to gain an advantage or inflict punishment or injury"*, and **7.1(c)(i)** a **match penalty** for *"[h]ead-butting an opponent with force, or injuring an opponent with this action."* ⚠️ **Its Interpretation 1 to Rule 7.1(a) describes the net front, not a fight:** examples of the double minor *"could include helmet-to-helmet or facial-protector-to-facial-protector contact, without the use of excessive force"*. **The cage-to-cage lean that feels like nothing is the rule's own example.**
- **CARHA writes a flat match.** **Rule 48(b)**: *"Players who engage in head-butting shall be assessed a Match penalty"* — and **Rule 34(a)** bars the player from *"any further games until their case has been dealt with by the Chairman"* (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only).
- **In the PWHL the attempt alone ends your game.** **Rule 47.2**: *"A major penalty shall be imposed on a player who head–butts or attempts to head-butt an opponent"*, and **47.4**: *"When a major penalty is assessed under this rule, a game misconduct penalty must be imposed"*; **47.3** is a match where it injures. **PWHL only** — a British women's player is under IIHF Rule 47.
- **The EIHL prints two examples of a double minor, for the Elite League alone:** *"a player who fakes a head butt at an opponent"*, and *"a player that forces his head, helmet or visor and pushes his head with forces against an opponent's head, helmet, or visor"* (Casebook, Rule 47). The first prices a gesture at four minutes; the second is a scrum. Every other British competition is under IIHF Rule 47 unmodified.

---

## Common Mistakes

- **Assuming your league's contact rules instead of checking them.** Ask before your first shift, get the answer in writing, and check the game in front of you as well as your age group — a checking team playing a non-checking team means nobody checks. **Getting it wrong is not capped at two minutes:** under USA Hockey a reckless check where checking is barred is a major plus a game misconduct, and plan on a match (602(a)). Under Hockey Canada a violent one can eject you, one that injures must, and one meant to injure is a match.

  **Clearing the age line does not settle it.** A USA Hockey association may make any class non-check, and Hockey Canada's ban reaches any division a Member approves. ⚠️ **In Britain no rulebook sets the age** — your competition's Rules of Competition do. **CARHA adult leagues have no checking division at all:** a major there ends your game and brings a suspension, and a boarding double minor counts as two of the three penalties that eject you. Until you have an answer, do not hit anyone.
- **Treating the warm-up as off the record.** Skate it as though the game had started. USA Hockey, Hockey Canada and CARHA all penalise warm-up offences. ⚠️ **The IIHF's Rule 5.6 is not an amnesty** — a warm-up fight is a major plus an automatic game misconduct — and in the EIHL the warm-up is non-contact by rule.
- **Thinking "non-contact" means nobody touches you.** Hold the ice you have taken, keep the puck the only object of any contact, and expect to be leaned on. The books differ. USA Hockey says the game *"can be very physical"*. IIHF women's hockey lets two players chasing the puck push and lean, but never step or glide into her or use the boards on her. Hockey Canada allows only incidental contact in pursuit of the puck. **CARHA is narrowest: you must avert body contact**, and only accidental contact goes unpenalised — except to the head, a minor, or from behind into the boards or net on a player who cannot protect themselves, a match. ⚠️ **A CARHA defender may still *"stand their ground"*, but never lean to keep it.**
- **Skating with your head down.** Get your eyes up before you arrive at the puck — you cannot brace for a hit you cannot see. **Some books protect you less for it:** the IIHF does not penalise a square check with no upward motion on a puck carrier skating head down toward the checker, and the NHL and PWHL weigh your posture. None of that lets a checker target your head. Carrying the puck head down is not **ducking** a check, which has its own penalty.
- **Checking a player who has already passed or shot.** Once the puck has left them, go for the puck and do not finish the hit. **It is not capped at two minutes:** Hockey Canada and the IIHF reach a major plus a game misconduct with nobody hurt. ⚠️ **Under the IIHF, a check on a player who has lost the puck and has not seen you or is not set for it is *"automatically considered reckless"*.**

  **Under USA Hockey.** ⚠️ **The window is the narrowest:** outside adult men's hockey any *avoidable* check after the shot or pass is a minor, and *"It is not acceptable to skate one or two more strides after the puck is gone and then decide to deliver the check."* For a reckless one plan on a match (602(a)).
- **Finishing a check into a player who is facing the wall, or hitting anyone from behind.** When their numbers are toward you, the hit is off — take the puck or the ice. **No book lets you count on two minutes.** The NHL, PWHL and IIHF have no minor, and the IIHF's major, with an automatic game misconduct, is the referee's call, so plan on it. Hockey Canada's and CARHA's lightest add a game misconduct to the minor. USA Hockey's floor is a minor plus a misconduct — ⚠️ **but a forceful hit on a player facing the wall, a reckless hit from behind, or one that sends them head first into the boards or goal frame ejects you, and for a reckless one plan on a match (602(a)).** A match is mandatory under Hockey Canada and CARHA when the player driven into the boards or frame could not protect themselves — and under USA Hockey for excessive force on a defenceless player (608(c)). Under Hockey Canada a hit that reaches the head is head contact (7.6/7.7), and a head-first drive into the boards or frame takes the harsher of 7.5 and 7.6.
- **Assuming checking from behind, head contact, charging and boarding apply only in checking leagues.** They bind every division, yours included. The division scope is in the body-checking rules (Hockey Canada 7.3, USA Hockey 604), not in these four.
- **Getting lower than your opponent to hit them.** Hit from your own height; if you have to drop your body to reach them, take the ice instead. Going in at or below their knees is **clipping** (at the *hips* under Hockey Canada). ⚠️ **From behind, a low hit is priced as checking from behind.** **A knee arriving first is kneeing** — at least a double minor under Hockey Canada, and under USA Hockey at least a minor plus a misconduct, a mandatory major and game misconduct if reckless, with a match to plan on (602(a)).
- **Treating boarding as a two-minute foul — or as something you can only do with your body.** Along the wall, play the puck and ease off as their numbers turn toward you. ⚠️ **Under USA Hockey a trip or push into the boards is boarding too.** No book caps it at a minor, and five of the six reach a match.
- **Treating "the onus is on the checker" as strict liability — in either direction.** Give a turned player the ice, and never turn into a hit. Most boarding rules let the referee weigh whether the opponent made themselves vulnerable. The NHL, IIHF and PWHL waive the penalty when the player turned into the hit on purpose (43.1), but ⚠️ **USA Hockey, Hockey Canada and CARHA write no such waiver** — Hockey Canada gives that exact turn to the checker.
- **Turning your back to the checker when contact is coming near the boards.** **Get skates parallel to the wall and take it on the forearm and hip, head up with your chin off your chest.** Turning your back gives the checker your spine, puts your head first into the boards and makes the contact a hit from behind. Squaring your chest to the boards is the fallback when you cannot turn, not the aim.
- **Arriving at the boards perpendicular, on the point of your shoulder, or upright with your feet together.** Go in on an angle, skates parallel, feet apart and knees bent, forearm and hip to the wall. **Never the point of the shoulder.** Stuck facing the wall? Gloves and stick up and spread on the glass.
- **Angling at the player instead of at the ice.** Skate at the space; skate at the body and a good carrier changes the puck to their far side and goes around you.
- **Angling flat across the ice.** Take the angle so your momentum heads back toward your own net — otherwise one pass leaves you facing the wrong way.
- **Veering into them at the last instant to finish the angle.** Pick the line early, hold it, and add no arm, shoulder or hip — leaving your lane to make contact turns it into a body check.
- **Committing your feet on a poke check.** Extend from the shoulder rather than lunging — poke, recover, stay skating.
- **Never learning the stick lift.** Ask to be shown it, and use it in place of the shove: taken up, under the shaft and below the bottom hand, it is lower-risk than a poke or a check.
- **Taking the lift on an opponent bent low over the puck as if it were any other lift.** Take that one under the shaft, or leave it alone — a miss that catches their face is high-sticking **however low your own hands were**. ⚠️ **It is not capped at the double minor:** the IIHF, USA Hockey (plan on a match for a reckless one, 602(a)) and Hockey Canada reach a major plus a game misconduct with nobody injured, and under CARHA any stick contact above the shoulders is a major.
- **Cross-checking at the net front to create space.** Tie up their stick instead — lift it low or press the blade as the puck arrives, then let go; a press you hold can be called holding. **A cross-check is not capped at two minutes in any book, and no major needs an injury.** Under CARHA one on a goalie in the crease, or one above the shoulders, is a mandatory major plus a game misconduct. ⚠️ **Never put the shaft into their numbers** — in the NHL, IIHF and PWHL that is checking from behind, with no minor at all. "Blade on the ice" is USA Hockey's phrase alone, not a licence.
- **Setting the screen with a skate on the crease line.** Plant a skate's width outside the red line, not merely off the blue paint. The IIHF, USA Hockey, Hockey Canada and CARHA count the line as crease, and it is your teammate's goal that pays. ⚠️ **Outside the crease, contact with the goalie — not the screen itself — is what costs the goal**, and on the line USA Hockey and CARHA take it off on position alone.
- **Bracing against the goalie the way you brace against a defender.** **Give ground the moment a goalie leans into you, then re-set outside the paint and off its red line.** Under NHL and IIHF 69.3 and PWHL 71.3, not giving ground costs the goal or a penalty. ⚠️ **No book caps a deliberate shove into a goalie at a minor:** under USA Hockey it is charging, a reckless one a mandatory major plus a game misconduct with a match to plan on (602(a)). Under Hockey Canada a charge on the goalie, or contact that injures, is a mandatory major plus a game misconduct, and under CARHA so is a charge in the crease or any charge that injures. ⚠️ **Being shoved in is no defence** — only clearly trying to avoid the goalie keeps you out of the box. Never stop a goalie who has left the crease getting back (69.4).
- **Going to the net front as though the screen were only a place to stand.** Know when the puck is about to be released and have your head and face protection right — chin strap done up and a full cage's chin cup on your chin ([Equipment](../off-the-ice/equipment.md)).
- **Clearing the net front by moving a body — and worst of all across your own goalmouth.** **Hold your ground beside the screener instead: walking them out can be called interference in every game.** A body moved across your goalmouth travels sideways or backwards toward a steel frame, and a hit from behind into the frame can end your game. Under USA Hockey, plan on a match if it is reckless (602(a)). ⚠️ **Never take a player's skates off the ice there** — without edges they cannot brace or choose what meets the post.
- **Standing up in a board battle.** Get low — wide base, bent knees, body between opponent and puck. Your base is the part you control.
- **Forgetting your feet exist.** With your stick tied up, kick the puck out — at the puck, never at a player. **No book makes kicking a player a two-minute mistake:** it is a match in the NHL, USA Hockey, Hockey Canada, PWHL and CARHA books, and under the IIHF the referee may give a major and game misconduct.
- **Putting a leg behind an opponent in a battle.** Keep the leg in front of you. **Slew-footing ejects you at the floor in four books**: a mandatory match in the NHL and PWHL, a major plus a game misconduct under the IIHF and USA Hockey. Hockey Canada starts at a double minor and reaches a match, mandatorily if it injures. Meant to injure, plan on a match under Hockey Canada and CARHA too.
- **Pinning the player rather than the puck in a corner or a board battle.** Go for the puck, never holding them against the boards. Where checking is barred, making the *player* the object is an illegal check — under USA Hockey a reckless one is a major plus a game misconduct, with a match to plan on (602(a)). **In IIHF women's hockey the pin itself is barred**, and In-House Rule 101 ejects for any major. In a CARHA adult league a boarding double minor counts as two of the three penalties that eject you.
- **Second man diving into the pile.** Take the space instead — seal the outlet pass and be first to the loose puck.
- **Blocking a shot on a 2-on-1.** Your job is the pass. Let the goalie have the shooter.
- **Blocking a shot from a screening position.** Either commit fully and take the shot, or get out of the lane and let your goalie see it.
- **Turning sideways or lifting a leg to block.** Stay square to the puck with both skates on the ice and your padded surfaces front-on.
- **Bending forward to get your hands behind your shin pads while you are still on your feet.** **On your feet the hands go behind your body, backs of the gloves out**; folding at the waist drops your head into the low shot lane.
- **Winding up from across the ice to deliver a check.** Arrive with speed you already had — if you need extra strides, you do not have the gap, so angle them off. **Treat more than a couple of strides of build-up as charging in any book**, and two as already too many under Hockey Canada. Charging is not capped at a minor anywhere.
- **Checking with your hands or forearms.** Hit with the shoulder or the hip, or do not hit. ⚠️ **A hand or forearm in a check is outside the definition of a legal check altogether.**
- **Assuming a hit is safe because the player was facing you and had not turned.** Hit the trunk and nothing above it — if the only hit available would find their head, take the puck instead. *"There is no clean check to the head or neck"* (IIHF 48.1). ⚠️ **USA Hockey writes no head-down exception at all:** a square hit that finds a bent-over head is your foul. It is at least a minor plus a misconduct, a mandatory major plus a game misconduct if intentional, careless or reckless, a match to plan on if reckless (602(a)), and by its Casebook a mandatory match for excessive force meant to intimidate or punish.
- **Checking a player who is already in a battle with a teammate.** Leave that battle to your teammate and take the space. ⚠️ **USA Hockey makes players engaged for the puck ineligible to be body checked**, along the boards or in front of the goal; a reckless hit is a major plus a game misconduct, with a match to plan on (602(a)). The IIHF treats a check on a vulnerable player as *"automatically considered reckless"*.
- **Retaliating after the whistle.** Take the hit and skate away. USA Hockey rewards *not* retaliating explicitly (Rule 615(a)).
- **Skating in to help a teammate who is already in a scrum.** Hands down, gloves on, stay out. ⚠️ **Coming to break it up is no excuse:** CARHA, Hockey Canada and USA Hockey each write a mandatory game misconduct that reaches a peacemaker, and under the NHL, IIHF and PWHL the third man in can take a game misconduct. A fight is a major plus a game misconduct under USA Hockey, Hockey Canada and the IIHF's current book, and a match for the instigator or aggressor under CARHA, which suspends any fighter. Under USA Hockey an attempted punch is fighting whether or not it lands.
- **Coming off the bench, or out of the penalty box, because it has kicked off.** Stay put until the referee enters the crease or signals a faceoff, whether your penalty is up or not. ⚠️ **Under USA Hockey every player who leaves takes a major plus a game misconduct** — including one whose penalty had just expired.
- **Taking your own helmet off when it gets heated — or reaching for theirs.** Chin strap done up, hands down, skate away. **Under USA Hockey, taking your own helmet off or pulling an opponent's off is a match penalty, *"(for all age classifications)"*, and it does not need a fight.** A chin strap loose enough for more than one finger is a game misconduct if the helmet then comes off.
