- **Getting a hand on an opponent's cage.** Hands down. **Under USA Hockey grabbing a facemask is a major plus a game misconduct, a reckless grab a match to plan on (602(a)).** The other books run from a minor to a match.
- **Leaning cage-to-cage in a scrum, and thinking it is not head-butting because nobody swung.** Back your head away — Hockey Canada uses helmet-to-helmet contact as its example of the double minor. ⚠️ **Under USA Hockey there is no four-minute option:** head-butting is a major plus a game misconduct, and for a reckless one plan on a match (602(a)).
- **Sending a player back out after a head knock because they were not knocked out — or once they say the headache has gone.** Suspected concussion means off the ice, no return that day, **and a medical professional before they play again**, *"even if the symptoms resolve"*.
- **Letting them drive home, or sending them home on their own.** Somebody else drives, and a responsible adult stays with them — CRT6 says no driving *"until cleared to do so by a healthcare professional"*.
- **Helping up a player who may have hurt their neck.** Do not move them except to keep their airway clear, and do not let them move. Call for medical help, check airway and breathing, keep them warm and still, and leave the helmet on ([If it happens anyway](#if-it-happens-anyway--a-suspected-neck-injury-on-the-ice)).

## Check yourself

*Answer each question before you go on — a real attempt, not a guess. Then go
and check, because the checking is the part that does the work. Producing an
answer and getting it wrong is worth more than recognising a right one, but only
when the right answer follows: the section named with each question, and the Key
Takeaways after them, are where you get it. Where the answer lives in another
document, that is named too.*

1. What is the difference between body contact and body checking, which of the two
   is legal at every age in almost every league, and what does "non-check" therefore not
   mean? *(§1. Which Contact Rules Are You Actually Playing Under?)*

2. You play in Britain and want to know the age at which body checking becomes
   legal. What do the IIHF Rule Book and the In-House Rules say about it, and
   where does the answer actually live? *(§If you play in Britain)*

3. You are closing on a puck carrier heading for the wall. Name the four things
   you are managing in the angle, and say why you must not cross over in the last
   few strides. *(§2. Angling — the four things you are managing)*

4. Contact is coming and you are near the boards. Turning your back feels like
   protecting yourself — why is it the opposite, and what are the three
   cases you should be aiming for instead, best first? *(§6. Do not turn your back
   to an oncoming checker near the boards)*

5. You want to take away a net-front player's tip and rebound. Why is a
   cross-check the wrong tool even with your blade on the ice — and where exactly
   on their stick must a lift be taken instead, and why there? *(§The cross-check
   problem, and §3. The stick lift)*

6. A teammate is already tangled with an opponent along the wall and you arrive
   third. Why is that hit not available to you? Give the route each of the two
   books takes to get there. *(§Boarding, and the player who cannot see you coming)*

7. A player is down after a hit and may have hurt their neck. What is the most
   common and most damaging thing bystanders do, and what are your actual steps in
   order? *(§If it happens anyway — a suspected neck injury on the ice)*

8. Your league does not permit checking and you still want to defend properly.
   What does Rule 56.1 entitle you to do with your body, and what are the two
   things that turn all of it into a penalty? *(§4. Body Position as Defence)*

9. "The onus is on the checker." Which rulebooks actually state that onus for
   checking from behind, and which well-known one does not state it at all? Then
   name the one thing the sentence does *not* mean for the player delivering the
   hit, and the one thing it does not mean for the player receiving it — and say
   why neither changes what you should do.
   *(§Checking from behind, and what the onus does and does not mean, and §6. Do not turn your back to an
   oncoming checker near the boards)*

10. Your penalty has just expired, play stops because a fight has broken out,
    and you step out of the box and skate to your own bench without going near
    it. What does USA Hockey assess you, and what has to happen before leaving
    stops being the offence? Then say how the answer differs in the other
    books. *(§Leaving the bench or the box during an altercation)*

11. You are setting a screen in front of the net. Say where your skates go and
    why the blue paint is not the boundary; say what a skate on the red crease
    line costs you under USA Hockey, Hockey Canada and CARHA, and which book answers
    the same play in two directions. Then say what you must do the moment the
    goalie leans into you to establish position, and what holding your
    ground costs. Then say which of the six books writes no bare-minor rung
    for a braced shove into a goalie at all, and how far the "privileged
    area" that rule is keyed to actually reaches — whether it reaches the corners, and what being outside it does *not* mean for the goalie standing there. Then say which books stop leaving the shove to the referee once the contact becomes a charge, and what each one needs first — and which also does so for a cross-check in the crease. Last, name the one screening act that is a minor with no contact and no crease involved at all. *(§9. Net-Front Battles —
    Attacking the net front; Where you may stand when you screen;
    What it costs to touch the goalie)*

---

## Key Takeaways

1. **Before your first shift — and for the game in front of you, not just your age group — find out whether body checking is allowed, and get it in writing.** ⚠️ **Until you have it, do not hit anyone.** Where it is banned, a check can end your game. Hands off opponents in the warm-up too.
2. **When you close on a puck carrier, angle them: pick your line early, take away the middle, keep your momentum heading back toward your own net, and arrive stick on puck, adding nothing.** A late veer, or an arm, shoulder or hip added once you arrive, turns the angle into a body check.
3. **To take the puck off a carrier, lift their stick from under the shaft, well below their bottom hand — never chop at their hands or body.** A chop at the hands can get you thrown out with nobody hurt, in every book. Against a player bent low over the puck, lift under the shaft or leave it.
4. **In front of an opponent, hold that ice and lengthen their route to the puck, stick down and free hand off them.** Never use the spot as cover for a hit. **Under Hockey Canada or in a CARHA adult league, never lean or body to keep it — and in any game, never walk a net-front screener out.**
5. **Where checking is allowed and you have the gap, hit trunk to trunk with shoulder or hip — above the knees (the hips under Hockey Canada), at or below the shoulders, skates on the ice.** Need extra strides or a jump? Angle instead. ⚠️ **Never drop under them or lead with your knee — a penalty in every division.**
6. **When their numbers are toward you, the puck has gone, or only their head is open, the hit is off — take the puck.** ⚠️ **A hit that stays on the back ends your game under the NHL, PWHL, Hockey Canada and CARHA; under the IIHF, plan on it, and under USA Hockey, when forceful at the boards, head first or reckless.**
7. **When contact is coming near the wall: head up, chin off your chest, skates parallel to the boards, forearm and hip to it — never turn your back to the checker, never duck.** A turned back makes it a hit from behind; a tucked chin is how a head-first collision breaks a neck, even at walking speed.
8. **If a player may have a head or neck injury, do not help them up: keep them still except to clear the airway, leave the helmet on, and call for help.** ⚠️ **After any head knock: off for the day, a medical professional before playing again, even if symptoms clear** — and someone else drives them home. Neck pain means an ambulance.
9. **In a board or corner battle, arrive with your body between the opponent and the puck, get low and wide, and play the puck, not the player.** The second player in stays out of the pile. Kick the puck free, never a player. ⚠️ **Keep your leg in front of you: slew-footing ejects you in four of the six books.**
10. **Defending the net front, stand beside the screener and play their stick as the puck arrives: lift it low or press the blade, then let go.** Never cross-check them, walk them out or move a body across your goalmouth. ⚠️ **Head up, chin off your chest: the goal post is in the same head-first injury mechanism as the boards.**
11. **When you screen, plant a skate's width outside the crease's red line, hands and blade down, and give ground the moment a goalie leans into you.** ⚠️ **Never touch a goalie, in the crease or out — it can cost the goal, and a charge on one ends your game under Hockey Canada, under CARHA in the crease or if it injures, and under USA Hockey if reckless, and can in every book.**
12. **On a loose puck, win the inside lane, then get there early and decide late.** Half a step behind but between the opponent and the puck usually beats first there; hold the lane with no hands. Rim against a checker sealing you on the wall; reverse past one who has overskated. In your own slot, get it out.
13. **When you block a shot, get close to the shooter, stay on your feet and square to it, hands behind your body and head out of the lane — but not on a 2-on-1, where your job is the pass.** How much your team blocks is a coaching choice, so ask your goalie.
14. **When you lose position, skate back into it — never reach, hook or hold. When it boils over: gloves and helmet on, hands off their cage, head out of theirs, skate away.** Never go in to help or break it up. On the bench or in the box, stay put until the officials wave you out.

---

*Sources — retrieved 27 July 2026:*

*Rulebooks: [NHL Official Rules 2025-2026 (PDF)](https://media.d3.nhle.com/image/private/t_document/prd/slwjuaqwmuvj5bkplixo.pdf) — **Rule 21 Match Penalties** (21.1, *"A match penalty shall be imposed on any player who deliberately attempts to injure or who deliberately injures an opponent in any manner"* — tied to no named foul, read 19 September 2026), **Rule 54 Holding** (54.1–54.4, which write a minor, a penalty shot and an awarded goal and no tier above them, read 19 September 2026), Rule 41 Boarding, 42 Charging, 43 Checking from Behind, 44 Clipping, 45 Elbowing, **46 Fighting** (46.2's aggressor definition and tier; 46.11's instigator tier — *"an instigating minor penalty, a major penalty for fighting and a ten-minute misconduct"* — with its escalation to a game misconduct for a second instigator penalty in the same game and a third in one regular season; 46.14's major; and 46.16's third-man game misconduct, including its exception, *"except when a match penalty is being imposed in the original altercation"*, all read 31 August 2026), 48 Illegal Check to the Head, **49 Kicking** (49.1's definition, 49.2's four kicked-puck clarifiers, and 49.3's match penalty), 51 Roughing, **52 Slew-footing** (52.1's two-limbed definition, ending on a player made to *"fall violently to the ice"*, and 52.2's flat, mandatory match penalty with no tier beneath it), 55 Hooking (with **55.3**, whose major requires that the player *"injures an opponent"*, and 55.4), 56 Interference, **57 Tripping** (57.1's definition — a stick, knee, foot, arm, hand or elbow placed *"in such a manner that causes his opponent to trip or fall"* — and 57.3's puck-first carve-out, which converts the penalty shot to a minor and does not remove the minor), **59 Cross-checking** (59.1's definition; 59.2 and 59.3, whose minor and major both turn on *"the severity of the contact"* and not on injury; 59.4's match penalty where the player *"attempted to or deliberately injured his opponent by cross-checking"*; and 59.5, *"When a major penalty is assessed for cross-checking, an automatic game misconduct penalty shall be imposed"*, all read 30 August 2026), 60 High-sticking, 61 Slashing (with **61.3**, whose major turns on *"the severity of the contact"* and not on injury, and 61.5), 63.2(i) Delaying the Game (freezing the puck), **69 Interference on the Goalkeeper** — 69.1, the premise that position inside or outside the crease does not by itself decide the goal, its clause (1) reaching an attacker who *"either by his positioning or by contact, impairs the goalkeeper's ability to move freely within his crease or defend his goal"*, and its permission for incidental contact *"initiated outside of the goal crease, provided the attacking player has made a reasonable effort to avoid such contact"*; 69.3 on contact inside the crease; **69.4**, whose separate permission is keyed to the goalkeeper being *"in the act of playing the puck outside his goal crease"*; and **69.6**, whose neutral-zone face-off follows only a goal already disallowed — with **Reference Table 14 — Interference on the Goalkeeper Situations**, read 18 September 2026: **5 C** and **5 E**, each *"Goal is allowed"*, and **6 D**, *"Goal is disallowed. A minor penalty is not assessed (loss of goal only)"*, **70 Leaving the Bench** — 70.1's flat bar, *"No player may leave the players' or penalty bench at any time during an altercation or for the purpose of starting an altercation"*; 70.3 and 70.6, whose game misconduct is written for *"the first or second player to leave"*; 70.4 and 70.6's minor plus game misconduct for *"[a]ny penalized player leaving the penalty bench during a stoppage of play and during an altercation"*; and 70.10's automatic ten-game and five-game suspensions for the first and second player off, all read 1 September 2026 in the 2025-2026 edition and found word for word identical in the 2024-2025 one · [NHL Official Rules 2024-2025 (PDF)](https://media.d3.nhle.com/image/private/t_document/prd/yikcdsxofkmgsrhjl3di.pdf) — **Rule 60 High-sticking only**, read against the 2025-2026 text on 31 August 2026, which is what dates the high-sticking rewrite stated above. Its **60.1** reads *"A 'high stick' is one which is carried above the height of the opponent's shoulders"* and its **60.3** *"When a player carries or holds any part of his stick above the shoulders and makes contact with his opponent's neck, face or head so that injury results"* — so the waist floor is absent from both clauses in the 2024-2025 edition and present in both in the 2025-2026 one, and the 2025/26 IIHF wording quoted above is word for word the NHL's superseded 60.1. **60.2 is identical in the two editions.** No other rule was compared across them, and no NHL edition earlier than 2024-2025 was consulted, so *when* before 2025-26 the older wording had stood is not established here. The NHL book carries no summary of rule changes and dates no rule · [USA Hockey 2025-29 Official Playing Rules](https://cdn2.sportngin.com/attachments/document/945a-3442848/2025-29_USAH_Playing_Rules.pdf) — its two **Standard of Play and Rule Emphasis** Summaries, read 7 September 2026 — Body Checking (*"A player is entitled to use proper body position and competitive contact in all age classifications"*) and Restraining Fouls (*"Players are entitled to the ice they occupy as long as they are able to maintain their own foot speed and body position between opponent and puck"*), neither of which announces an age or category scope, against the **Note to Rule 604(c)** (*"simply maintaining established body position … in front of the goal or along the boards"*), which sits under a rule scoped to the Competitive Contact categories, and the **Note to Rule 625 Interference**, whose title announces none; the Declaration of Player Safety glossary (Body Checking, Competitive Contact, Angling) and its **Late Avoidable Body Check** paragraph, which is where *"[t]he concept of 'finishing the check' is an unacceptable action as it is one that is meant to intimidate or punish the opponent with no intent to gain possession of the puck"* is written — the book's own **Glossary** repeats it under *"Late Body Check"* in the wider form, *"no intent, or possibility, to gain possession"*; **Rule 603 Boarding**, whose Note states the onus flat — *"The onus is on the player delivering the check to avoid placing a vulnerable or defenseless opponent in danger"*, with no balancing sentence after it, unlike NHL 41.1, IIHF 41.1 and Hockey Canada 7.2, read 31 August 2026, and **read again in full on 1 September 2026** for two clauses not previously carried: the Note's **first** paragraph, which defines boarding as the action where a player *"pushes, trips or body checks an opponent causing them to go dangerously into the boards"* — the only one of the four books to name a trip, against *"checks or pushes"* in NHL 41.1, IIHF 41.1 and Hockey Canada 7.2 — and the sentence immediately after 603(a), *"'Rolling' an opponent along the boards where they are attempting to go through too small an opening is not considered boarding"*, which likewise has no counterpart in the other three books; **Rule 602(a) Attempt to Injure or Recklessly Endanger an Opponent** ("A match penalty shall be assessed to any player or team official who recklessly endangers or attempts to injure any opposing player or team official" — tied to no named foul at all); **Rule 640 Unnecessary Roughness (Roughing)** — **640(b)**, the late-hit minor written *"(except Adult Male Classifications)"*; **640 Note 2**, which directs a roughing penalty for *"an avoidable body check to an opponent after the whistle or who is no longer in control of the puck"* with no classification exception; and **640(g)** and **640(h)**, the major plus game misconduct and the match penalty for reckless endangerment, both of which reach a late hit only through sub-section (b), while (d) and (e) carry no classification limit; **Rule 629 Leaving the Players' Bench or Penalty Bench** — 629(a) read in full on 1 September 2026, including the proviso that *"[s]ubstitutions made prior to the altercation shall not be penalized under this rule provided the players so substituting do not enter the altercation"* and the clause concluding an altercation *"when the referee enters the referee's crease or, in the absence of penalties, signals a face-off location"*; **Rule 404(b)**, whose game misconduct suspends the player for *"the team's next game"*; **Rule 411 Progressive Suspensions** — 411(a) read in full on 31 August 2026, including its three-, four- and five-major rungs and the seventeen-rule aggressive-infractions list, which **runs across a page break and is complete only across both pages**, its last two entries being Rule 639 and Rule 640; **Rule 615 Fighting** — 615(a), whose third paragraph reads *"A minor, double minor or major plus game misconduct penalty, at the discretion of the Referee, shall be assessed to any player who, having been struck, continues the altercation by retaliating. A player who does not retaliate after being struck shall not be assessed a penalty for fighting under this rule"* — note the protection is written *"under this rule"* and so does not reach roughing or misconduct; **Rule 617(c)(1)**, which disallows a goal where *"[a]n attacking player kicks the puck directly into the goal or the kicked puck deflects off any player, including goalkeeper, prior to entering the goal"*; **Rule 627 Kicking Opponent or Puck** — 627(a) the push-off, 627(b) the match penalty, 627(c) the kicked-puck goal, which writes no stick-versus-body distinction; all read 30 August 2026. Rule 604 Body Checking (604(a)–(e) read in full on 10 September 2026, including the sentence that follows 604(a)'s classification list — *"A local governing body may prohibit body checking in any classification and is encouraged to provide Competitive Contact options at all levels of recreational play"* — which is the basis for saying an age class nominally permitting checking does not settle whether your league does; the **Glossary**, under *Age Classifications*, **Note 3** — *"Adult classifications shall include Adult non-check, Adult U.S., Adult Elite and Adult Women, and shall be governed by these rules, except where otherwise noted"* — read 10 September 2026, which is the whole of the basis for §1's statement that an adult classification is not a checking classification by default; **the book gives no count of how much adult hockey is played in each of the four, and none is claimed here**; ⚠️ **one further USA Hockey body-checking prohibition sits outside Rule 604's own structure**, in the book's *Disabled Hockey* section and so not reachable by reading Rule 604 alone — *"Body checking is prohibited during all Blind Hockey games. Body checking shall be penalized under the guidelines within Rule 604 in the Official Rules of Ice Hockey."* It writes **no age or classification limb**, and the same section provides that a Blind Hockey roster *"can comprise all youth-aged players (players under 18), all adult-aged players (18 and older), or mixed youth and adult-aged players"*, which is the whole of the basis for saying it reaches adult rosters that 604(a)'s list does not; read 2 September 2026. ⚠️ **Only the Blind Hockey body-checking provision was read** — the Sled Hockey rules carry a separate Non-Contact Player provision that this document does not describe), 608 Checking from Behind, **609 Cross-Checking** (the Note's *"with no portion of the stick on the ice"* wording, and 609(b) and 609(c) — the major plus game misconduct that *"shall be assessed"* and the match penalty that *"may also be assessed"*, both for recklessly endangering an opponent by cross-checking, both read 30 August 2026), 610(a) Delaying the Game (deliberately freezing the puck along the boards), 620 Head Contact, **621 High Sticks** (621(b), the major plus game misconduct that *"shall be assessed to any player who recklessly endangers an opponent as a result of high sticking"*, and 621(c), the match penalty available for the same, both read 1 September 2026; the 621 Note measures against *"the normal height of the opponent's shoulders"*, and a whole-book search, including across line breaks, of the playing rules **and** the Casebook on 1 September 2026 finds *"normal"* defined in neither), **639 Tripping/Clipping/Leg Checking/Slew Footing** (Note 4's definition; 639(a), which writes the minor for the family *"except slew footing"*; 639(b), whose second sentence reads *"The minimum penalty to be assessed for slew footing is a major plus game misconduct penalty"*; and 639(c), the match penalty for reckless endangerment available *"under this rule"*), 623 Hooking (with **623(b)** and **623(c)**, the major plus game misconduct that *"shall be assessed"* for recklessly endangering an opponent by hooking, and the match penalty above it), 625(b) (its first paragraph, under which a face-off *"shall be conducted at the nearest neutral zone face-off spot any time an attacking player stands, holds their stick, or skates through the goal crease provided the puck is in the attacking zone, the attacking team has possession of the puck and the goalkeeper is in contact with the crease"*, read 18 September 2026; the position bar on a goal with an attacking player in the goal crease, and the push-in relief in its third paragraph — "if the attacking player has been physically interfered with by the actions of a defending player that causes them to be in the goal crease, play shall not be stopped and any legal goal scored shall be allowed" — read 31 August 2026; the book was read in full for a screening or goaltender-vision provision and has none), **607 Charging** — **607(c)**, *"A minor plus a misconduct or a major plus a game misconduct penalty shall be assessed to a player who body checks or charges a goalkeeper while the goalkeeper is within the goal crease or privileged area"*; **607(e)**, *"A match penalty for reckless endangerment may also be assessed for charging"*; **607(b)**, *"A major penalty plus game misconduct penalty shall be assessed to any player who recklessly endangers an opponent as a result of charging"*; **607(d) Note 1**, which routes *"any accidental or unavoidable contact"* with the goalkeeper to interference and *"[a]ny deliberate body contact or check that is delivered to the goalkeeper"* to charging; and **607(d) Note 2**, defining the goalkeeper's *"Privileged Area"* as *"an area outlined by connecting the end zone face-off spots with an imaginary line and imaginary lines from each face-off spot running perpendicular to the end boards"*, all read 18 September 2026, 634 Slashing (its Note — *"Any forceful chop with the stick on an opponent's body or opponent's stick, on or near the opponent's hands, shall be considered slashing"* — carrying no *"attempt to play the puck"* let-out, with **634(b)** and **634(c)** matching 623(b) and (c)) · [USA Hockey Standard of Play — Body Checking 2025-29](https://cdn1.sportngin.com/attachments/document/ef49-3383370/USAH_Playing_Rules_SOP_Body_Checking_2025-29.pdf) — its **CROSS-CHECKING** heading, read 30 August 2026, whose two worked examples are the net-front shove *"in a dangerous manner to create space"* and the same shove on a player *"protecting the puck along the boards"*; the playing rule at 609 carries none. Also its **ROUGHING**, **HEAD CONTACT** and **CHARGING** headings, quoted above · [USA Hockey Official Rules and Casebook 2025-29 (PDF)](https://cdn3.sportngin.com/attachments/document/4f1a-3442850/2025-29_USAH_Playing_Rules___Casebook.pdf) — **Rule 604, Situation 1**, read 10 September 2026 — a non-league fixture where *"[b]oth coaches agree to play the game under the Body Checking category rules"*, answered *"No. Rule Reference 604(b)"*, with *"Coaches do not have the authority to decide what rules they choose to play under on any given day"* and the direction that, absent specific information from *"a USA Hockey Local Supervisor or Registrar"*, the playing rules govern; **Standard of Play, Situation 10**, on when a body check may still be finished after the puck has gone: a prior effort to gain possession, *"an arm's length plus stick length"* at the instant the puck was dumped, the check already *"in the process of being delivered"*, and minimised force — offered there as a *"general rule of thumb"*, and followed by a second and separate route to legal contact where the defender *"maintains their normal skating lane and reasonable foot speed"* — with *"one or two more strides after the puck is gone"* ruled out in terms — corroborated at **Rule 640, Situation 3** and **Standard of Play, Situation 23**, which state the same test as avoidability and give *"finishing the check"* *"no place in youth hockey"*; and **Standard of Play, Situation 2** with **Rule 623, Situation 1**, which permit the stick lift *"at any time provided they do not impede their progress"* after Situation 1 has ruled that hooking an opponent's stick is itself a penalty. Also **Standard of Play, Situations 5, 6 and 7**, which are where USA Hockey writes its free-arm permission — absent from the playing rules — and its conditions; **Standard of Play, Situation 15**, read 18 September 2026 — the angling case, which asks whether a body-checking penalty should be called where a pursuing player establishes an angle, closes the gap along the boards and the carrier is knocked into them and loses the puck, and answers *"No, provided the Team B player does not use any overt hip, shoulder or forearm action to make contact with the opponent and simply maintains their skating lane and focuses on closing the gap and gaining possession of the puck"*; **Rule 620, Situations 1–4** and **Rule 607, Situation 2**, read for any exception excusing a check on a player whose head was low, of which they have none — and **read again on 1 September 2026 for their mandatory limbs**, which the playing rule does not carry: **Situation 4**, *"Any check using excessive force for the purpose of intimidating or punishing the opponent that makes direct contact with the head area of the opponent must be assessed as a match penalty"*, and **Situation 3**, *"Any time the Referee deems the contact to the head area to be intentional or a reckless act, a major plus game misconduct or match penalty must be assessed"*, both saying *must* where 620(c) says a match penalty *"may also be assessed"*; **Rule 608, Situation 1**, read for any exception for a player who turned into the check, of which it has none, and re-read 26 September 2026 for the tier it makes mandatory — *"[t]he major plus a game misconduct penalty, or match penalty, must be called"* in three named instances, the third *"regardless of whether or not board contact is made"*; **Rule 621, Situations 1–15** — the Casebook prints fifteen, and Situations 4–15 are all 621(d) high-sticking-the-puck cases — read in full on 1 September 2026 for a windup or follow-through carve-out and for anything addressing a crouching opponent, of which they have none, and where Situation 2 rules that contact is not required at all and that *"[i]t is not a requirement that a stick make contact above shoulder height for high sticking to be called"*; **Rule 603, Situations 1 and 2** with **Standard of Play, Situation 19**, read 1 September 2026 — Situation 2 restates the onus scoped *"[f]or USA Hockey youth games"*, asks *"was the check reckless and/or what was the vulnerability of the player being checked?"*, and runs the force-and-preparedness example both ways; **Standard of Play, Situation 19 prints the same weighing a second time**, asking instead *"was the check necessary and/or what was the vulnerability of the player being checked?"* and conditioning its no-penalty example on a checker who *"clearly attempted to gain possession of the puck"*, a condition Situation 2 does not carry; **Rule 603, Situation 3**, read 1 September 2026, which answers *"Yes"* to whether boarding may be called where a trailing player *"trips the opponent and causes them to lose control and crash into the end boards"*, and adds that *"[e]ven though a tripping penalty may also be appropriate, the boarding call is preferred to draw attention to the more aggressive infraction"*; **Rule 632, Situation 1**, read 18 September 2026, which puts a third player who *"sees the two players physically engaged and skates in their direction with the clear intent to deliver a check to the opposing player"*, asks whether the Referee should stop play, answers *"Yes"* on *"Rule Reference 632(a Note)"*, and directs that *"The Referee must stop play the instant they realize that unnecessary contact will result from allowing the play to continue"* — with a penalty to the third player if contact is made; **Rule 629, Situations 1 and 2**, read 1 September 2026 — Situation 1 assessing a major plus game misconduct to each of two players who left the penalty box on expired penalties at a stoppage caused by an altercation, including the one who *"does not enter the altercation, but skates to their players' bench"*, on the ground that *"[n]o player may leave the players' or penalty bench at any time during an altercation"*, and Situation 2 stating that the penalty is assessed *"to any player leaving the bench during an altercation, not just the first player to do so"*; **Rule 639, Situations 1 and 4**, read 18 September 2026 — Situation 1 answering *"No"* to whether the fouled player must fall to the ice for tripping to be assessed, on *"Rule Reference 639(Note)"*, reasoning that the rule uses the words *"trip or fall"* and that where the player does not fall *"it must be clearly obvious their progress was impeded and a competitive advantage was gained as a result of the tripping action"*; and Situation 4 ruling that where a player leaves their feet and slides into an opponent, *"a penalty for tripping must be called regardless of who gains possession of the puck"*, with the single exception *"when a player drops to their knees to block a shot and their momentum carries them into the player shooting the puck, causing them to fall"*; **Rule 639, Situation 5**, which names leg checking, clipping and slew footing as the tripping actions calling for a more severe penalty, states *"A minor penalty is NOT an option for slew footing"*, and closes, after a further sentence on clipping, *"Any time one of these actions has been deemed to recklessly endanger the opponent, a match penalty must be considered"*; **Rule 627, Situations 4 and 5**, read 30 August 2026, which put the kicked-puck deflection directly — *"The puck is kicked by an attacking player, deflects off their stick and enters their opponent's goal. Should the goal be allowed?"* — and answer *"No. Rule Reference 627(c)"*, the same answer following for a deflection off a teammate; **Rule 607, Situations 4, 5 and 6**, read 18 September 2026 for contact with the goalkeeper — Situation 4 runs three rungs for charging a goalkeeper *"while in their crease or privileged area"*, from *"no penalty should be assessed"* where *"a defending player has forced the attacking player into their own goalkeeper"* and the attacker has *"clearly made every attempt to avoid the contact with the goalkeeper"*, through *"a minor plus misconduct penalty is deemed appropriate"*, up to *"the major plus game misconduct, or match penalty option"*; Situation 5 holds that a goalkeeper *"can be legally checked when outside the privileged area"* — in a game Rule 604 permits body checking in — but is not *"fair game"* there, a penalty being due *"in every instance where unnecessary or avoidable contact is made with the goalkeeper – even when outside the privileged area"*; and Situation 6 answers *"Yes"* to whether the Privileged Area includes the area behind the goal line, which *"extends to the closer end boards"*; and **Rule 625, Situations 1 to 17**, read for a screening or goaltender-vision provision, of which they have none — **Situation 10** is written on a different question, whether the Referee must stop play *"every time an attacking player places their stick and/or skates in the opposing team’s goal crease"*, and answers *"No"*, since play *"should not be stopped because a player momentarily places their stick and/or skates in the goal crease, unless they interfere with the goalkeeper in any manner"*; and **Rule 621, Situations 1 to 15**, read in full on 1 September 2026 — **Situation 2** answers *"No."* to *"Must contact with an opponent occur in order for the Referee to assess a penalty for high sticking?"*, adds that *"[i]t is not a requirement that a stick make contact above shoulder height for high sticking to be called"*, and requires the call *"[w]henever a player raises their stick above their shoulders in an attempt to intimidate an opponent or is careless in their actions"*, all of which the playing rule's 621 Note does not say; **Situation 1** routes a stick to the head *"during the process of delivering a check"* to Rule 620 Head Contact instead; **Situation 3** confirms the 621(b) and 621(c) tiers on a player who recklessly endangers an opponent with a stick while falling; and **Situations 4 to 15** are all 621(d), high-sticking the puck, and bear on none of the above.
